\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})
for some
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}
. This means that
\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})
is a subgradient of
G
at
\mathbf{x}
, so
\mathbf{x}
is the weighted QA pool of the
p_{i}
’s. In other words, if
s
has convex exposure, then we may write

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*})=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),

(5.4)

where
\mathbf{p}^{*}
is the weighted QA pool of the given forecasts. The convex exposure property thus allows us to write down relations between exposures of forecasts that would otherwise not necessarily be true.

The quadratic and logarithmic scoring rules, as well as all proper scoring rules for binary outcomes, have convex exposure. In Appendix
B.3
we explore in more depth the question of which commonly used proper scoring rules have convex exposure property.

5.3
QA pooling as a max-min optimal method

Our goal is to give a formal justification for quasi-arithmetic pooling. Remark
5.2.7
established that the QA pool is optimizes (i.e. minimizes) the weighted average Bregman divergence to the experts’ forecasts. This section gives another justification for QA pooling in terms of max-min optimality. We will give additional justifications in later sections.

Theorem 5.3.1
.

Let
s
be a proper scoring rule and let
\mathbf{g}
be the exposure function of
s
. Fix any forecasts
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}\in\mathcal{D}
with non-negative weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{m}
adding to
1
. Define

u(\mathbf{p};j):=s(\mathbf{p};j)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}s(\mathbf{p}_{i};j).

Then the quantity
\min_{j}u(\mathbf{p};j)
is uniquely maximized by setting
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{p}^{*}:=\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}\limits_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i})
. Furthermore, this minimum (across outcomes
j
) is achieved simultaneously by all
j
with
p^{*}_{j}>0
. This quantity is non-negative, and is positive unless all reports
\mathbf{p}_{i}
with positive weights are equal.

One interpretation for this theorem statement is as follows. Consider an agent who is tasked with submitting a forecast, and who will be paid according to
s
. The agent decides to sub-contract
m
experts to get their opinions, paying expert
i
the amount
w_{i}s(\mathbf{p}_{i};j)
if the expert reports
\mathbf{p}_{i}
and outcome
j
happens. (Perhaps experts whom the agent trusts more have higher
w_{i}
’s.) Finally, the agent reports some (any) forecast
\mathbf{p}
. Then
u(\mathbf{p};j)
is precisely the agent’s profit (utility).

The quantity
\min_{j}u(\mathbf{p};j)
is the agent’s minimum possible profit over all outcomes. It is natural to ask which report
\mathbf{p}
maximizes this quantity. Theorem
5.3.1
states that this maximum is achieved by the QA pool of the experts’ forecasts with respect to
s
, and that this is the unique maximizer.

A possible geometric intuition to keep in mind for the proof (below): for each expert
i
, draw the plane tangent to
G
at
\mathbf{p}_{i}
. For any
j
, the value of this plane at
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}
is
s(\mathbf{p}_{i};j)
. Now take the weighted average of all
m
planes; this is a new plane whose intersection with any
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}
is the total score received by the experts if
j
happens. Since
G
is convex, this plane lies below
G
. To figure out which point maximizes the agent’s guaranteed profit, push the plane upward until it hits
G
. It will hit
G
at
\mathbf{p}^{*}
and the agent’s worst-case profit will be the vertical distance that the plane was pushed.

Proof of Theorem
5.3.1
.

By Equation
5.3
, computing the QA pool amounts to finding the minimizer
\mathbf{p}^{*}
of the function
G(\mathbf{x})-\left\langle\mathbf{x},\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})\right\rangle
over
\Delta_{n}
. If
\mathbf{p}^{*}
is in the interior of
\Delta_{n}
, then this expression is differentiable at
\mathbf{p}^{*}
. If
\mathbf{p}^{*}
is on the boundary, then the expression can be extended to a differentiable function in a neighborhood around
\mathbf{p}^{*}
.
12
12

12

This follows e.g. from
Azagra & Mudarra 2015a
, where we take
C
in the theorem statement to be a compact subset of
\Delta_{n}
containing
\mathbf{p}^{*}
where
G
is differentiable. Here we use that
\mathbf{p}^{*}\in\mathcal{D}
; by continuity of
\mathbf{g}
, such a subset necessarily exists.
Thus, applying the KKT conditions (see e.g.
Boyd & Vandenberghe 2004a
) tells us that

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})+\lambda\mathbf{1}_{n}+\boldsymbol{\mu}

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mu_{j}\geq 0
and
\mu_{j}=0
if
p^{*}_{j}>0
. We therefore have

\displaystyle u(\mathbf{p}^{*};j)

\displaystyle=s(\mathbf{p}^{*};j)-\sum_{i}w_{i}s(\mathbf{p}_{i};j)

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}^{*})+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\right\rangle-\sum_{i}w_{i}(G(\mathbf{p}_{i})+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}_{i}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}^{*})-\sum_{i}w_{i}G(\mathbf{p}_{i})+\left\langle\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})+\lambda\mathbf{1}_{n}+\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\right\rangle-\sum_{i}w_{i}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}^{*})-\sum_{i}w_{i}G(\mathbf{p}_{i})+\sum_{i}w_{i}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),\mathbf{p}_{i}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\right\rangle+\left\langle\lambda\mathbf{1}_{n}+\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}^{*})-\sum_{i}w_{i}G(\mathbf{p}_{i})+\sum_{i}w_{i}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),\mathbf{p}_{i}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\right\rangle+\mu_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}w_{i}D_{G}(\mathbf{p}^{*}\parallel\mathbf{p}_{i})+\mu_{j}.

The second-to-last step follows from the fact that
\left\langle\lambda\mathbf{1}_{n},\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\right\rangle=0
and
\left\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{p}^{*}\right\rangle=0
, and the last step follows by the definition of Bregman divergence. Since
\mu_{j}=0
for every
j
such that
p^{*}_{j}>0
(of which there is at least one), the minimum of
u(\mathbf{p}^{*};j)
over
j
is achieved simultaneously for all
j
with
p^{*}_{j}>0
, and this minimum is equal to
\sum_{i}w_{i}D_{G}(\mathbf{p}^{*}\parallel\mathbf{p}_{i})
. This quantity is non-negative, and positive except when all
\mathbf{p}_{i}
’s with positive weights are equal.

Finally we show that
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{p}^{*}
maximizes
\min_{j}u(\mathbf{x};j)
. Suppose that for some report
\mathbf{q}
we have that
\min_{j}u(\mathbf{q};j)\geq\min_{j}u(\mathbf{p}^{*};j)
. Then
u(\mathbf{q};j)\geq u(\mathbf{p}^{*};j)
for
every

j
such that
p^{*}_{j}>0
. But this means that the expected score (according to
s
) of an expert who believes
\mathbf{p}^{*}
is larger if the expert reports
\mathbf{q}
than if the expert reports
\mathbf{p}^{*}
. This contradicts the fact that
s
is proper.
∎

Remark 5.3.2
.

We can reformulate Theorem
5.3.1
as follows: suppose that an agent has access to forecasts
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}
and needs to issue a forecast, for which the agent will be scored using a proper scoring rule
s
. The agent can improve upon selecting an expert at random according to weights
w_{1},\dots,w_{m}
, no matter the outcome
j
, by reporting
\mathbf{p}^{*}
. This improvement is the same no matter the outcome – so long as the outcome is assigned positive probability by
\mathbf{p}^{*}
– and is a strict improvement unless all forecasts with positive weights are the same.

Remark 5.3.3
.

Theorem
5.3.1
is closely related to work by
Grünwald & Dawid 2004a
that establishes a connection between entropy maximization and worst-case expected loss minimization. Their work studies a generalized notion of entropy functions that, for the case of a proper scoring rule
s
, is equal to the negative of the expected score function
G
. They show that the forecast
\mathbf{x}
that maximizes entropy (minimizes
G
) also maximizes an expert’s worst-case score (over outcomes
j
). Considering instead the entropy function
\left\langle x,\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})\right\rangle-G(\mathbf{x})
yields results that are very similar to our Theorem
5.3.1
. In particular, if
\mathcal{D}
is a closed set or an open set, then the max-min result in Theorem
5.3.1
can be derived from
Grünwald & Dawid 2004a
and
Grünwald & Dawid 2004a
, respectively. However, our proof below captures cases that their results do not address. See also
Chambers et al. 2019a
, from which (upon considering the scoring rule with expected score function
G(\mathbf{x})-\left\langle x,\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})\right\rangle
) yields the simultaneity result of Theorem
5.3.1
if
\mathbf{p}^{*}
lies in the interior of
\Delta_{n}
.

5.4
Convex losses and learning expert weights

Thus far, when discussing QA pooling, we have regarded expert weights as given. Where do these weights come from? As demonstrated by the results in this section, if the proper scoring rule
s
has convex exposure (see Definition
5.2.10
), these weights can be learned from experience. This learning property for weights falls out of the following key observation, which states that an agent’s score is a concave function of the weights it uses for the experts.

Theorem 5.4.1
.

Let
s
be a proper scoring rule with convex exposure and forecast domain
\mathcal{D}
, and fix any
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}\in\mathcal{D}
. Given a weight vector
\mathbf{w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})\in\Delta_{m}
, define the
weight-score
of
\mathbf{w}
for an outcome
j
as

\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{w}):=s\left(\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i});j\right).

Then for every
j\in[n]
,
\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{w})
a concave function of
\mathbf{w}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
be two weight vectors. We wish to show that for any
c\in[0,1]
, we have

\text{WS}_{j}(c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w})-c\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{v})-(1-c)\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{w})\geq 0.

Recall the notation
\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}
from Definition
5.2.5
. Note that

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(cv_{i}+(1-c)w_{i})\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})=c\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}})+(1-c)\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}).

(5.5)

We have

\displaystyle\quad\text{WS}_{j}(c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w})-c\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{v})-(1-c)\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=s(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}};j)-cs(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}};j)-(1-c)s(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}};j)

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}})+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad-c(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}})+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}\right\rangle)-(1-c)(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}})+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}})-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad-c(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}})-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}\right\rangle)-(1-c)(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}})-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle)

Step 1 follows from the definition of
WS
. Step 2 follows from Equation
5.2
. Step 3 follows from Equation
5.5
, and specifically that the inner product of each side with
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}
is the same (so the
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}
terms cancel out, leaving a quantity that does not depend on
j
). Continuing where we left off:

\displaystyle\quad\text{WS}_{j}(c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w})-c\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{v})-(1-c)\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}})-c\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle-(1-c)\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad-c(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}})-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}\right\rangle)-(1-c)(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}})-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=c(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}})-G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}})-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}-\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1-c)(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}})-G(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}})-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}),\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}-\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=cD_{G}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}\parallel\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{v}})+(1-c)D_{G}(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{c\mathbf{v}+(1-c)\mathbf{w}}\parallel\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}})\geq 0.

Step 4 again follows from Equation
5.5
. Step 5 is a rearrangement of terms. Finally, step 6 follows from the definition of Bregman divergence, and step 7 follows from the fact that Bregman divergence is always non-negative. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.4.2
.

Theorem
5.4.1
can be stated in more generality:
s
need not have convex exposure; it suffices to have that for the particular
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}
, the QA pool of these forecasts exists for every weight vector.

Beyond Theorem
5.4.1
’s instrumental use for no-regret online learning of expert weights (Theorem
5.4.5
below), the result is interesting in its own right. For example, the following fact – loosely speaking, that QA pooling cannot benefit from weight randomization – follows as a corollary. (Recall the definition of
\mathbf{p}_{\mathbf{w}}^{*}
from Definition
5.2.5
.)

Corollary 5.4.3
.

Consider a randomized algorithm
A
with the following specifications:

•

Input: a proper scoring rule
s
with convex exposure, expert forecasts
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}
.

•

Output: a weight vector
\mathbf{w}\in\Delta_{m}
.

For any input
s,\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}
and for every
j
, we have

s(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbb{E}_{A}\left[\mathbf{w}\right]};j)\geq\mathbb{E}_{A}\left[s(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{w}};j)\right],

where
\mathbf{p}^{*}_{\mathbf{x}}
denotes the QA pool of
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}
with weight vector
\mathbf{x}
.

Remark 5.4.4
.

Theorem
5.4.1
would
not
hold if the pooling operator in the definition of
WS
were replaced by linear pooling or by logarithmic pooling.
13
13

13

For a counterexample to logarithmic pooling, consider
n=2
, let
s
be the quadratic scoring rule,
p_{1}=(0.1,0.9)
,
p_{2}=(0.5,0.5)
,
j=1
,
\mathbf{v}=(1,0)
,
\mathbf{w}=(0,1)
, and
c=\frac{1}{2}
. For a counterexample to linear pooling, consider
n=2
, let
s
be given by
G(p_{1},p_{2})=\sqrt{p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}}
(this is known as the
spherical
scoring rule),
p_{1}=(0,1)
,
p_{2}=(0.2,0.8)
,
j=1
,
\mathbf{v}=(1,0)
,
\mathbf{w}=(0,1)
, and
c=\frac{1}{2}
.
This is an advantage of QA pooling over using the linear or logarithmic method irrespective of the scoring rule.

We now state the no-regret result that we have alluded to. This result is quite strong in that it does not merely achieve low regret compared to the best
expert
, but in fact compared to the best possible
weighted pool of experts
in hindsight. This is a substantial distinction, as it is possible for a mixture of experts to substantially outperform any individual expert.

Theorem 5.4.5
.

Let
s
be a bounded proper scoring rule with convex exposure and forecast domain
\mathcal{D}
. For time steps
t=1\dots T
, an agent chooses a weight vector
\mathbf{w}^{t}\in\Delta_{m}
. The agent then receives a score of

s\left(\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{p}_{i}^{t},w_{i}^{t});j^{t}\right),

where
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{t},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}^{t}\in\mathcal{D}
and
j^{t}\in[n]
are chosen adversarially. By choosing
\mathbf{w}^{t}
according to Algorithm
6
(online gradient descent on the experts’ weights), the agent achieves
O(\sqrt{T})
regret in comparison with the best weight vector in hindsight. In particular, if
M
is an upper bound
14
14

14

This bound
M
exists because
s
is bounded by assumption, and so
\mathbf{g}
is also bounded (this follows from Equation
5.2
).
on
\left\lVert\mathbf{g}\right\rVert_{2}
, then for every
\mathbf{w}^{*}\in\Delta_{m}
we have

\sum_{t=1}^{T}s\left(\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{p}_{i}^{t},w_{i}^{*});j^{t}\right)-s\left(\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{p}_{i}^{t},w_{i}^{t});j^{t}\right)\leq 3\sqrt{m}M\sqrt{T}.

\mathbf{w}^{1}\leftarrow(1/m,\dots,1/m)
;

for

t=1
to
T

do

\eta_{t}\leftarrow\frac{1}{M\sqrt{mt}}
;

Play
\mathbf{w}^{t}
and observe loss
L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})
;

\tilde{\mathbf{w}}^{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{w}^{t}-\eta_{t}\nabla L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})
;

\mathbf{w}^{t+1}\leftarrow
projection of
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}^{t+1}
onto
\Delta_{m}
;

end for

ALGORITHM 6

Online gradient descent algorithm for Theorem
5.4.5

Algorithm
6
, referenced in the theorem statement, is an application of the standard online gradient descent algorithm (see e.g.
Hazan 2023a
) to our particular setting. In our context, the loss function is
L^{t}(\mathbf{w})=-\text{WS}_{j^{t}}(\mathbf{w})
, where
WS
is as in Theorem
5.4.1
, relative to forecasts
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{t},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}^{t}
. We defer the proof of Theorem
5.4.5
to Appendix
B.1
. The proof amounts to applying the standard bounds for online gradient descent, though with an extra step: we use the bound
M
on
\left\lVert\mathbf{g}\right\rVert
to bound the gradient of the loss as a function of expert weights.

5.5
QA pooling connects two notions of overconfidence

Overconfidence is a well-studied phenomenon in which forecasters are systematically biased toward reporting probabilities that are too extreme. For example, suppose that an expert assigns probabilities to a large number of events, and that, among those events to which the expert assigns a 1% probability, 10% end up happening. This is a sign of overconfidence: the expert’s forecasts would be better if all of their forecasts of 1% were systematically increased.

What, formally, is meant by overconfidence? Arguably, a definition of overconfidence ought to depend on the proper scoring rule used to elicit or grade the expert’s forecast. This is because -- as we have already discussed -- a proper scoring rule expresses a subjective opinion about which probabilistic distinctions are important. If an expert assigns a 0.01% probability to an outcome that has a 1% chance of occurring, is this a larger mistake than if the expert assigns a 40% probability to an outcome that has a 41% chance of occurring? The logarithmic scoring rule says yes; the quadratic scoring rule says no.
15
15

15

Formally, recall that the Bregman divergence from the true probability to the reported forecast with respect to the expected score function
G
measures the score that the expert loses out on due to their inaccuracy. We have
D_{G_{\text{log}}}(1\%\parallel 0.01\%)\gg D_{G_{\text{log}}}(41\%\parallel 40\%)
, whereas
D_{G_{\text{quad}}}(1\%\parallel 0.01\%)\approx D_{G_{\text{quad}}}(41\%\parallel 40\%)
.

In this section, we present two definitions of overconfidence with respect to a proper scoring rule. The first definition is natural and well-motivated, but (to our knowledge) has not previously appeared in the literature. The second definition adapts a standard notion of overconfidence to respect the intuition of the previous paragraph by making use of QA pooling. We then prove that the two definitions are equivalent.

For both definitions, we consider the following setting: an expert forecasts
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{N}\in\Delta_{n}
for
N
events. Outcomes
j^{1},\dots,j^{N}\in[n]
are realized. We are defining what it means for the expert to be overconfident on this sample of
N
events.
16
16

16

We define overconfidence with respect to a particular realization of the outcomes (as opposed to on average over realizations) so that our definitions do not require a prior over the probabilities of the outcomes.

The first definition uses a quite simple principle:
an overconfident expert expects a larger score than they will receive.

Definition 5.5.1

(Expected score definition of overconfidence)
.

Suppose that an expert forecasts
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{N}\in\Delta_{n}
, and outcomes
j^{1},\dots,j^{N}\in[n]
are realized. The expert is
expected-score overconfident
with respect to a proper scoring rule
s
(with expected score function
G
) if their expected score exceeds their actual score:

\sum_{k=1}^{N}G(\mathbf{p}^{k})>\sum_{k=1}^{N}s(\mathbf{p}^{k};j^{k}).

(5.6)

On a basic level, the intuition behind Definition
5.5.1
is straightforward: an overconfident expert will overestimate their score. But to gain some further intuition, recall Figure
2.1
(repeated here). It shows the score of an expert who reports a probability of
x=40\%
, under the No outcome (the red dot on the left) and under the Yes outcome (the red dot on the right). Suppose that, in the setting of Definition
5.5.1
, an expert reports 40% for all
N
events. Then the expert expects a score of
G(0.4)
per event on average: more than that under a No outcome (assuming
G
is symmetric about
0.5
, as shown) and less than that under a Yes outcome, but
G(0.4)
on average.

If in fact 40% of the events happen, then the expert is correctly calibrated: neither over- nor underconfident. Their total score will be
N\cdot G(0.4)
and so the left-hand side of Equation
5.6
will equal the right-hand side. If only 20% of the events happen, then the expert is underconfident (should have assigned more extreme probabilities) – and indeed, the expert’s total score will be
higher
than the expert expects. And if more than 40% of the events happen, then the expert is overconfident, and indeed, that is the case in which Equation
5.6
holds.

Our second definition also uses a simple principle:
an overconfident expert would be better off assigning less extreme probabilities.
This basic notion has previously appeared in the literature. For example,
Schanbacher 2014a
uses the following definition: an expert is overconfident with respect to a proper scoring rule if shrinking all of the expert’s forecasts toward the uniform distribution by some positive constant factor will improve the expert’s score. Formally, an expert is overconfident by this definition if there exists
w\in(0,1)
such that

\sum_{k}s\left(w\mathbf{p}^{k}+(1-w)(1/n,\dots,1/n);j^{k}\right)>\sum_{k}s(\mathbf{p}^{k};j^{k}).

Our definition is similar in spirit, but importantly different: why shrink the expert’s report toward the uniform distribution by a constant factor? Again, it seems that the correct form of shrinkage ought to depend
s
. Perhaps the quadratic score treats 26% as being equally far from 2% as it is from 50% (and so shrinking 2% halfway toward 50% would produce a result of 26%), but the log score certainly does not: shrinking 2% halfway toward 50% “with respect to” the log scoring rule should give something much less than 26%. And so our definition instead uses the exposure function
\mathbf{g}
of
s
. Much as QA pooling averages the exposures of the experts’ forecasts, our method of shrinking a forecast is to average its exposure with the zero vector. Intuitively this means uniformly making the forecast “more indifferent” to the eventual outcome.

Definition 5.5.2
.

For a proper scoring rule
s
, we define
\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p},w)
to be the forecast
\mathbf{p}^{*}
such that
w\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})
is a subgradient of
G
at
\mathbf{p}^{*}
, where
\mathbf{g}
is the exposure function of
s
.

Note that
\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p},1)=\mathbf{p}
, i.e.
w=1
means no shrinkage (whereas
w=0
means complete shrinkage).

Remark 5.5.3
.

In the case that there exists
\mathbf{q}
such that
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q})=0
– i.e. a forecast
\mathbf{q}
such that the score of
\mathbf{q}
does not depend on the outcome – we have

\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p},w)=(\mathbf{p},w)\oplus(\mathbf{q},1-w).

For this reason, we will refer to
\text{Shrink}_{s}
as
QA shrinkage
with respect to
s
. Such a
\mathbf{q}
typically exists for proper scoring rules that are used in practice. Indeed, for any proper scoring rule that treats all outcomes symmetrically,
\mathbf{q}
is simply the uniform distribution
(1/n,\dots,1/n)
. In particular, QA shrinkage with respect to the quadratic scoring rule is equivalent to averaging with the uniform distribution. That is, in the case of
s_{\text{quad}}
, our definition coincides with that of
Schanbacher 2014a
. On the other hand, QA shrinkage with respect to
s_{\text{log}}
is equivalent to taking a logarithmic pool of the forecast with the uniform distribution.

Definition 5.5.4

(QA shrinkage definition of overconfidence)
.

Suppose that an expert forecasts
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{N}\in\Delta_{n}
, and outcomes
j^{1},\dots,j^{N}\in[n]
are realized. The expert is
QA-shrinkage overconfident
with respect to a proper scoring rule
s
if for some
w\in(0,1)
, we have

\sum_{k=1}^{N}s(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w);j^{k})>\sum_{k=1}^{N}s(\mathbf{p}^{k};j^{k}).

(5.7)

We can now state our main theorem, which is that (under some weak assumptions about
s
) these two definitions are the same.

Theorem 5.5.5
.

Let
s
be a proper scoring rule with convex exposure, for which there is a
\mathbf{q}\in\Delta_{n}
such that
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q})=\mathbf{0}
. Suppose that an expert forecasts
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{N}\in\Delta_{n}
, and outcomes
j^{1},\dots,j^{N}\in[n]
are realized. The expert is expected-score overconfident with respect to
s
if and only if they are QA-shrinkage overconfident with respect to
s
.

Proof.

First, observe that by using the Savage representation of
s
, we may rewrite the condition for expected-score overconfidence (Equation
5.6
) as

\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\mathbf{p}^{k}\right\rangle<0.

We now show that Definition
5.5.4
is equivalent. By Remark
5.5.3
, we may write
\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)=(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)\oplus(\mathbf{q},1-w)
, where
\mathbf{q}
is as in the theorem statement. Furthermore, because
s
has convex exposure, Equation
5.4
tells us that
\mathbf{g}(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w))=w\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k})
.

We now use Theorem
5.4.1
, which tells us that for all
k
, the quantity

s\left((\mathbf{p}^{k},w)\oplus_{s}(\mathbf{q},1-w);j^{k}\right)

is concave in
w
. This means that the left-hand side of Equation
5.7
is a concave function (call it
f
) of
w
. On the other hand, the right-hand side of Equation
5.7
is
f(1)
. The question of whether there exists
w\in(0,1)
such that
f(w)>f(1)
is therefore equivalent to the question of whether the derivative of
f
at
w=1
is negative.
17
17

17

Or more precisely, the left derivative, since
f
is only defined on
[0,1]
.
To evaluate this derivative, we note that

\displaystyle s(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w);j^{k})

\displaystyle=G(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w))+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=G(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w))+w\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)\right\rangle.

Therefore, we have

\frac{\partial s(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w);j^{k})}{\partial w}=\frac{\partial G(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w))}{\partial w}+\frac{\partial\left(w\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)\right\rangle\right)}{\partial w}.

Considering
\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)
as a function from
[0,1]
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\mathbf{J}(w)
be its Jacobian, i.e. the vector of partial derivatives of the
n
coordinates with respect to
w
. Applying the rules of differentiation, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial s(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w);j^{k})}{\partial w}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)),\mathbf{J}(w)\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-w\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\mathbf{J}(w)\right\rangle.

Now, we are specifically interested in the derivative at
w=1
. Plugging in
w=1
, we have

\frac{\partial s(\text{Shrink}_{s}(\mathbf{p}^{k},w);j^{k})}{\partial w}\mid_{w=1}=\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\mathbf{J}(1)\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\mathbf{p}^{k}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\mathbf{J}(1)\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\mathbf{p}^{k}\right\rangle.

Recalling that the expert is QA-shrinkage overconfident if and only if the derivative of
f
at
w=1
is negative, this is the case if and only if

\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{k}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j^{k}}-\mathbf{p}^{k}\right\rangle<0.

As we have already showed, this is the case precisely when the expert is expected-score overconfident. This completes the proof.
∎

5.6
Axiomatization of QA pooling

In this section, we aim to show that the class of all quasi-arithmetic pooling operators is a natural one, by showing that these operators are precisely those which satisfy a natural set of axioms.

Kolmogorov 1930a
;
Nagumo 1930a
independently considered the class of quasi-arithmetic means. Given an interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and a continuous, injective function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
, the
quasi-arithmetic mean with respect to
f
, or
f
-mean
, is the function
M_{f}
that takes as input
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in I
(for any
m\geq 1
) and outputs

M_{f}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}):=f^{-1}\left(\frac{f(x_{1})+\dots+f(x_{m})}{m}\right).

For example, the arithmetic mean corresponds to
f(x)=x
; the quadratic to
f(x)=x^{2}
; the geometric to
f(x)=\log x
; and the harmonic to
f(x)=\frac{-1}{x}
.

Kolmogorov proved that the class of quasi-arithmetic means is precisely the class of functions
M:\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}I^{m}\to I
satisfying the following natural properties:
18
18

18

Nagumo also provided a characterization, though with slightly different properties.

(1)

M(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
is continuous and strictly increasing in each variable.

(2)

M
is symmetric in its arguments.

(3)

M(x,x,\dots,x)=x
.

(4)

M(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{m})
=
M(y,\dots,y,x_{k+1},\dots,x_{m})
, where
y:=M(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
appears
k
times on the right-hand side. Informally, a subset of arguments to the mean function can be replaced with their mean.

The four properties listed above can be viewed as an
axiomatization
of quasi-arithmetic means.

Our notion of quasi-arithmetic pooling is exactly that of a quasi-arithmetic mean, except that it is more general in two ways. First, it allows for weights to accompany the arguments to the mean. Second, we are considering quasi-arithmetic means with respect to
vector-valued
functions
\mathbf{g}
. In the
n=2
outcome case,
\mathbf{g}
can be considered a scalar-valued function since it is defined on a one-dimensional space (see Remark
5.2.4
for details); but in general we cannot treat
\mathbf{g}
as scalar-valued.
19
19

19

Another difference is that in the
n=2
case, we are restricting
\mathcal{D}
to be an interval from
0
to
1
, though this is not a fundamental difference for the purposes of this section.

Our goal is to extend the above axiomatization of quasi-arithmetic means in these two ways: first (in Section
5.6.1
) to include weights as arguments, and second (in Section
5.6.2
) to general
n
(while still allowing arbitrary weights).

5.6.1
Generalizing to include weights as arguments

The objects that we will be studying in this section are ones of the form
(p,w)
, where
w\geq 0
and
p\in\mathcal{D}
. In this subsection,
\mathcal{D}
is a two-outcome forecast domain, whose elements we will identify with by the probability of the first outcome (see Remark
5.2.4
).
20
20

20

Proper scoring rules for two outcomes can have four possible forecast domains:
[0,1]
,
[0,1)
,
(0,1]
, and
(0,1)
.
We will fix the set
\mathcal{D}
for the remainder of the subsection. Our results generalize to any interval of
\mathbb{R}
(as in Kolmogorov’s work), but we focus on forecast domains since that is our application.

Definition 5.6.1
.

A
weighted forecast
is an element of
\mathcal{D}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
: a probability and a positive weight. Given a weighted forecast
\Pi=(p,w)
we define
\text{pr}(\Pi):=p
and
\text{wt}(\Pi):=w
.

We will thinking of the output of pooling operators as weighted forecasts. This is a simple extension of our earlier definition of quasi-arithmetic pooling (Definition
5.2.5
), which only output a probability.

Definition 5.6.2

(Quasi-arithmetic pooling with arbitrary weights (
n=2
))
.

Given a continuous, strictly increasing function
g:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, and weighted forecasts
\Pi_{1}=(p_{1},w_{1}),\dots,\Pi_{m}=(p_{m},w_{m})
, define the
quasi-arithmetic pool
of
\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{m}
with respect to
g
as

\sideset{}{{}_{g}}{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{m}(p_{i},w_{i}):=\left(g^{-1}\left(\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}g(p_{i})}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}\right),\sum_{i}w_{i}\right).

Remark 5.6.3
.

The equivalence of this notion to our earlier one uses the fact that all (continuous) proper scoring rules for binary outcomes have convex exposure (Proposition
B.3.1
), so the relationship between
g(p^{*})
and
g(p)
may be written as in Equation
5.4
.

In the case that
\sum_{i}w_{i}=1
, Definition
5.6.2
reduces to Definition
5.2.5
. In general, by linearly scaling the weights in Definition
5.6.2
to add to
1
, we recover quasi-arithmetic pooling as previously defined.

We find the following fact useful.

Proposition 5.6.4
.

Given two continuous, strictly increasing functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
,
\oplus_{g_{1}}
and
\oplus_{g_{2}}
are the same if and only if
g_{2}=ag_{1}+b
for some
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Clearly if
g_{2}=ag_{1}+b
for some
a>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
then
\oplus_{g_{1}}
and
\oplus_{g_{2}}
are the same. For the converse, suppose that no such
a
and
b
exist. Let
x<y
be such that
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are not equal (even up to positive affine transformation) on
[x,y]
. Let
g_{2}^{\prime}
be the positive affine transformation of
g_{2}
that makes it equal to
g_{1}
at
x
and
y
, and let
z\in(x,y)
be such that
g_{1}(z)\neq g_{2}^{\prime}(z)
. Let
\alpha
be such that
g_{1}(z)=\alpha g_{1}(x)+(1-\alpha)g_{1}(y)
. Then
(x,\alpha)\oplus_{g_{1}}(y,1-\alpha)=(z,1)
, but
(x,\alpha)\oplus_{g_{2}}(y,1-\alpha)\neq(z,1)
, so
\oplus_{g_{1}}
and
\oplus_{g_{2}}
are different.
∎

We now define properties (i.e. axioms) of a pooling operator
\oplus
, such that these properties are satisfied if and only if
\oplus
is
\oplus_{g}
for some
g
. Our axiomatization will look somewhat different from Kolmogorov’s, in part because we choose to define
\oplus
as a binary operator that (if it satisfies the associativity axiom) extends to the
m
-ary case. This is a simpler domain and will simplify notation. In Appendix
B.2
we exhibit an equivalent set of axioms that more closely resembles Kolmogorov’s.

Definition 5.6.5

(Axioms for pooling operators (
n=2
))
.

For a pooling operator
\oplus
on
\mathcal{D}
(i.e. a binary operator on weighted forecasts), we define the following axioms.

1.

Weight additivity
:
\text{wt}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=\text{wt}(\Pi_{1})+\text{wt}(\Pi_{2})
for every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
.

2.

Commutativity
:
\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}=\Pi_{2}\oplus\Pi_{1}
for every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
.

3.

Associativity
:
\Pi_{1}\oplus(\Pi_{2}\oplus\Pi_{3})=(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})\oplus\Pi_{3}
for every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\Pi_{3}
.

4.

Continuity
: For every
p_{1},p_{2}
, the quantity
21
21

21

We allow one weight to be
0
by defining
(p,w)\oplus(q,0)=(q,0)\oplus(p,w)=(p,w)
.

\text{pr}((p_{1},w_{1})\oplus(p_{2},w_{2}))

is a continuous function of
(w_{1},w_{2})
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
.

5.

Idempotence
: For every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
, if
\text{pr}(\Pi_{1})=\text{pr}(\Pi_{2})
then
\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=\text{pr}(\Pi_{1})
.

6.

Monotonicity
: Let
w>0
and let
p_{1}>p_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
. Then for
x\in(0,w)
, the quantity
\text{pr}((p_{1},x)\oplus(p_{2},w-x))
is a strictly increasing function of
x
.

The motivation for the weight additivity axiom is that the weight of a weighted forecast can be thought of as the
amount of evidence
for its prediction. When pooling weighted forecasts, the weight of an individual forecast can be thought of as the strength of its vote in the aggregate.

The monotonicity axiom essentially states that if one pools two forecasts with different probabilities and a fixed total weight, then the larger the share of the weight belonging to the larger of the two probabilities, the larger the aggregate probability.

We now state and prove this section’s main result: these axioms describe the class of QA pooling operators.

Theorem 5.6.6
.

A pooling operator is a QA pooling operator (as in Definition
5.6.2
) with respect to some
g
if and only if it satisfies the axioms in Definition
5.6.5
.
22
22

22

As we mentioned, for an associative pooling operator
\oplus
,
\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}\dots\oplus\Pi_{m}
is a well-specified quantity, even without indicating parenthesization. This lets us use the notation
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\Pi_{i}
. This is why the statement of Theorem
5.6.6
makes sense despite pooling operators not being
m
-ary by default.

We will use
\oplus
(without a
g
subscript) to denote an arbitrary pooling operator that satisfies the axioms in Definition
5.6.5
. Before presenting the proof of Theorem
5.6.6
, we will note a few important facts about weighted forecasts and pooling operators. First, we find it natural to define a notion of multiplying a weighted forecast pair by a positive constant.

Definition 5.6.7
.

Given a weighted forecast
\Pi=(p,w)
and
c>0
, define
c\Pi:=(p,cw)
.

Note that
m\Pi=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\Pi
for any positive integer
m
, by idempotence; this definition is a natural extension to all
c>0
. We note the following (quite obvious) fact.

Proposition 5.6.8
.

For every weighted forecast
\Pi
and
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, we have
c_{1}(c_{2}\Pi)=(c_{1}c_{2})\Pi
.

A natural property that is not listed in Definition
5.6.5
is
scale invariance
, i.e. that
\text{pr}((p_{1},w_{1})\oplus(p_{2},w_{2}))=\text{pr}((p_{1},cw_{1})\oplus(p_{2},cw_{2}))
for any positive
c
; or, equivalently, that
c(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=c\Pi_{1}\oplus c\Pi_{2}
. This in fact follows from the listed axioms.

Proposition 5.6.9

(Distributive property/scale invariance)
.

For every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
and any operator
\oplus
satisfying the axioms in Definition
5.6.5
, we have
c(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=c\Pi_{1}\oplus c\Pi_{2}
.

Proof.

First suppose
c
is an integer. Then

c\Pi_{1}\oplus c\Pi_{2}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{c}\Pi_{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{c}\Pi_{2}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{c}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=c(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}).

Here, the first and last steps follow by weight additivity and idempotence. Now suppose that
c=\frac{k}{\ell}
is a rational number. Let
\Pi_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\ell}\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\ell}\Pi_{2}
. We have

\frac{k}{\ell}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=\frac{k}{\ell}(\ell\Pi_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\ell\Pi_{2}^{\prime})=\frac{k}{\ell}\cdot\ell(\Pi_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\Pi_{2}^{\prime})=k(\Pi_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\Pi_{2}^{\prime})=k\Pi_{1}^{\prime}\oplus k\Pi_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{k}{\ell}\Pi_{1}\oplus\frac{k}{\ell}\Pi_{2}.

Here, the second and second-to-last steps follow from the fact that the distributive property holds for integers.

Finally, make use of the continuity axiom to extend our proof to all positive real numbers
c
. In particular, it suffices to show that
\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=\text{pr}(c\Pi_{1}\oplus c\Pi_{2})
. Let
p
be the former quantity; note that
\text{pr}(r\Pi_{1}\oplus r\Pi_{2})=p
for positive rational numbers
r
. Since the rationals are dense among the reals, it follows that for every
\epsilon>0
, we have
\left\lvert\text{pr}(c\Pi_{1}\oplus c\Pi_{2})-p\right\rvert\leq\epsilon
. Therefore,
\text{pr}(c\Pi_{1}\oplus c\Pi_{2})=p
. This completes the proof.
∎

Armed with these facts, we present a proof of Theorem
5.6.6
.

Proof of Theorem
5.6.6
.

We first prove that any QA pooling operator
\oplus_{g}
satisfies the axioms in Definition
5.6.5
. Weight additivity, commutativity, and idempotence are trivial. Associativity is also clear: given
\Pi_{1}=(p_{1},w_{1})
and likewise
\Pi_{2},\Pi_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle g(\text{pr}((\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{2})\oplus_{g}\Pi_{3}))

\displaystyle=\frac{(w_{1}+w_{2})g(\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{2}))+w_{3}g(p_{3})}{(w_{1}+w_{2})+w_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(w_{1}+w_{2})\frac{w_{1}g(p_{1})+w_{2}(p_{2})}{w_{1}+w_{2}}+w_{3}g(p_{3})}{w_{1}+w_{2}+w_{3}}=\frac{w_{1}g(p_{1})+w_{2}g(p_{2})+w_{3}g(p_{3})}{w_{1}+w_{2}+w_{3}}

and likewise for
g(\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}(\Pi_{2}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{3})))
, so
\text{pr}((\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{2})\oplus_{g}\Pi_{3})=\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}(\Pi_{2}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{3}))
(since
g
is strictly increasing and therefore injective). The fact that the weights are also the same is trivial. Continuity follows from the fact that

\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{2})=g^{-1}\left(\frac{w_{1}g(p_{1})+w_{2}g(p_{2})}{w_{1}+w_{2}}\right)

is continuous in
(w_{1},w_{2})
(when
w_{1},w_{2}
are not both zero). Here we are using the fact that
g
is
strictly
increasing, which means that
g^{-1}
is continuous.

Finally, regarding the monotonicity axiom, for any fixed
w
and
p_{1}>p_{2}
(as in the axiom statement), we have

g(\text{pr}((p_{1},x)\oplus_{g}(p_{2},w-x)))=\frac{xg(p_{1})+(w-x)g(p_{2})}{x+w-x}=\frac{xg(p_{1})+(w-x)g(p_{2})}{w}.

Since
p_{1}>p_{2}
, we have
g(p_{1})>g(p_{2})
, so the right-hand side strictly increases with
x
. Since
g^{-1}
is also strictly increasing, it follows that
\text{pr}((p_{1},x)\oplus_{g}(p_{2},w-x))
strictly increases with
x
.

The converse – that every pooling operator satisfying the axioms in Definition
5.6.5
is
\oplus_{g}
for some
g
– works by constructing
g
by fixing it at two points and constructing
g
at all other points. Right now we show how to do this when the forecast domain is
[0,1]
; see the proof of Theorem
B.2.7
for the argument in full generality.

Let
\oplus
be a pooling operator that satisfies our axioms. Define
g
as follows: let
g(0)=0
and
g(1)=1
. For
0<p<1
, define
g(p)=w
where
(1,w)\oplus(0,1-w)=(p,1)
. (This
w
exists by continuity and the intermediate value theorem; it is unique by the “strictly” increasing stipulation of monotonicity.) Note that
g
is continuous and increasing by monotonicity.
23
23

23

As a matter of fact,
g
is strictly increasing because it is impossible for
g(p_{1})
to equal
g(p_{2})
for
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
, as that would mean that
(1,g(p_{1}))\oplus(0,1-g(p_{1}))=(p_{1},1)=(p_{2},1)
. Another way to look at this is that it comes from the fact that
(1,w)\oplus(0,1-w)
is continuous in
w
by the continuity axiom. In a sense, the
continuity
of
g
corresponds to the
strictness
of increase in the monotonicity axiom and the
strictness
of increase of
g
corresponds to the
continuity
axiom.

We wish to show that for any
\Pi_{1}=(p_{1},w_{1})
and
\Pi_{2}=(p_{2},w_{2})
, we have that
\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}=\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{2}
. Clearly the weight of both sides is
w_{1}+w_{2}
, so we wish to show that the probabilities on each side are the same. We have
24
24

24

Steps 3 and 7 uses the distributive property (Proposition
5.6.9
).

\displaystyle\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})

\displaystyle=\text{pr}(w_{1}(p_{1},1)\oplus w_{2}(p_{2},1))

\displaystyle=\text{pr}(w_{1}((1,g(p_{1}))\oplus(0,1-g(p_{1})))\oplus w_{2}((1,g(p_{2}))\oplus(0,1-g(p_{2}))))

\displaystyle=\text{pr}(w_{1}(1,g(p_{1}))\oplus w_{1}(0,1-g(p_{1}))\oplus w_{2}(1,g(p_{2}))\oplus w_{2}(0,1-g(p_{2})))

\displaystyle=\text{pr}((1,w_{1}g(p_{1}))\oplus(0,w_{1}(1-g(p_{1})))\oplus(1,w_{2}g(p_{2}))\oplus(0,w_{2}(1-g(p_{2}))))

\displaystyle=\text{pr}((1,w_{1}g(p_{1})+w_{2}g(p_{2}))\oplus(0,w_{1}(1-g(p_{1}))+w_{2}(1-g(p_{2}))))

\displaystyle=\text{pr}\left(\frac{1}{w_{1}+w_{2}}((1,w_{1}g(p_{1})+w_{2}g(p_{2}))\oplus(0,w_{1}(1-g(p_{1}))+w_{2}(1-g(p_{2}))))\right)

\displaystyle=\text{pr}\left(\left(1,\frac{w_{1}g(p_{1})+w_{2}g(p_{2})}{w_{1}+w_{2}}\right)\oplus\left(0,\frac{w_{1}(1-g(p_{1}))+w_{2}(1-g(p_{2}))}{w_{1}+w_{2}}\right)\right),

which by definition of
g
is equal to the probability
p
such that
g(p)=\frac{g(p_{1})w_{1}+g(p_{2})w_{2}}{w_{1}+w_{2}}
. That is,
\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{2})
.

Showing that
\oplus
and
\oplus_{g}
are equivalent for more than two arguments is now trivial:

\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{m}\Pi_{i}=\Pi_{1}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{2}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{3}\dots\oplus_{g}\Pi_{m}=\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}\oplus_{g}\Pi_{3}\dots\oplus_{g}\Pi_{m}=\dots=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\Pi_{i}.

(Here we are implicitly using the fact that
\oplus_{g}
is associative, as we proved earlier.) This completes the proof.
∎

5.6.2
Generalizing to higher dimensions

In Appendix
B.2.1
, we generalize our axioms from
n=2
outcomes to arbitrary values of
n
. An important challenge is extending the monotonicity axiom: in higher dimensions, what is the appropriate generalization of an increasing function? We show that the correct notion is
cyclical monotonicity
, which we define and discuss. We then present our axiomatization (Definition
B.2.2
) and prove that the axioms constitute a characterization of the class of QA pooling operators (Theorem
B.2.7
). On a high level, the proof is not dissimilar to that of Theorem
5.6.6
, though the details are fairly different and more technical.

In conclusion, in Definition
5.6.5
we made a list of natural properties that a pooling operator may satisfy. Theorem
5.6.6
shows that the pooling operators satisfying these properties are exactly the QA pooling operators. In Appendix
B.2.1
, we generalize this theorem to higher dimensions, thus fully axiomatizing QA pooling. This result gives us an additional important reason to believe that QA pooling with respect to a proper scoring rule is a fundamental notion.

5.7
Conclusions and future directions

While in this work we have focused on proper scoring rules for eliciting probability distributions over possible outcomes, scoring rules can be used to elicit various other properties, such as the expectation or the median of a random variable
(
Savage 1971a
;
Gneiting & Raftery 2007a
;
Lambert et al. 2008c
)
. The topic of
property elicitation
studies such scoring rules. The Savage representation of a proper scoring rule generalizes to arbitrary linear properties
(
Frongillo & Kash 2015a
, Theorem 11)
. That is, consider a random variable (or
n
-tuple of random variables)
X
and a convex function
G
whose domain is (a superset of) the possible values of
X
. If
\mathbf{p}
is an expert’s forecast for
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]
and
\mathbf{x}
is the realized outcome, then the scoring rule
s(\mathbf{p};\mathbf{x}):=G(\mathbf{p})+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{p}\right\rangle
is proper.
25
25

25

In the setting of this work,
X
is the vector of random variables where the
j
-th variable is
1
if outcome
j
happens and
0
otherwise, and
\mathbf{x}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}
where
j
is the outcome that happens.
Modulo a fairly straightforward generalization, all proper scoring rules for
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]
take this form (see Section
2.1.3
).

Our definition of QA pooling extends verbatim to the setting of eliciting linear properties. In this more general setting, for any proper scoring rule that has convex exposure, it remains the case
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*})=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})
; as a consequence, Theorems
5.3.1
,
5.4.1
,
5.4.5
, and
5.5.5
generalize.

We now discuss several promising directions for future work. First: as we discussed in Section
2.2.3
, it is natural to generalize QA pooling by dropping the requirement that weights add to
1
. In Section
2.4
, we gave a Bayesian justification for linear pooling with arbitrary weights, and gave a different and novel Bayesian justification for logarithmic pooling with arbitrary weights. This raises a few questions:

•

Which of our results generalize if the requirement that weights add to
1
is dropped? Note that dropping this requirement means that the idempotence axiom of Section
5.6
is no longer satisfied. Is there an axiomatization that characterizes the class of all generalized QA pools?

•

Just as we gave Bayesian justifications for generalized linear and logarithmic pooling, is there a Bayesian justification for generalized QA pooling with respect to an arbitrary proper scoring rule? What does it look like?

Second: it is natural to wonder about connections to prediction markets. After all, a prediction market is a mechanism for eliciting forecasts from multiple experts and aggregating them! As mentioned in Section
5.1.4
, QA pooling can be interpreted in terms of cost function markets: if
\mathbf{q}_{i}
denotes the quantity of shares that each expert would buy in order to bring a market into line with the expert’s beliefs, then the QA pool of the experts’ beliefs is the probability distribution corresponding to the quantity vector
\mathbf{q}^{*}:=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{q}_{i}
. This follows from
Abernethy et al. 2013a
; the underlying reason is the convex duality between an expected score function
G
and the corresponding cost function
C
. Exploring this connection further may yield insights into forecast aggregation through market mechanisms.

Third: some of our results may be able to be extended or generalized. In fact, we do so in Chapter
6
, where we relax the assumption made in Theorem
5.4.5
that the scoring rule is bounded. Additionally, many of our theorem statements depend on the convex exposure property, but it is possible that some of our definitions and theorem statements could be modified so as to not require this assumption.

QA pooling connects forecast elicitation and aggregation in a way that seems quite fundamental. Further research in this area may yield important insights.

Learning weights for logarithmic pooling

This chapter presents “No-Regret Learning with Unbounded Losses: The Case of Logarithmic Pooling”
(
Neyman & Roughgarden 2022d
)
. It assumes knowledge of logarithmic pooling, presented in Section
2.2.1
. Background on proper scoring rules (Section
2.1
), as well as on information structures (Section
2.3
), is useful for context but not required. Additionally, Chapter
5
is useful for context, as this work builds on it directly.

Summary:
For each of
T
time steps,
m
experts report probability distributions over
n
outcomes; we wish to learn to aggregate these forecasts in a way that attains a no-regret guarantee. We focus on logarithmic pooling, which is in a certain sense the optimal choice of pooling method if one is interested in minimizing log loss (see Theorem
5.3.1
). We consider the problem of learning the best set of parameters (i.e. expert weights) in an online adversarial setting. We assume (by necessity) that the adversarial choices of outcomes and forecasts are consistent, in the sense that experts report calibrated forecasts. Imposing this constraint creates a (to our knowledge) novel semi-adversarial setting in which the adversary retains a large amount of flexibility. In this setting, we present an algorithm based on online mirror descent that learns expert weights in a way that attains
O(\sqrt{T}\log T)
expected regret as compared with the best weights in hindsight.

6.1
Introduction

6.1.1
Learning expert weights for logarithmic pooling

In Section
2.2
, we motivated logarithmic pooling as a natural method of forecast aggregation that has compelling theoretical properties and works well in practice. To recall, the logarithmic pool of forecasts
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{m}\in\Delta_{n}
with weight vector
\mathbf{w}\in\Delta_{m}
is defined by

p^{*}_{j}(\mathbf{w})=c(\mathbf{w})\prod_{i=1}^{m}(p^{i}_{j})^{w_{i}},

for all
j\in[n]
, where
c(\mathbf{w})
is a normalizing constant (that depends on the weights).

But where do the expert weights come from? That is, how should an aggregator determine what weight to assign to each expert? It is common for the aggregator to learn the correct vector of weights over time, based on the experts’ track record
(
Tetlock & Gardner 2015a
)
. Learning expert weights over time is a sequential decision making problem: for each of
T
time steps, experts submit forecasts to the aggregator, and the aggregator aggregates them using logarithmic pooling, with weights that the aggregator chooses. Then an outcome is realized and the aggregator receives a score depending on the accuracy of the aggregate forecast. The aggregator adjusts expert weights for the next time step, perhaps increasing the weights of experts who did well while decreasing the weights of experts who did poorly.

This problem falls into the well-studied and wide-reaching field of online learning – and more specifically, online prediction with expert advice
(
Cesa-Bianchi & Lugosi 2006a
)
. Generally, algorithms for online learning problems are assessed based on their
regret
relative to some baseline. In our case, the natural baseline is the best possible weight vector in hindsight. That is, we are looking for a weight selection algorithm whose overall performance over the
T
time steps is guaranteed to be almost as good as using the best weight vector
\mathbf{w}^{*}
in hindsight.

We previously discussed online learning of expert weights in Chapter
5
. In that chapter, we introduced
quasi-arithmetic (QA) pooling
as a way of aggregating forecasts with respect to a given proper scoring rule
s
. Specifically, the QA pool with respect to a proper scoring rule
s
is the forecast that guarantees the largest possible expected overperformance (as judged by the scoring rule) compared with the strategy of choosing a random expert to trust.
1
1

1

More formally, the QA pool maximizes the minimum improvement in the score (over possible outcomes).
We saw that QA pooling with respect to the quadratic scoring rule is linear pooling (averaging the experts’ forecasts), while QA pooling with respect to the logarithmic scoring rule is logarithmic pooling.

In Section
5.4
, we gave an algorithm for online-learning expert weights for QA pooling with respect to any
bounded
proper scoring rule
s
. The algorithm had the property that, asymptotically with the number of time steps
T
, the average score of the algorithm (according to
s
) was guaranteed to be almost as large as the average score of an aggregator who chose the best vector of weights in hindsight. In online learning terminology, this means that the algorithm has
vanishing regret
(or, synonymously,
no regret
).

However, the log scoring rule is not bounded, so the aforementioned result does
not
give a no-regret algorithm for learning weights for logarithmic pooling (if regret is defined with respect to the log scoring rule). The purpose of this chapter is to fill that gap.

A note on terminology:
In this chapter, we will use the
log loss
instead of the log score, as is standard in the learning literature. The log loss is simply the negative of the log score: while the log score of the forecast
\mathbf{p}
under outcome
j
is
\ln(p_{j})
, the log loss is
-\ln(p_{j})
.

6.1.2
Choosing the right benchmark

In Chapter
5
, we established a connection between the log loss and logarithmic pooling, arguing that if the log loss is used for elicitation, then it makes sense to use logarithmic pooling for aggregation. The goal of this work is to develop an algorithm for learning weights for logarithmic pooling in a way that achieves vanishing regret as judged by the log loss function. Within the field of online prediction with expert advice, this is a particularly challenging setting. In part, this is because the losses are potentially unbounded. However, that is not the whole story: finding weights for
linear
pooling, by contrast, is a well-studied problem that has been solved even in the context of log loss. On the other hand, because logarithmic pooling behaves more as a geometric than an arithmetic mean, if some expert assigns a very low probability to the eventual outcome (and the other experts do not) then the logarithmic pool will also assign a low probability, incurring a large loss. This makes the combination of logarithmic pooling with log loss particularly difficult.

We require that our algorithm not have access to the experts’ forecasts when choosing weights: an algorithm that chooses weights in a way that depends on forecasts can output an essentially arbitrary function of the forecasts, and thus may do something other than learn optimal weights for logarithmic pooling. For example, suppose that
m=n=2
and an aggregator wishes to subvert our intentions and take an equally weighted
linear
pool of the experts’ forecasts. Without knowing the experts’ forecasts, this is impossible; on the other hand, if the aggregator knew that e.g.
\mathbf{p}_{1}=(90\%,10\%)
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}=(50\%,50\%)
, they could assign weights for logarithmic pooling so as to produce the post-hoc desired result, i.e.
(70\%,30\%)
. We wish to disallow this.

One might suggest the following setup: at each time step, the algorithm selects weights for each expert. Subsequently, an adversary chooses each expert’s forecast and the outcome, after which the algorithm and each expert incur a log loss. Unfortunately – due to the unboundedness of log loss and the behavior of logarithmic pooling – vanishing regret guarantees in this setting are impossible.

Example 6.1.1
.

Consider the case of
m=n=2
. Without loss of generality, suppose that the algorithm assigns Expert 1 a weight
w\geq 0.5
in the first time step. The adversary chooses reports
(e^{-T},1-e^{-T})
for Expert 1 and
\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)
for Expert 2, and for Outcome 1 to happen. The logarithmic pool of the forecasts turns out to be approximately
(e^{-wT},1-e^{-wT})
, so the algorithm incurs a log loss of approximately
wT\geq 0.5T
, compared to
O(1)
loss for Expert 2. On subsequent time steps, Expert 2 is perfect (assigns probability
1
to the correct outcome), so the algorithm cannot catch up.

What goes wrong in Example
6.1.1
is that the adversary has full control over experts’ forecast
and
the realized outcome, and is not required to couple the two in any way. This unreasonable amount of adversarial power motivates assuming that the experts are
calibrated
: for example, if an expert assigns a 10% chance to an outcome, there really is a 10% chance of that outcome (conditional on the expert’s information).

We propose the following setting: an adversary chooses a joint probability distribution over the experts’ beliefs and the outcome – subject to the constraint that each expert is
calibrated.
Loosely speaking, this means that each expert correctly assesses the probability each outcome based on the information they have: if an expert reports
\mathbf{p}
, then each event
j
really does have a probability
p_{j}
of happening, based on the expert’s information. (We formally define calibration in Section
6.3.1
.) The adversary retains full control over correlations between forecasts and outcomes, subject to this calibration property. Subsequently, nature randomly samples each expert’s belief and the eventual outcome from the distribution. In this setting, we seek to prove upper bounds on the expected value of our algorithm’s regret.

Why impose this constraint, instead of a different one? Our reasons are twofold: theoretical and empirical. From a theoretical standpoint, the assumption that experts are calibrated is natural because experts who form Bayesian rational beliefs based on evidence will be calibrated, regardless of how much or how little evidence they have. The assumption is also motivated if we model experts as learners rather than Bayesian agents: even if a forecaster starts out completely uninformed, they can quickly become calibrated in a domain simply by observing the frequency of events
(
Foster & Vohra 1997a
)
.

Second, recent work has shown that modern deep neural networks are calibrated when trained on a proper loss function such as log loss. This is true for a variety of tasks, including image classification
(
Minderer et al. 2021a
;
Hendrycks et al. 2020a
)
and language modeling
(
Kadavath et al. 2022a
;
Desai & Durrett 2020a
;
OpenAI 2023a
)
; see
Blasiok et al. 2023a
for a review of the literature. We may wish to use an ensemble of off-the-shelf neural networks for some prediction or classification task. If we trust these networks to be calibrated (as suggested by recent work), then we may wish to learn to ensemble these experts (models) in a way that has strong worst-case theoretical guarantees under the calibration assumption.

Logarithmic pooling is particularly sensible in the context of calibrated experts because it takes confident forecasts “more seriously” as compared with linear pooling (simple averaging). If Expert 1 reports probability distribution
(0.1\%,99.9\%)
over two outcomes and Expert 2 reports
(50\%,50\%)
, then the logarithmic pool (with equal weights) is approximately
(3\%,97\%)
, as compared with a linear pool of roughly
(25\%,75\%)
. If Expert 1 is calibrated (as we are assuming), then the
(0.1\%,99.9\%)
forecast entails very strong evidence in favor of Outcome 2 over Outcome 1. Meanwhile, Expert 2’s forecast gives no evidence either way. Thus, it is sensible for the aggregate to point to Outcome 2 over Outcome 1 with a fair amount of confidence.

As another example, suppose that Expert 1 reports
(0.04\%,49.98\%,49.98\%)
and Expert 2 reports
(49.98\%,0.04\%,49.98\%)
(a natural interpretation: Expert 1 found strong evidence against Outcome 1 and Expert 2 found strong evidence against Outcome 2). If both experts are calibrated, a sensible aggregate should arguably assign nearly all probability to Outcome 3. Logarithmic pooling returns roughly
(2.7\%,2.7\%,94.6\%)
, which (unlike linear pooling) accomplishes this.

Since we are allowing our algorithm to learn the optimal logarithmic pool, perhaps there is hope to compete not just with the best expert in hindsight, but the optimally weighted logarithmic pool of experts in hindsight. We will aim to compete with this stronger benchmark.

This work demonstrates that the “calibrated experts” condition allows us to prove regret bounds when no such bounds are possible for an unrestricted adversary. While that is our primary motivation, the relaxation may also be of independent interest. For example, even in settings where vanishing regret is attainable in the presence of an unrestricted adversary, even stronger regret bounds might be achievable if calibration is assumed.

6.1.3
Our main result

Is vanishing regret possible in our setting? Our main result is that the answer is yes. We exhibit an algorithm that attains expected regret that scales as
O(\sqrt{T}\log T)
with the number of time steps
T
. Our algorithm uses online mirror descent (OMD) with the
Tsallis entropy regularizer

R(\mathbf{w})=\frac{-1}{\alpha}(w_{1}^{\alpha}+\dots+w_{m}^{\alpha})
and step size
\eta\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}\ln T}
, where any choice of
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
attains the regret bound.

Our proof has two key ideas. One is to use the calibration property to show that the gradient of loss with respect to the weight vector is likely to be small (Section
6.4.4
). This is how we leverage the calibration property to turn an intractable setting into one where – despite the unboundedness of log loss and the behavior of logarithmic pooling – there is hope for vanishing regret.

The other key idea (Section
6.4.3
) involves keeping track of a function that, roughly speaking, reflects how much “regret potential” the algorithm has. We show that if the aforementioned gradient updates are indeed small, then this potential function decreases in value at each time step. This allows us to upper bound the algorithm’s regret by the initial value of the potential function.

This potential argument is an important component of the proof. A naïve analysis might seek to use our bounds on the gradient steps to myopically bound the contribution to regret at each time step. Such an analysis, however, does not achieve our
O(\sqrt{T}\log T)
regret bound. In particular, an adversary can force a large accumulation of regret if some experts’ weights are very small (specifically by making the experts with small weights more informed than those with large weights) – but by doing so, the small weights increase and the adversary “spends down” its potential. Tracking this potential allows us to take this nuance into consideration, improving our bound.

We extend our main result by showing that the result holds even if experts are only approximately calibrated: so long as no expert understates the probability of an outcome by more than a constant factor, we still attain the same regret bound (see Corollary
6.4.11
). We also show in Section
6.5
that no OMD algorithm with a constant step size can attain expected regret better than
\Omega(\sqrt{T})
.

6.2
Related work

In the subfield of
prediction with expert advice,
for
T
time steps, experts report “predictions” from a decision space
\mathcal{D}
(often, as in our case, the space of probability distributions over a set of outcomes). A forecaster must then output their own prediction from
\mathcal{D}
. Then, predictions are assessed according to a loss function. See
Cesa-Bianchi & Lugosi 2006a
for an survey of this field.

We are particularly interested in
mixture forecasters
: forecasters who, instead of choosing an expert to trust at each time step, aggregate the expert’ reports. Linear mixtures, i.e. convex combinations of predictions, have been especially well-studied, generally with the goal of learning weights for the convex combination to compete with the best weights in hindsight. Standard convex optimization algorithms achieve
O(\sqrt{T})
regret for bounded, convex losses, but it is sometimes possible to do better. For example, if the loss function is bounded and exp-concave, then logarithmic regret in
T
is attainable
(
Cesa-Bianchi & Lugosi 2006a
, §3.3)
.

Portfolio theory studies optimal stock selection for maximizing return on investment, often in a no-regret setting.
Cover 1991a
introduced the “universal portfolio” algorithm, which, for each of
T
time steps, selects a portfolio (convex combination of stocks). Our setting translates naturally to Cover’s: experts play the role of stocks, and the return of a stock corresponds to the probability that and expert assigns to the eventual outcome. The universal portfolio algorithm achieves logarithmic regret compared with the best portfolio in hindsight
(
Cover & Ordentlich 1996a
)
; in our terms, this means that logarithmic regret (for log loss) is attainable for the linear pooling of experts. See
Li & Hoi 2014a
for a survey of this area.

To our knowledge, learning weights for
logarithmic
pooling has not been previously studied. As shown in Example
6.1.1
, it is not possible to achieve vanishing regret if the setting is fully adversarial. We relax our setting by insisting that the experts be calibrated (see Section
6.3.1
). To our knowledge, online prediction with expert advice has also not previously been studied under this condition.

The calibration condition can be equivalently restated as follows: the experts’ forecasts are based on signals drawn from an information structure – possibly a different one at each time step – that is unknown to the aggregator, and experts report accurate probabilities conditional on their signals (see Remark
6.3.2
).
Babichenko & Garber 2021a
investigated online prediction with expert advice under the assumption that the experts’ signals are drawn from a particular type of information structure, which they called the ‘‘partial evidence environment.’’
2
2

2

Essentially, the partial evidence environment is an adaptation of the partial information framework (see Section
2.3
) to the case of binary outcomes. There is a set of signals, which are independent conditional on the outcome, and every expert knows a subset of the signals (the same subset on each time step). Further, the authors assume that knowledge of the information structure would allow a perfect aggregator to infer all relevant information about the experts’ signals from their forecasts.
Under the paper’s assumptions, the Bayesian optimal aggregate is a generalized logarithmic pool (as introduced in Section
2.2.3
), and the authors show that an aggregator can often learn this optimal aggregate with vanishing regret. By contrast, we do not make any assumptions about the information structures, and instead aim to compete not with the optimal aggregate but with the best weighted mixture of experts in hindsight.

6.3
Preliminaries

6.3.1
Calibration property

We define calibration as follows. Note that the definition is in the context of our setting, i.e.
m
experts reporting probability distributions
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{m}
over
n
outcomes. We will use
J
to denote the random variable corresponding to the outcome, i.e.
J
takes values in
[n]
.

Definition 6.3.1
.

Consider a joint probability distribution
\mathbb{P}
over experts’ reports and the outcome. We say that expert
i
is
calibrated
if for all
\mathbf{p}\in\Delta_{n}
and
j\in[n]
, we have that

\text{Pr}\left[J=j\mid\mathbf{p}^{i}=\mathbf{p}\right]=p_{j}.

That is, expert
i
is calibrated if the probability distribution of
J
conditional on their report
\mathbf{p}^{i}
is precisely
\mathbf{p}^{i}
. We say that
\mathbb{P}
satisfies the
calibration property
if every expert is calibrated.

The key intuition behind the usefulness of calibration is that if an expert claims that an outcome is very unlikely, this is strong evidence that the outcome is in fact unlikely. In Section
6.4.4
we will use the calibration property to show that the gradient of the loss with respect to the weight vector is likely to be relatively small at each time step.

6.3.2
Our online learning setting

The setting for our online learning problem is as follows. For each time step
t\in[T]
:

(1)

Our algorithm reports a weight vector
\mathbf{w}^{t}\in\Delta_{m}
.

(2)

An adversary (with knowledge of
\mathbf{w}^{t}
) constructs a probability distribution
\mathbb{P}
, over reports and the outcome, that satisfies the calibration property.

(3)

Reports
\mathbf{p}^{t,1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{t,m}
and an outcome
j^{t}
are sampled from
\mathbb{P}
.

(4)

The
loss
of a weight vector
\mathbf{w}
is defined as
L^{t}(\mathbf{w}):=-\ln(p^{*}_{j^{t}}(\mathbf{w}))
, the log loss of the logarithmic pool of
\mathbf{p}^{t,1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{t,m}
with weights
\mathbf{w}
. Our algorithm incurs loss
L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})
.

Remark 6.3.2
.

An equivalent, more mechanistic definition of calibration views
\mathbb{P}
instead as a joint probability distribution over signals received by each expert and the outcome, i.e. as an information structure (see Section
2.3
). Each expert’s probability distribution is then the actual probability distribution over the outcome conditioned on their signal. We can reinterpret our online learning setting in light of this view. Specifically, instead of directly selecting a distribution over the experts’ forecasts and the outcome, the adversary selects an information structure describing the probability distribution over the outcome and each expert’s signal. The signals and outcome are then drawn at random from the information structure, and each expert’s report is the true probability distribution over the outcome conditioned on their signal.

Formally, the adversary selects an information structure
\mathcal{I}_{t}=(\Omega^{t},\mathbb{P}^{t},(\sigma_{1}^{t},\dots,\sigma_{m}^{t}),Y^{t})
, where
Y^{t}
is a random variables taking on one of
n
possible values, namely the standard basis vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, nature randomly samples
\omega^{t}
according to
\mathbb{P}^{t}
. The outcome
j^{t}
is the coordinate in which
Y^{t}(\omega^{t})
has a
1
, and each expert
i
’s report
\mathbf{p}^{t,i}
is
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{t}\mid\sigma_{i}^{t}(\omega^{t})\right]
.

We define the
regret
of our algorithm as

\text{Regret}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})-\min_{\mathbf{w}\in\Delta_{m}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}L^{t}(\mathbf{w}).

That is, the benchmark for regret is the best weight vector in hindsight. Since our setting involves randomness, our goal is to provide an algorithm with vanishing
expected
regret against any adversarial strategy, where the expectation is taken over the sampling in Step
(3)
.

Even subject to the calibration property, the adversary has a large amount of flexibility, because the adversary retains control over the correlation between different experts’ forecasts. An unrestricted adversary has exponentially many degrees of freedom (as a function of the number of experts), whereas the calibration property imposes a mere linear number of constraints.
3
3

3

This follows from the perspective of the adversary choosing an information structure from which experts’ signals are drawn. The information structure specifies the probability of every possible combination of signals received by the experts, and thus has dimension that is exponential in the number of experts. The calibration property imposes linearly many constraints on this space.

6.3.3
Our algorithm

We use Algorithm
3
to accomplish this goal. The algorithm is online mirror descent (OMD) on the weight vector. Fix any
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
. We use the regularizer

R(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{-1}{\alpha}(w_{1}^{\alpha}+\dots+w_{m}^{\alpha}).

This is known as the Tsallis entropy regularizer; see e.g.
Zimmert & Seldin 2021a
for previous use in the online learning literature. We obtain the same result (up to a multiplicative factor that depends on
\alpha
) regardless of the choice of
\alpha
. Because no choice of
\alpha
stands out, we prove our result for all
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
simultaneously.

We will generally use a step size
\eta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}\ln T}\cdot\frac{1}{12m^{(1+\alpha)/2}n}
. However, in the (unlikely, as we show) event that some expert’s weight becomes unusually small, we will reduce the step size.

R(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{-1}{\alpha}(w_{1}^{\alpha}+\dots+w_{m}^{\alpha})
;

//
Any
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
will work

\eta\leftarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}\ln T}\cdot\frac{1}{12m^{(1+\alpha)/2}n}
;

\mathbf{w}^{1}\leftarrow(1/m,\dots,1/m)
;

for

t=1
to
T

do

if

\eta\leq\min_{i}((w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha})

then

\eta_{t}\leftarrow\min(\eta_{t-1},\eta)
;

else

\eta_{t}\leftarrow\min(\eta_{t-1},\min_{i}w_{i}^{t})
;

//
Edge case; happens with low probability

end if

Observe loss function
L^{t}
;

//
L^{t}
is chosen as described in Section
6.3.2

Define
\mathbf{w}^{t+1}
such that
\nabla R(\mathbf{w}^{t+1})=\nabla R(\mathbf{w}^{t})-\eta_{t}\nabla L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})
;

end for

ALGORITHM 3

OMD algorithm for learning weights for logarithmic pooling

In Appendix
C
, we prove that Algorithm
3
is efficient, taking
O(mn)
time per time step.

Theorem
6.3.3
formally states our no-regret guarantee.

Theorem 6.3.3
.

For any adversarial strategy, the expected regret
4
4

4

The given asymptotics assume that
T\gg m,n
, i.e. ignore terms that are lower-order in
T
.
of Algorithm
3
is at most

O\left(m^{(3-\alpha)/2}n\sqrt{T}\log T\right).

6.4
Proof of no-regret guarantee

In this section, we prove Theorem
6.3.3
.

6.4.1
Outline of proof

We use the following fact, which follows from the fact that the score of a QA pool of forecasts is concave in the forecasts’ weights (see Chapter
5
).

Proposition 6.4.1

(Follows from Theorem
5.4.1
)
.

Let
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{m}
be forecasts over
n
outcomes,
j\in[n]
be an outcome, and
\mathbf{w}\in\Delta_{m}
be a weight vector. Let
\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})
be the logarithmic pool of the forecasts with weight vector
\mathbf{w}
and let
L(\mathbf{w}):=-\ln(p_{j}^{*}(\mathbf{w}))
be the log loss of
\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})
if Outcome
j
is realized. Then
L
is a convex function.

In particular, all of our loss functions
L^{t}
are convex, which means that standard regret bounds apply. In particular, to bound the expected regret of Algorithm
3
, we will use a well-known regret bound for follow the regularized leader (FTRL) with linearized losses
(
Hazan 2023a
, Lemma 5.3)
, which in our case is equivalent to OMD.
5
5

5

This equivalence is due to our choice of regularizer, as we never need to project
\mathbf{w}^{t}
.

Lemma 6.4.2

(Follows from
Hazan 2023a
)
.

If
\eta_{t}=\eta
for all
t
, the regret of Algorithm
3
is at most

\frac{1}{\eta}\left(\max_{\mathbf{w}\in\Delta_{m}}R(\mathbf{w})-\min_{\mathbf{w}\in\Delta_{m}}R(\mathbf{w})\right)+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle\nabla L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t}),\mathbf{w}^{t}-\mathbf{w}^{t+1}\right\rangle.

Informally, this bound means that if the vectors
\nabla L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})
are small in magnitude, our regret is also small. Conversely, if some
\nabla L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})
is large, this may be bad for our regret bound. We expect the gradient of the loss to be large if some expert is very wrong (assigns a very low probability to the correct outcome), since the loss would then be steeply increasing as a function of that expert’s weight. Fortunately, the calibration property guarantees this to be unlikely. Specifically, we define the
small gradient assumption
as follows.

Definition 6.4.3
.

Define
\gamma:=12n\ln T
. The
small gradient assumption
holds for a particular run of Algorithm
3
if for every
t\in[T]
and
i\in[m]
, we have

-\frac{\gamma}{w_{i}^{t}}\leq\partial_{i}L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})\leq\gamma,

where
\partial_{i}
denotes the partial derivative with respect to the
i
-th weight.
6
6

6

See Equation
C.2
for an expression of this quantity in terms of the experts’ reports and weights.

In Section
6.4.4
, we prove that the small gradient assumption is very likely to hold. This is a key conceptual step in our proof, as it is where we leverage the calibration property to prove bounds that ultimately let us bound our algorithm’s regret. We then use the low likelihood of the small gradient assumption failing in order to bound the contribution to the expected regret from the case where the assumption fails to hold.

In Sections
6.4.2
and
6.4.3
, we bound regret under the condition that the small gradient assumption holds. We show that under the assumption, for all
i,t
we have
(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha}\geq\eta
. Consequently,
\eta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}\ln T}\cdot\frac{1}{12m^{(1+\alpha)/2}n}
at all time steps, so we can apply Lemma
6.4.2
. The first term in the bound is
O(1/\eta)=O(\sqrt{T}\log T)
. As for the summation term, we upper bound it by keeping track of the following quantity:

\varphi(t):=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left\langle\nabla L^{s}(\mathbf{w}^{s}),\mathbf{w}^{s}-\mathbf{w}^{s+1}\right\rangle+19m^{2}\gamma^{2}\eta(T-t)-4\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ln w_{i}^{t+1}.

The first term is exactly the summation in Lemma
6.4.2
up through step
t
. The
19m^{2}\gamma^{2}\eta
is something akin to an upper bound on the value of
\left\langle\nabla L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t}),\mathbf{w}^{t}-\mathbf{w}^{t+1}\right\rangle
at a given time step (times
T-t
