a circuit tends to accept certain classes of inputs.

Deductive estimates of mathematical quantities abound in fields such as number theory, theoretical computer science, and discrete mathematics, but there is little understanding of the rules that govern such estimation. Much as there is a formal definition of a mathematical proof and an algorithm that checks proofs for validity, there may be a formal definition of a valid deductive argument and an algorithm that uses such arguments to estimate mathematical quantities.

In this chapter, we search for such a formalization, focusing our attention on deductively estimating the acceptance probabilities of boolean circuits. We define two properties –
linearity
and
respect for proofs
– that a deductive estimation algorithm ought to satisfy, and then provide an efficient algorithm that satisfies those properties. We then show a negative result: that no efficient algorithm can satisfy linearity, respect for proofs, and another property that we call
0-1 boundedness,
assuming that
P\neq PP
. We discuss additional desirable properties for a deductive estimation algorithm and then conclude with a discussion of potential applications to detecting anomalous neural network behavior.

More so than any previous chapter, this one shows a work in progress. It raises more questions than it answers, and some of the raised questions are not formally stated. However, the question of whether deductive estimation can be formalized seems like a fundamental one, and further progress in this direction may have important applications for building advanced AI systems safely.

9.1
Introduction

Suppose we wish to estimate the acceptance probability
p(C)
of a boolean circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
on a uniformly random input – that is, the fraction of inputs
x
for which
C(x)=1
. We can contrast two different types of approaches to such an estimation problem:
inductive
approaches and
deductive
approaches.

By an
inductive
approach, we mean an approach based on observing the input-output behavior of
C
on some inputs. For example, one inductive approach is to sample inputs to
C
uniformly at random and observe the fraction of inputs that
C
accepts (outputs
1
on).
1
1

1

We consider pseudorandom sampling to also be inductive. The use of randomness is not required for an argument to be considered inductive.
Such an estimation procedure is very effective, at least in terms of estimating
p(C)
with a small additive error. However, it does not provide any insight about
why

C
’s acceptance probability is what it is.

By a
deductive
approach, we mean an approach that uses structural observations about
C
in order to estimate
p(C)
. We call such structural observations
deductive arguments.
2
2

2

We use the term “deductive argument” instead of the term “heuristic argument” (as used in e.g.
Christiano et al. 2022a
) to stress the difference with inductive arguments (which we do not consider in this chapter) and to emphasize that a proof is a special type of deductive argument.
While an inductive approaches would generally treat a circuit as a black box and estimate its acceptance probability using input-output behavior, deductive arguments treat circuits as white boxes. Let us illustrate with a few examples.

Example 9.1.1
.

Suppose that
C
takes as input a triple
(a,b,c)
of positive integers, computes
\max(a,b)
and
\max(b,c)
, and accepts if they are equal. A deductive argument about
p(C)
might point out that if
b
is the largest of the three integers, then
\max(a,b)=b=\max(b,c)
and so
C
will accept, and that this happens with probability roughly
\frac{1}{3}
.

Example 9.1.2
.

Suppose that
C(x)
computes
\text{SHA-256}(x)
(the output of SHA-256 is a 256-bit string) and accepts if the first 128 bits (interpreted as an integer) are larger than the last 128 bits. One can make a deductive argument about
p(C)
by making repeated use of the
presumption of independence

(
Christiano et al. 2022a
)
. In particular, the SHA-256 circuit consists of components (such as the majority circuit, or addition modulo
2^{32}
) that produce uniformly random outputs on independent, uniformly random inputs. Thus, a deductive argument that repeatedly presumes that the inputs to each component are independent concludes that the output of SHA-256 consists of independent, uniformly random bits. It would then follow that the probability that the first 128 bits of the output are larger than the last 128 bits is
\frac{1}{2}
.

Example 9.1.3
.

Suppose that
C
is a particular CNF with
k
clauses of three literals each. A deductive argument about
p(C)
might point out that
C
is a CNF with
k
clauses of three literals each, and that on average, CNFs with this structure accept
\left(\frac{7}{8}\right)^{k}
fraction of inputs. A more sophisticated argument might point out particular structural patterns in
C
(e.g. that the variable
x_{1}
appears with positive sign in every clause) and reason about the average acceptance probability of CNFs with that structure.

Example 9.1.4
.

Suppose that
C
takes as input an integer
k
between
e^{100}
and
e^{101}
and accepts if
k
and
k+2
are both prime. A deductive argument about
p(C)
might point out that the density of primes in this range is roughly
1\%
, so if we presume that the event that
k
is prime and the event that
k+2
is prime are independent, then we get an estimate of
0.01\%
. A more sophisticated argument might take this one step further by pointing out that if
k
is prime, then
k
is odd, so
k+2
is odd, which makes
k+2
more likely to be prime (by a factor of
2
), suggesting an estimate of
0.02\%
. A yet more sophisticated argument might point out that additionally, if
k
is prime, then
k
is not divisible by
3
, which makes
k+2

more
likely to be divisible by
3
, which reduces the chance that
k+2
is prime, and would make similar arguments for divisibility by
5
,
7
, and so on.

In a sense, deductive arguments generalize proofs. A formal proof about the value of
p(C)
is a type of deductive argument, but in many cases it is infeasible to prove even basic facts about a circuit’s acceptance probability. In Example
9.1.2
, for instance, there is likely no short proof that
p(C)
is between
\frac{1}{3}
and
\frac{2}{3}
, but there is a very simple deductive argument. Deductive arguments let us draw conclusions based on circuits’ structural properties in many cases where proofs do not.

Inductive approaches to circuit estimation hold some obvious advantages over deductive ones. First, they are fast and straightforward. Second, they have strong accuracy guarantees: by sampling random inputs, one can efficiently estimate
p(C)
with a small additive error with high probability. By comparison, a deductive estimate can be incorrect (for example, the naïve argument that gave the
0.01\%
estimate in Example
9.1.4
was substantially wrong). So why bother with deductive estimates?

One answer is that deductive estimation may be helpful in situations where sampling-based approaches will fail. For example, suppose we wish to estimate the acceptance probability of a CNF
C
that has a low acceptance probability, with a small multiplicative error. Sampling inputs will likely fail to find any satisfying assignments. On the other hand, one could hope to be able to reason deductively about
C
’s acceptance probability if given deductive arguments based on
C
’s structure.

More importantly, however, deductive arguments can give us a
mechanistic understanding
of a circuit and provide insight about
why
the circuit has a certain acceptance probability. Such understanding allows us to answer questions like: does
C
accept inputs
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
“for the same reason” or “for different reasons”? In Example
9.1.1
,
C
might accept an input because
b
is larger than both
a
and
c
, or because
a=c
. Deductive arguments can allow us to draw such distinctions; inductive arguments do not. Gaining such a mechanistic understanding is an important motivation for exploring deductive circuit estimation.

The purpose of this line of work is to create a deductive estimation algorithm. A
deductive estimation algorithm
takes as input a boolean circuit,
3
3

3

We are also interested in the more general problem of estimating the expected outputs of arithmetic circuits and of computer programs more generally, but in this chapter we focus on boolean circuits. Boolean circuits are a special case that may capture the core difficulties of the more general problem. Further, boolean circuits have a canonical choice for the distribution of inputs (uniformly random bit strings), and some of our discussion (e.g. our definition of linearity in Section
9.3
) is simplified in the case of boolean cirucits.
together with a list of deductive arguments about the circuit, and outputs an estimate of the circuit’s acceptance probability based on the provided arguments. Creating such an algorithm requires surmounting several challenges. First, it is necessary to specify a formal language in which deductive arguments may be stated; deductive arguments given as input to the estimation algorithm will be written in this formal language. Second, desiderata for the algorithm should be defined: what properties should a deductive estimation algorithm satisfy? Finally, the third challenge is to create an algorithm that satisfies those properties.

An analogy to formal proof verification may be instructive. The central task of proof verification is the (solved) problem of finding a verification algorithm that, given a mathematical statement and an alleged proof of the statement, verifies the proof. In our setting, the circuit (whose acceptance probability we want to estimate) is analogous to the mathematical statement, and the list of deductive arguments is analogous to the proof. Table
9.1
describes the analogy in more detail.

Deductive circuit estimation

Formal proof verification

Deductive estimation algorithm

Proof verifier

Boolean circuit

Formal mathematical statement

List of deductive arguments

Alleged proof of statement

Formal language for deductive arguments

Formal language for proofs

Desiderata for estimation algorithm

Soundness and completeness

Algorithm’s estimate of circuit

Proof verifier’s output (accept or reject)

Table 9.1:
We are interested in developing a deductive estimation algorithm for boolean circuits. There are similarities between this task and the (solved) task of developing an algorithm for verifying formal proofs of mathematical statements. This table illustrates the analogy. Importantly, the purpose of a deductive estimation algorithm is to incorporate the deductive arguments that it has been given as input, rather than to generate its own arguments. The output of a deductive estimation algorithm is only as sophisticated as the arguments that it has been given.

In Section
9.2
, we begin with a discussion of deductive circuit estimation via linear regression. That is, we will be interested in producing a linear estimator for a class of circuits in terms of a list of features. We illustrate this approach with an example of CNF estimation. The purpose of this section is to provide an example of what deductive arguments for circuit estimation might look like, and how a deductive estimation algorithm might incorporate those arguments into an estimate of the acceptance probability of a circuit.

In Section
9.3
, we examine the problem of deductive circuit estimation in more generality, asking the question: what properties should a deductive estimation algorithm satisfy? We introduce two properties – which we call
linearity
and
respect for proofs
– and describe an algorithm that satisfies both properties. Then, in Section
9.4
, we discuss additional properties. We introduce a basic property called
0-1 boundedness
and show that it is impossible to satisfy in addition to linearity and respect for proofs, unless
P=PP
. We discuss two other properties –
iterated estimation
and
pulling out known factors
– that it would be desirable to satisfy, and leave open the question of whether they can be satisfied.

Finally, in Section
9.5
, we conclude with a discussion of potential applications to detecting anomalous behavior in neural networks.

9.1.1
Related work

The work most closely related to ours is
Christiano et al. 2022a
, which posed the question of whether it is possible to formalize the process of deductively estimating the value of a mathematical expression, such as the expectation of a random variable. The authors posited the
presumption of independence
as an underlying principle of deductive estimation: two random variables should be presumed independent until an argument to the contrary is presented. Thus, a deductive estimation algorithm might start by assuming that all sub-expressions are independent, and then update its estimate after incorporating knowledge about various dependencies. This chapter attacks the problem of deductive estimation from a different perspective, but is a continuation of that work.

There has not been much other prior work on formalizing deductive estimation. Perhaps the closest is
Tao 2012a
, which also posits that two quantities should be presumed independent unless there is a good reason to the contrary, and uses this principle to give a heuristic justification for the ABC conjecture from number theory. Heuristic arguments for mathematical claims are common in fields such as number theory, theoretical computer science, and discrete mathematics. For example,
Erdős & Ulam 1971a
give a heuristic argument for the truth of Fermat’s last theorem;
Mézard & Zecchina 2002a
use heuristic methods to analyze random
k
-SAT instances; and
Cornu & Hilhorst 2019a
heuristically estimate the frequencies of certain patterns in Conway’s game of life. However, little prior work attempts to formalize the rules governing such arguments.

Barak 2015a
;
Barak 2016a
are also closely related to our work. Barak asks whether it is possible to construct an estimation algorithm for a given quantity that appears reasonable to a broad class of observers. By contrast, in our framing, “observers" are deductive arguments that are given as input. Our perspective is in some ways less ambitious and in some ways more: on the one hand, we want our estimate to appear reasonable to an observer (i.e. incorporate a deductive argument) only if the observer is explicitly given as input; on the other hand, this may allow us to consider a much wider range of possible observers.

More recently,
Gowers 2023a
posited a
no-coincidence principle:
‘‘If an apparently outrageous coincidence happens in mathematics, then there is a reason for it.’’ In the language of this chapter, we might instead write: ‘‘If a circuit exhibits a surprising behavior, then there is a short deductive argument that explains the behavior.’’ (An example of a surprising behavior might be that the acceptance probability of a circuit is much larger than a naïve estimate would suggest.
4
4

4

Informally speaking: for a circuit that always outputs
1
, there should be a deductive argument that makes it seem
plausible
that the circuit always outputs
1
. On the other hand, for most circuits that do not always output
1
, no such deductive argument should exist. See
Christiano et al. 2022a
for further discussion.
)

There is a significant literature on the problem of circuit estimation. Inductive estimates are common, often under the name
approximate model counting.
Beyond straightforward uniform sampling, there are more algorithms based on more sophisticated methods such as Markov chain Monte Carlo
(
Wei & Selman 2005a
)
. If given access to an NP oracle, hashing-based approaches can give strong guarantees
(
Yang & Meel 2023a
)
. See
Chakraborty et al. 2021a
for a survey of this area. There has also been work on derandomization-based deterministic algorithms for approximate DNF counting
(
Gopalan et al. 2013a
)
.

There has also been extensive research into heuristic methods for the satisfiability problem (see e.g.
Gu et al. 1996a
). This work is similar in spirit to ours, but our goal is different. Rather than finding particular heuristic algorithms and validating them on particular instances, we are seeking a unified theoretical framework for making estimates based on a very large class of arguments.

9.2
Circuit estimation via linear regression

Often, families of circuits are easier to reason about than the individual circuits they contain. For example, it may be difficult to compute the acceptance probability of a given 3CNF with
k
clauses, but it is easy to compute the average acceptance probability of
all
3CNFs with
k
clauses. For this reason, estimating the acceptance probability of a circuit
C
is made easier by instead estimating the acceptance probabilities of
all
circuits in a family that contains
C
.

Let us denote the average acceptance probability of a (finite) family of boolean circuits
\mathscr{C}
as
p(\mathscr{C})
. That is,
p(\mathscr{C}):=\mathbb{E}_{C\sim\mathscr{C}}\left[p(C)\right]
. Consider a circuit
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
for which
p(C)
is difficult to compute. Let
\mathscr{C}
be a family of circuits containing
C
. In a sense,
p(\mathscr{C})
is a reasonable (if uninformative) estimate for
p(C)
, because this estimate is correct on average over
\mathscr{C}
.

We can ask for more informative estimates: ones that use more information about a circuit than just the fact that it belongs to
\mathscr{C}
. In general, we can hope to estimate the acceptance probabilities of the circuits in
\mathscr{C}
by using features of those circuits.

To be more precise, suppose that we have
m

features
: efficiently computable functions
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}
. Suppose further that we can compute the average value of each feature on
\mathscr{C}
(that is,
\mathbb{E}_{C\sim\mathscr{C}}\left[\varphi_{i}(C)\right]
for all
i\in m
, covariances between the features (that is,
\text{Cov}_{C\sim\mathscr{C}}(\varphi_{i}(C),\varphi_{j}(C))
for
i,j\in m
), and the covariance of each feature with the acceptance probability (that is,
\text{Cov}_{C\sim\mathscr{C}}(p(C),\varphi_{i}(C))
for all
i\in m
). Then the best linear estimator of
p(C)
in terms of the features, as measured by average squared error over
\mathscr{C}
, is given by the least squares regression formula:

\hat{p}(C)=p(\mathscr{C})+\begin{pmatrix}\text{Cov}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}(p(C^{\prime}),\varphi_{1}(C^{\prime}))\\
\vdots\\
\text{Cov}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}(p(C^{\prime}),\varphi_{m}(C^{\prime}))\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{+}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{1}(C)-\mathbb{E}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}\left[\varphi_{1}(C^{\prime})\right]\\
\vdots\\
\varphi_{n}(C)-\mathbb{E}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C^{\prime}}}\left[\varphi_{n}(C^{\prime})\right]\end{pmatrix},

(9.1)

where
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
denotes the covariance matrix of the features and
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{+}
is its Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse.

9.2.1
Example: CNF estimation

In this section, we work with a family
\mathscr{C}
consisting of
2^{3k}
3CNFs on
n
variables with
k
clauses: specifically, these CNFs will be identical except for the sign of each literal. More formally, for every
j\in[k]
and
\ell\in[3]
, fix an index
i_{j,\ell}
in
[n]
. Then, for every
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{k\times 3}
, we define

C_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}):=(x_{i_{1,1}}=b_{1,1}\vee x_{i_{1,2}}=b_{1,2}\vee x_{i_{1,3}}=b_{1,3})\wedge\dots\wedge(x_{i_{k,1}}=b_{k,1}\vee x_{i_{k,2}}=b_{k,2}\vee x_{i_{k,3}}=b_{k,3}).

That is, the indices
i_{j,\ell}
specify which variable appears at each position in the CNF, and
b_{j,\ell}
specifies the sign of the
\ell
-th literal in clause
j
. Then, for a particular choice of indices
i_{1,1},\dots,i_{k,3}
(which is implicit in the notation),
\mathscr{C}
is the set of CNFs
C_{\mathbf{b}}
for all
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{k\times 3}
.

Claim 9.2.1
.

p(\mathscr{C})=(7/8)^{k}
.

Proof.

The quantity
p(\mathscr{C})
is equal to the fraction of all choices of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
such that
C_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})=1
. Choose any given
\mathbf{x}
; we ask: if we select a random
\mathbf{b}
, what is the probability that
C_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})=1
? For each of the
k
clauses, there is a
\frac{7}{8}
probability that the clause will be satisfied by the chosen
\mathbf{b}
, and these probabilities are independent.
∎

Thus, we could estimate
p(C_{\mathbf{b}})
as
(7/8)^{k}
for all
\mathbf{b}
. As we have discussed, this is a reasonable estimate, but not a very informative one. To refine this estimate, we consider
n
features – one for each variable – which we call
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}
:

Definition 9.2.2
.

Given a circuit
C\in\mathscr{C}
, let
\mathscr{C}_{i}(C)
(for
i\in[n]
) be the set of circuits in
\mathscr{C}
that agree define with
C
on the signs of all instances of the variable
x_{i}
. Define
\varphi_{i}(C):=p(\mathscr{C}_{i}(C))
, i.e. the average acceptance probability of these circuits.

Example 9.2.3
.

Suppose that
C(x)=(x_{1}=1\vee x_{2}=1\vee x_{3}=1)\wedge(x_{1}=0\vee x_{2}=1\vee x_{4}=1)
. There are
2^{4}=16
circuits in
\mathscr{C}_{1}(C)
: specifically all circuits of the form

(x_{1}=1\vee x_{2}=b_{1,2}\vee x_{3}=b_{1,3})\wedge(x_{1}=0\vee x_{2}=b_{2,2}\vee x_{4}=b_{2,3}),

for some choice of bits
b_{1,2},b_{1,3},b_{2,2},b_{2,3}
. We can compute
\varphi_{1}(C)
by conditioning on the value of
x_{1}
:

\varphi_{1}(C)=\frac{1}{2}\text{Pr}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}}\left[x_{2}=b_{1,2}\vee x_{3}=b_{1,3}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\text{Pr}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}}\left[x_{2}=b_{2,2}\vee x_{4}=b_{2,3}\right]=\frac{3}{4}.

We can think of each
\varphi_{i}(C)
as a more informative estimate of
p(C)
than
p(\mathscr{C})
is: it is still correct on average, but now uses more information about
C
.
5
5

5

In general, features do not need to be reasonable estimates for acceptance probability – they just happen to be in this case.
An analogy to forecast aggregation may be instructive. Suppose that a circuit
C
is drawn at random from
\mathscr{C}
, and that there are
n
experts: Expert
i
knows the signs of all instances of
x_{i}
and no other signs. Then
p(\mathscr{C})
is the prior for the value of
p(C)
, and
\varphi_{i}(C)
is Expert
i
’s estimate of the value of
p(C)
.

Note that
\varphi_{i}(C)
is easy to compute. In particular, we have

\varphi_{i}(C)=\text{Pr}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\{0,1\}^{n},C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}_{i}(C)}\left[C^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=1\right]=\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{Pr}_{\mathbf{x}:x_{i}=1,C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}_{i}(C)}\left[C^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=1\right]+\text{Pr}_{\mathbf{x}:x_{i}=0,C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}_{i}(C)}\left[C^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=1\right]\right).

Now, let
C[x_{i}=1]
be the CNF obtained by plugging
x_{i}=1
into
C
and simplifying. Note that
\text{Pr}_{\mathbf{x}:x_{i}=1,C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}_{i}(C)}\left[C^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=1\right]
is equal to the average acceptance probability of all circuits that are identical to
C[x_{i}=1]
except for the signs of the literals – and finding this average probability is easy (see Claim
9.2.1
). Computing
\text{Pr}_{\mathbf{x}:x_{i}=0,C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}_{i}(C)}\left[C^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=1\right]
is similarly easy.

Thus, we can obtain
n
estimates
\varphi_{1}(C),\dots,\varphi_{n}(C)
, each of which is more informative than
p(\mathscr{C})
. We can combine them into a single estimate of
p(C)
with linear regression, as per Equation
9.1
. In particular:

•

\mathbb{E}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}\left[\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime})\right]=p(\mathscr{C})
for all
i
.

•

The
n
features are uncorrelated. This is because the
\mathscr{C}_{i}(C)
depends only on the signs of the variable
x_{i}
in
C
, and these bits are independent for each value of
i
. Thus,
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
is a diagonal matrix containing the feature variances.

•

\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime})
is a calibrated estimate of
p(C^{\prime})
, meaning that
\mathbb{E}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}\left[p(C^{\prime})\mid\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime})\right]=\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime})
. This means that
\text{Cov}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}(p(C^{\prime}),\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime}))=\text{Var}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}(\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime}))
. Therefore, we have

\begin{pmatrix}\text{Cov}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}(p(C^{\prime}),\varphi_{1}(C^{\prime}))\\
\vdots\\
\text{Cov}_{C^{\prime}\sim\mathscr{C}}(p(C^{\prime}),\varphi_{m}(C^{\prime}))\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

Putting these facts together tells us that the linear regression estimator for
p(C)
is given by

\hat{p}(C)=p(\mathscr{C})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\varphi_{i}(C)-p(\mathscr{C}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}(C)-(n-1)p(\mathscr{C}).

(9.2)

Intuitively, the estimator
\hat{p}
treats
p(\mathscr{C})
as a prior and treats the quantities
\varphi_{i}(C)-p(\mathscr{C})
as independent updates from the prior, which it combines additively.
6
6

6

We can think of this additive estimate in the context of Section
2.4
. There, we saw that if experts report estimates
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
for a mean-zero quantity
Y
, then the best linear estimate of
Y
in terms of
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
is given by
\text{diag}(\Sigma)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{Y}
, where
\mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})
and
\Sigma
is the covariance matrix of the experts’ estimates. If we consider
Y=p(C)-p(\mathscr{C})
, then
Y_{i}=\varphi_{i}(C)-p(\mathscr{C})
, and
\Sigma
is a diagonal matrix (since the
Y_{i}
’s are independent). This perspective also yields the estimate given by Equation
9.2
.

It turns out that
\hat{p}(C)
is a substantial improvement upon the prior
p(\mathscr{C})
. Figure
9.1
plots
\hat{p}(C)
versus
p(C)
for ten thousand randomly generated 3CNFs on ten variables with five clauses. In this sample,
\hat{p}(C)
explains 78% of the variance in
p(C)
.

By adding more features, it would be possible to further refine the estimate, so that it explains even more variance in
p(C)
. For example, one could add features based on the signs of
pairs
of variables, or based on other structural properties of the CNFs in
\mathscr{C}
.

Figure 9.1:
Ten thousand 3CNFs on
n=10
variables with
k=5
clauses were randomly generated. This scatter plot shows each CNF’s estimated acceptance probability
\hat{p}(C)
on the
x
-axis and its true acceptance probability
p(C)
on the
y
-axis. In this sample,
\hat{p}(C)
explains 78% of the variance in
p(C)
(that is,
r^{2}\approx 0.78
).

9.2.2
Challenges for linear regression

One challenge faced by the linear regression estimator given in Equation
9.1
is that it may output estimates
\hat{p}(C)
that are not in
[0,1]
. This makes sense, given that we sought an estimator that minimizes average squared error over
\mathscr{C}
: if
p(C)=0.1
, the estimate
\hat{p}(C)=-0.2
is no worse than the estimate
\hat{p}(C)=0.4
. On the other hand, such errors mean that the linear regression estimator may output estimates that are obviously incorrect. These errors can be corrected for individual circuits by introducing additional features. However, in Section
9.4.1
, we show that requiring all estimates to lie between
0
and
1
must come at the expense of a different desirable property.

A more significant challenge to using Equation
9.1
for deductive circuit estimation is that one may want to use features whose average values cannot be easily computed, or whose covariances with
p(C)
or with each other cannot be easily computed. In such situations, it makes sense to deductively estimate those quantities. How can we do this?

We can think of each feature
\varphi_{i}
as being computed by some arithmetic circuit.
7
7

7

The input to
\varphi_{i}
is ordinarily a circuit
C
; when thinking of
\varphi_{i}
as a circuit, we can think of its input as a string of bits that describes
C
.
Equation
9.1
applies just as well for estimating expected values of arithmetic circuits, so we can hope to use linear regression in turn to estimate the quantities we need. However, this is difficult for several reasons. First, it is unclear what family of circuits
\varphi_{i}
should be thought of as belonging to (that is, over what family of circuits to do the recursive linear regression step). Second, if the features are almost as complex as
C
itself, then estimating the covariances between them means estimating quantities that are more complex than
C
. Third, this recursive estimation scheme would need a base case: what if no features are supplied? That is, we want a reasonable “default guess” for the expressions in Equation
9.1
. These challenges are priorities for future work, because fully solving them might yield a much more generic deductive estimation algorithm.

9.3
Linearity and respect for proofs

So far we have discussed how a deductive estimation algorithm might behave when given certain kinds of arguments (features for linear regression). In this section, we take a different perspective: what properties should a deductive estimation algorithm satisfy? We define two properties, which we call
linearity
and
respect for proofs,
and give a deductive estimation algorithm that satisfies both properties.

Let us introduce notation (mostly following
Christiano et al. 2022a
). We will use the letter
G
to denote a deductive estimation algorithm.
G
will take two inputs: first, a circuit
C
(whose acceptance probability it needs to estimate), and second, a set of deductive arguments
\{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m}\}
. We will write
G(C\mid\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m})
to denote the output of
G
on these inputs.
8
8

8

We choose to use the
\mid
symbol because
G
needs to estimate
p(C)

given
– i.e. in light of – the arguments
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m}
. As we will discuss later, we may want
G
to behave akin to a conditional expectation.
For convenience, we will use
\Pi
to denote a set of arguments and write
G(C\mid\Pi)
.

How do features for linear regression, as in Section
9.2
, translate into arguments? Since we have not specified a formal language for deductive arguments, we cannot give an exact answer; however, the arguments should specify
\mathscr{C}
and the features
\varphi_{i}(C)
. The arguments should also verify the values of the quantities in Equation
9.1
(namely,
p(\mathscr{C})
,
\text{Cov}(p(C^{\prime}),\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime}))
,
\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
,
\varphi_{i}(C)
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi_{i}(C^{\prime})\right]
), so that
G
can compute
\hat{p}(C)
. For example, there should be a proof of the value of
p(\mathscr{C})
among the arguments given to
G
.
9
9

9

More generally,
G
should be able to accept deductive arguments about the values of these quantities, instead of exact proofs, as briefly discussed in Section
9.2.2
.

We now define the following notation for circuit substitutions, and then introduce the aforementioned linearity and respect for proofs properties.

Definition 9.3.1
.

Let
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit with input variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
. For
b\in\{0,1\}
, we define
C[x_{i}=b]
to be the circuit on
n-1
variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}
obtained by setting
x_{i}=b
in
C
. More generally, given a partial assignment
A
to some
\ell
of the
n
variables, we define
C[A]
to be the circuit on the remaining
n-\ell
variables that is obtained from
C
by setting the variables in
\ell
to their corresponding values in
A
. A particular input
\mathbf{x}
can be thought of as a full assignment; thus,
C[\mathbf{x}]
is a circuit that takes no inputs and outputs
C(\mathbf{x})
.

Definition 9.3.2
.

A deductive estimation algorithm
G
satisfies
linearity
if for all
n
,
C:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
,
i\in[n]
, and
\Pi
, we have

G(C\mid\Pi)=\frac{1}{2}(G(C[x_{i}=0]\mid\Pi)+G(C[x_{i}=1]\mid\Pi)).

In other words: a basic fact about circuits is that
p(C)=\frac{1}{2}(p(C[x_{i}=0])+p(C[x_{i}=1]))
, and
G
’s estimates should respect this fact. Note that linearity entails the following quite strong property: if
C
is a circuit on
n
inputs, then

G(C\mid\Pi)=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\sim\{0,1\}^{n}}G(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi).

We note, however, that
G(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi)
need not equal
C(\mathbf{x})
. Although calculating
C(\mathbf{x})
is easy,
G
does not necessarily do so:
G
’s task is not to estimate a circuit as well as it can, but rather to fully incorporate the arguments in
\Pi
into its estimate. Unless
\Pi
contains an explicit computation of
C(\mathbf{x})
,
G(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi)
may be different from
C(\mathbf{x})
.

We now introduce the “respect for proofs” property. Recall from the introduction that deductive arguments can be thought of as generalizing proofs. The respect for proofs property essentially states that proofs constitute valid deductive arguments that
G
must respect.

Definition 9.3.3
.

A deductive estimation algorithm
G
satisfies
respect for proofs
if there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given boolean circuits
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
and a proof that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}p(C_{i})\leq b
for some rational constants
a_{1},\dots,a_{k},b
, outputs an argument
\pi
such that for every set of arguments
\Pi
containing
\pi
, it is the case that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}G(C_{i}\mid\Pi)\leq b
.

In other words, if
G
is given a proof of a linear constraint on the acceptance probabilities of some circuits, then
G
’s estimates of the acceptance probabilities must also obey that linear constraint. For example:

•

For a circuit
C
, a proof that
p(C)\leq\frac{1}{2}
can be translated into an argument
\pi
such that
G(C\mid\pi)\leq\frac{1}{2}
. In fact, Definition
9.3.3
gives a stronger guarantee: for all further arguments
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\dots,\pi_{m}
, we must still have
G(C\mid\pi,\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m})\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

•

Similarly, a proof that
p(C)\geq\frac{1}{2}
can be translated into an argument
\pi^{\prime}
. If
G
is given
both

\pi
(as in the previous bullet point) and
\pi^{\prime}
, then
G(C\mid\pi,\pi^{\prime},\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m})
must
equal

\frac{1}{2}
for any further arguments
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m}
.

•

For circuits
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, the property
p(C_{1})=p(C_{2})
can likewise be stated as an intersection of two linear constraints:
p(C_{1})-p(C_{2})\leq 0
and
p(C_{2})-p(C_{1})\leq 0
. A proof of this property can be translated into arguments
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
such that
G(C_{1}\mid\pi,\pi^{\prime},\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m})=G(C_{2}\mid\pi,\pi^{\prime},\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m})
for all
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m}
.

Definition
9.3.3
requires the constraints to be linear. Why not allow
all
constraints? Suppose we have a proof that
p(C)
is either less than
\frac{1}{3}
or greater than
\frac{2}{3}
. What should
G
’s estimate of
p(C)
be, in light of such a proof? Answers between
\frac{1}{3}
and
\frac{2}{3}
are quite reasonable: while it is known that
p(C)<\frac{1}{3}
or
p(C)>\frac{2}{3}
, it is not known which of these is true, and so it would be reasonable for
G
to “split the difference” and say
\frac{1}{2}
.

Meanwhile, if it is known that
\sum_{i}a_{i}p(C_{i})\leq b
, then any reasonable tuple of estimates for
p(C_{1}),\dots,p(C_{k})
ought to satisfy this constraint as well. One formal justification for this is that for any probability distribution over tuples
(p(C_{1}),\dots,p(C_{k}))
that satisfy the constraint, the mean of the distribution will also satisfy the constraint. In fact, this property holds for all convex constraints, not just linear ones, and it would be reasonable to state a stronger version of Definition
9.3.3
that requires
G
to respect arbitrary
convex
constraints. The results of this section generalize to this stronger definition, although with some caveats; see Remark
9.3.9
for further discussion.

Essentially, linearity is a basic consistency condition on
G
’s estimates for different circuits derived from the same
base circuit
– that is, circuits that are derived from the same circuit via different variable substitutions (i.e.
C[A_{1}]
and
C[A_{2}]
for partial assignments
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
). By contrast, respect for proofs is a more general consistency condition on
G
’s estimates, but it only applies if
G
is supplied a proof. For example, if
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
provably implement the same function, then
G
does not need to output the same estimate for
C_{1}
and
C_{2}

by default
– but if it is given a proof that
p(C_{1})=p(C_{2})
, then its estimates must respect that proof. Taken together, linearity and respect for proofs are fairly powerful.
10
10

10

For example, given a network of circuits that relate to each other by elementary equivalence transformations (such as distributing AND over OR) and variable substitution, one can turn this network into a set of arguments
\Pi
that forces
G
to give consistent estimates for all circuits in the network.

On the other hand, the respect for proofs property is importantly constrained, because it
cannot
force
G
to obey a universal quantification of proofs. For example, a proof that
p(C[\mathbf{x}])\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{x}

cannot
be turned into an argument
\pi
such that
G(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\pi)\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{x}
. The respect-for-proofs property merely guarantees that for all
\mathbf{x}
, the proof that
p(C[\mathbf{x}])\geq 0
can be turned into an argument
\pi_{\mathbf{x}}
such that
G(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\pi_{\mathbf{x}})\geq 0
.
11
11

11

In theory,
G
can be given an argument
\pi_{\mathbf{x}}
for every
\mathbf{x}
, but in that case the runtime of
G
may be exponential in the size of
C
, even if
G
is a polynomial-time algorithm.
In Section
9.4.1
, we will show that one cannot hope to satisfy a stronger version of respect for proofs that allows for universal quantification (in conjunction with linearity).

9.3.1
An efficient algorithm that satisfies linearity and respect for proofs

In this section, we give a polynomial-time deductive estimation algorithm that satisfies linearity and respect for proofs. Before we do so, we clarify some conventions and definitions that will become relevant:

•

Circuits will always have labeled inputs and gates. For example, if
C^{\prime}
is an exact gate-for-gate copy of
C
, we still consider
C
and
C^{\prime}
to be different circuits. A linear constraint involving
C
does not force
G
’s estimate of
p(C^{\prime})
to satisfy the same constraint. However, a proof that
p(C)=p(C^{\prime})
will force
G
to output the same estimate for
C
as for
C^{\prime}
.

•

We can formally define a circuit as a DAG of labeled inputs and gates with one sink (output) – we will call this DAG the
base circuit
– together with a (possibly empty) partial assignment to the input variables. For example, if
C^{\prime}=C[x_{1}=0]
and
C^{\prime\prime}=C^{\prime}[x_{2}=1]
, then we formally define
C^{\prime\prime}
to be the base circuit
C
together with the partial assignment
(x_{1}=0,x_{2}=1)
. That is,
C^{\prime\prime}:=C[x_{1}=0,x_{2}=1]
. If a circuit
C
is defined as
\tilde{C}[A]
, then we call
\tilde{C}
the base circuit of
C
. Every circuit has exactly one base circuit.

•

The
size
of a partial assignment
A
, denoted
\left\lvert A\right\rvert
, is the number of variables that it defines. The
measure
of a partial assignment
A
, denoted
\mu(A)
, is defined as
2^{-\left\lvert A\right\rvert}
(this is the fraction of full assignments that satisfy
A
). Given partial assignments
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, we define
\mu(A_{1}\cup A_{2})
to be the fraction of full assignments satisfying
both

A_{1}
and
A_{2}
; we have
\mu(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\leq\mu(A_{1}),\mu(A_{2})
, and in particular
\mu(A_{1}\cup A_{2})=0
if
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
assign opposite values to the same variable.

Our deductive estimation algorithm, which we call
G_{\text{lin}}
, is defined in Algorithm
4
. We call this algorithm
G_{\text{lin}}
because it creates a linear model of a base circuit’s behavior on individual inputs, and then computes acceptance probabilities based on that model. (See the discussion immediately after the proof of Theorem
9.3.5
for details of this interpretation.) The algorithm’s implementation details are somewhat complex, but the basic idea behind it is fairly simple. Before proving the algorithm’s correctness and efficiency, we describe the intuition behind it.

input :
Boolean circuit
C
, set of deductive arguments
\Pi

output :

G_{\text{lin}}(C\mid\Pi)
, a deductive estimate of
p(C)

[leftmargin=*]

1.

Initialize an empty list
\ell
of linear constraints. For each
\pi\in\Pi
, check whether
\pi
is a valid proof of a rational linear constraint
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}p(C_{i})\leq b
. If so, add the constraint to
\ell
.

2.

For every circuit
C^{\prime}
that is the base circuit of some circuit that appears in some constraint in
\ell
: let
(A_{1},\dots,A_{m})
be the tuple of all partial assignments
A
such that
C^{\prime}[A]
appears in some constraint in
\ell
. Let
M
be the
m\times m
matrix defined by
M_{i,j}=\mu(A_{i}\cup A_{j})
. Compute vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
that span the orthogonal complement of the column space of
M
. For each
i\in[k]
, add the constraint
\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},(\mu(A_{1})p(C^{\prime}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})p(C^{\prime}[A_{m}]))\right\rangle=0
to
\ell
.

3.

Use the ellipsoid method to find a point
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
satisfying all constraints in
\ell
– that is, an estimate
\hat{p}(C^{\prime})
of
p(C^{\prime})
for every
C^{\prime}
appearing in at least one constraint in
\ell

(not just base circuits)
, such that
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
simultaneously satisfies all of the constraints in
\ell
.

4.

Let
C=\tilde{C}[A]
, where
\tilde{C}
is
C
’s base circuit. Let
(A_{1},\dots,A_{m})
be the (possibly empty) tuple of all partial assignments
A^{\prime}
such that
\tilde{C}[A^{\prime}]
appears in some constraint in
\ell
. Let
M
be the
m\times m
matrix defined by
M_{i,j}=\mu(A_{i}\cup A_{j})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}
be the lowest-norm solution to the equation
M\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\mu(A_{1})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{m}]))
.
(Below we show that this equation has a solution.)
Return
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\mu(A\cup A_{j})}{\mu(A)}\beta_{j}
.

ALGORITHM 4

Deductive estimation algorithm
G_{\text{lin}}

In Step
1
and
2
, the algorithm creates a list
\ell
of constraints on its estimates, which it will then work to satisfy. In Step
1
, it searches through all linear constraints that have been proven in
\Pi
and adds them to
\ell
. Its job will be to satisfy all of those constraints. Then, in Step
2
, the algorithm adds some additional constraints: ones that it can infer must hold simply from how the acceptance probabilities of circuits derived from the same base circuit must relate to each other. For example, we know that
p(C[x_{1}=0])+p(C[x_{1}=1])=2p(C)
, and that
p(C[x_{1}=0,x_{2}=0])+p(C[x_{1}=0,x_{2}=1])+2p(C[x_{1}=1])=4p(C)
, and so on. Adding these constraints to
\ell
is necessary to make sure that the linear system in Step
4
is consistent.

Then, in Step
3
, the algorithm solves all of the constraints that it found in
\Pi
or subsequently appended to
\ell
. The result is a point
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
whose coordinates correspond to estimated acceptance probabilities of all circuits that appeared in some constraint in
\ell
. For a circuit
C^{\prime}
, we use
\hat{p}(C^{\prime})
to denote the coordinate of
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
corresponding to the circuit
C^{\prime}
.
12
12

12

We use the ellipsoid algorithm for concreteness, but any worst-case polynomial-time algorithm for solving linear programs would work.

Finally, in Step
4
, the algorithm looks at the circuit
C
that it was provided as input – it is only at this final step that the algorithm’s behavior depends on the input circuit
C
. Let
C=\tilde{C}[A]
, where
\tilde{C}
is
C
’s base circuit.
13
13

13

Note that
C
did not necessarily appear as a constraint in
\ell
, so
\hat{p}(C)
may not be defined.
While the algorithm will only output an estimate of
C
, it must satisfy linearity, which means that its estimate for
p(C)
must be consistent with the counterfactual estimates that it would have given, had it been asked to estimate the acceptance probability of a different circuit with the same base circuit
\tilde{C}
. In order to do this, it finds coefficients
\beta_{i}
– one for every circuit
\tilde{C}[A_{i}]
that appeared in
\ell
– with the following property: if for every
\mathbf{x}
,
\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x})
were equal to the sum of
\beta_{j}
over all
j
such that
\mathbf{x}
is consistent with
A_{j}
, then for every
A_{i}
, the average output of
\tilde{C}[A_{i}]
would be equal to
\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{i}])
. That is, the algorithm creates a linear model of
\tilde{C}^{\prime}s
behavior on individual inputs that is consistent with the estimates given by
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
. From there, it is straightforward to compute the average output of
C
based on this linear model – and this is the number that the algorithm outputs.

Example 9.3.4
.

Recall from Section
9.2.1
our class of
2^{3k}
3CNFs on
n
variables with
k
clauses, parameterized by
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{k\times 3}
:

C_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})=(x_{i_{1,1}}=b_{1,1}\vee x_{i_{1,2}}=b_{1,2}\vee x_{i_{1,3}}=b_{1,3})\wedge\dots\wedge(x_{i_{k,1}}=b_{k,1}\vee x_{i_{k,2}}=b_{k,2}\vee x_{i_{k,3}}=b_{k,3}).

(Here, the indices
i_{j,\ell}\in[n]
are fixed.) Define
\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}):=C_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x})
.

Now, suppose we want to estimate
p(C)
, where
C=C_{\mathbf{z}}
for some particular
\mathbf{z}
. We can recover the estimate from Section
9.2.1
as a simple example of Algorithm
4
. Note that
C=\tilde{C}[\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{z}]
. For
i\in[n]
, define
A_{i}
to be the partial assignment that sets all bits in
\mathbf{b}
corresponding to appearances of the variable
x_{i}
in the CNF formula the same way that they are set in
\mathbf{z}
. Note that
C=\tilde{C}[A_{1},\dots,A_{n}]
. For convenience, let
\varphi_{i}:=p(\tilde{C}[A_{i}])
.

Let
\pi
be the proof that
p(\tilde{C})=(7/8)^{k}
(
\pi
might look essentially like the proof of Claim
9.2.1
, though in a formal language).
14
14

14

More precisely, we should have two arguments:
\pi^{\leq}
, which proves that
p(\tilde{C})\leq(7/8)^{k}
, and
\pi^{\geq}
, which proves that
-p(\tilde{C})\leq(7/8)^{k}
, and similarly for
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n}
.
Let
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n}
be proofs of the values of
\varphi_{i}
(these proofs are straightforward, as discussed in Section
9.2.1
). Let
\Pi=\{\pi,\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n}\}
.

What is
G_{\text{lin}}(C\mid\Pi)
? In Step
1
, the algorithm adds constraints that enforce the values of
\hat{p}(\tilde{C}),\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{1}]),\dots,\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{n}])
. Assuming that every variable appears at least once in the CNF formula, no further constraints are added in Step
2
, because the matrix
M
defined in that step has full rank. Step
3
is trivial, because the constraints in Step
1
define a polytope consisting of a single point – at this point we have found
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
, and we have
\hat{p}(\tilde{C})=p(\tilde{C})
and
\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{i}])=\varphi_{i}
for
i\in[n]
.

What happens in Step
4
? Call
\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}
the
\beta
-values corresponding to
\tilde{C},\tilde{C}[A_{1}],\dots,\tilde{C}[A_{n}]
, respectively. Because
C=\tilde{C}[A_{1},\dots,A_{n}]
, the algorithm will return
\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}+\dots+\beta_{n}
. Now, let’s compute
\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Note that
\mu(A_{i}\cup A_{j})=\mu(A_{i})\mu(A_{j})
for
i\neq j
. This fact lets us simplify the matrix equation, giving us the following
n+1
linear equations:

\displaystyle\beta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu(A_{j})\beta_{j}

\displaystyle=p(\tilde{C})

\displaystyle\beta_{0}+\beta_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}\mu(A_{j})\beta_{j}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i}\enskip\forall i\in[n].

Subtracting the first equation from the last
n
equations, we find that any solution must have
(1-\mu(A_{i}))\beta_{i}=\varphi_{i}-p(\tilde{C})
for all
i
, and so
\beta_{i}=\frac{\varphi_{i}-p(\tilde{C})}{1-\mu(A_{i})}
. This means that

\beta_{0}=p(\tilde{C})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu(A_{j})\beta_{j}=p(\tilde{C})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu(A_{j})\cdot\frac{\varphi_{j}-p(\tilde{C})}{1-\mu(A_{j})}.

Therefore, the algorithm returns

\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}+\dots+\beta_{n}=p(\tilde{C})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(1-\mu(A_{j}))\cdot\frac{\varphi_{j}-p(\tilde{C})}{1-\mu(A_{j})}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varphi_{j}-(n-1)p(\tilde{C}).

This is exactly the same as Equation
9.2
, our linear regression estimate from Section
9.2.1
!

We now prove that Algorithm
4
is efficient, and that it satisfies linearity and respect for proofs.

Theorem 9.3.5
.

The deductive estimation algorithm
G_{\text{lin}}
, as defined in Algorithm
4
, runs in polynomial time in the length of its input, and satisfies linearity and respect for proofs.

Proof.

We begin by proving that Algorithm
4
runs in polynomial time. Step
1
is straightforwardly fast, because checking a proof for validity takes linear time. Step
2
is also fast: first, computing
M
is straightforward. To compute
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
, we first (greedily) find a subset of
M
’s column space that spans the column space (which will have size
m-k
) and use the Gram-Schmidt process to find
m-k
orthogonal vectors that have the same span. Then we continue the Gram-Schmidt process in order to find
k
vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
that are orthogonal to these
m-k
orthogonal vectors. These are our
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
.

Step
3
is an application of the ellipsoid method, which – given rational linear constraints that define a nonempty region – can find a point in the region in polynomial time
(
Grötschel et al. 1993a
)
. The only detail that we must check is that the intersection of the constraints in
\ell
is nonempty. We show this by showing that the vector
\mathbf{p}
of true acceptance probabilities satisfies all constraints.

Clearly,
\mathbf{p}
satisfies the constraints that are added to
\ell
in Step
1
(after all,
\Pi
contains proofs that
\mathbf{p}
satisfies the constraints). What about the constraints added in Step
2
? For
C^{\prime}
,
(A_{1},\dots,A_{m})
, and
M
as defined in Step
2
, we must check that if
\mathbf{v}
is orthogonal to the column space of
M
, then
\left\langle\mathbf{v},(\mu(A_{1})p(C^{\prime}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})p(C^{\prime}[A_{m}]))\right\rangle=0
. In order to do so, it suffices to show that
(\mu(A_{1})p(C^{\prime}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})p(C^{\prime}[A_{m}]))
lies in the column space of
M
.

Claim 9.3.6
.

For
C^{\prime}
,
(A_{1},\dots,A_{m})
, and
M
as in Step
2
,
(\mu(A_{1})p(C^{\prime}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})p(C^{\prime}[A_{m}]))
lies in the column space of
M
.

Proof.

Let
n
be the number of inputs to
C^{\prime}
. Associate to each coordinate of
\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}}
a unique element of
\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
\Phi
be the
2^{n}\times m
matrix whose
i
-th column is the indicator vector for the partial assignment
A_{i}
– that is,
\Phi_{i}
contains a
1
in the coordinate corresponding to
\mathbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
if
\mathbf{x}
is consistent with
A_{i}
. Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2^{n}}
be the indicator vector for
C^{\prime}
– that is,
\mathbf{y}
contains a
1
in the coordinate corresponding to
\mathbf{x}
if
C^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
be such that
\Phi\boldsymbol{\beta}=\text{proj}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{y})
– that is,
\Phi\boldsymbol{\beta}
is the projection of
\mathbf{y}
onto the column space of
\Phi
. We claim that
M\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\mu(A_{1})p(C^{\prime}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})p(C^{\prime}[A_{m}]))
. To see this, we first observe that
M=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Phi^{T}\Phi
. This is because
\Phi^{T}\Phi
is an
m\times m
matrix whose
(i,j)
-entry is the number of assignments satisfying both
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
, which is
2^{n}\mu(A_{i}\cup A_{j})
. Therefore, we have

M\boldsymbol{\beta}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Phi^{T}\Phi\boldsymbol{\beta}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Phi^{T}\text{proj}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Phi^{T}\mathbf{y},

where the last step follows from the fact that
\Phi^{T}(\mathbf{y}-\text{proj}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{y}))=\mathbf{0}
, as
\mathbf{y}-\text{proj}_{\Phi}(\mathbf{y})
is orthogonal to the column space of
\Phi
. Finally, observe that
\Phi^{T}\mathbf{y}
is a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
whose
i
-th coordinate counts the number of inputs
\mathbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
that satisfy
C

and
are consistent with partial assignment
A_{i}
. This means that
\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Phi^{T}\mathbf{y}
is the
fraction
of inputs with this property, so the
i
-th coordinate of
\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Phi^{T}\mathbf{y}
is equal to
\mu(A_{i})p(C^{\prime}[A_{i}])
. This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we consider Step
4
. The equation
M\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\mu(A_{1})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{m}]))
has a solution. After all, we enforced this in Step
2
! Assuming that
\tilde{C}
is the base circuit of some constraint in
\ell
,
15
15

15

And if not, then the empty vector is a solution to the linear system (which has zero equations and zero variables).
when we considered
C^{\prime}=\tilde{C}
in Step
2
, we added linear constraints that enforced that
(\mu(A_{1})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{m}]))
is orthogonal to every vector in the orthogonal complement of the column space of
M
. This means that
(\mu(A_{1})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{1}]),\dots,\mu(A_{m})\hat{p}(\tilde{C}[A_{m}]))
lies in
M
’s column space.

Now, since a solution exists, the space of solutions is an affine subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The lowest-norm solution is just the projection of
\mathbf{0}
onto the space, which can be found in polynomial time. Computing the output is also straightforward. This completes the proof that Algorithm
4
runs in polynomial time.

We now prove that
G_{\text{lin}}
satisfies linearity and respect for proofs.

Claim 9.3.7
.

G_{\text{lin}}
satisfies respect for proofs.

Proof.

We begin by noting the following fact: if
C
appears in a constraint in
\ell
, then
G_{\text{lin}}(C\mid\Pi)=\hat{p}(C)
. To see this, let
\tilde{C},A_{1},\dots,A_{m},M,\boldsymbol{\beta}
be as in Step
4
of the algorithm, and without loss of generality assume that
C=\tilde{C}[A_{1}]
. The matrix equation defining
\boldsymbol{\beta}
gives us
m
linear constraints, the first of which says that

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(A_{1}\cup A_{j})\beta_{j}=\mu(A_{1})\hat{p}(C),

and so indeed we have

G_{\text{lin}}(C\mid\Pi)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\mu(A_{1}\cup A_{j})}{\mu(A_{1})}\beta_{j}=\hat{p}(C).

Now, to prove that
G_{\text{lin}}
satisfies respect for proofs, let us consider boolean circuits
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
and a proof that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}p(C_{i})\leq b
. The polynomial-time algorithm in Definition
9.3.3
that converts the proof into an argument
\pi
is the trivial algorithm that just outputs the proof that it receives as input. Now, consider any
\Pi
containing
\pi
. Let us consider the behavior of Algorithm
4
if given
\Pi
as input.

Note that the vector
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
computed in Step
3
only depends on
\Pi
, and not at all on
C
: after all the algorithm first looks at
C
in Step
4
. Note also that
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
all appear in some constraint in
\ell
(because the algorithm receives
\pi
as part of
\Pi
), and so the quantities
\hat{p}(C_{1}),\dots,\hat{p}(C_{k})
are well-defined and do not depend on the input
C
given to Algorithm
4
.

Because the constraint
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}p(C_{i})\leq b
appeared in
\ell
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\hat{p}(C_{i})\leq b
. Therefore, we have that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}G_{\text{lin}}(C_{i}\mid\Pi)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\hat{p}(C_{i})\leq b
, as desired.
∎

Claim 9.3.8
.

G_{\text{lin}}
satisfies linearity.

Proof.

Let
C
,
\Pi
, and
i
be as in Definition
9.3.2
. We are interested in the algorithm’s outputs when run on
(C,\Pi)
,
(C[x_{i}=0],\Pi)
, and
C[x_{i}=1],\Pi)
. Let
\tilde{C}
be the base circuit of
C
, which is also the base circuit of
C[x_{i}=0]
and
C[x_{i}=1]
. Observe that
\boldsymbol{\beta}
as defined in Step
4
is the same in all three cases. This is because the steps before Step
4
only depend on
\Pi
and not on the input circuit, and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
depends only
\Pi
and the base circuit defined in Step
4
, which is
\tilde{C}
in all three cases.

Let
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}
be as in Step
4
, which again are the same in all three cases. Let
A
be such that
C=\tilde{C}[A]
; then
C[x_{i}=0]=\tilde{C}[A\cup(x_{i}=0)]
and
C[x_{i}=1]=\tilde{C}[A\cup(x_{i}=1)]
. We want to show that

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\mu(A\cup A_{j})}{\mu(A)}\beta_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=0)\cup A_{j})}{\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=0))}\beta_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=1)\cup A_{j})}{\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=1))}\beta_{j}\right).

(9.3)

We have that
\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=0))=\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=1))=\frac{1}{2}\mu(A)
. We also have that
\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=0)\cup A_{j})+\mu(A\cup(x_{i}=1)\cup A_{j})=\mu(A\cup A_{j})
. Equation
9.3
follows straightforwardly from these two facts.
∎

We have now proven that Algorithm
4
runs in polynomial time and that it satisfies linearity and respect for proofs, and so we have completed the proof of Theorem
9.3.5
.
∎

Observe that inputs to a circuit
C
are in correspondence with full assignments to the variables in
C
. That is, we have
C(\mathbf{x})=p(C[\mathbf{x}])
, where on the right-hand side we think of
\mathbf{x}
as a partial assignment that happens to be a
full
assignment. This lets us define
G_{\text{lin}}
’s estimate of
C(\mathbf{x})

on a particular input
\mathbf{x}
: namely, it is the quantity
G_{\text{lin}}(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi)
. So, what is this quantity? For
A_{1},\dots,A_{m},\boldsymbol{\beta}
as in Step
4
of Algorithm
4
, we have

G_{\text{lin}}(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\mu(\mathbf{x}\cup A_{j})}{\mu(\mathbf{x})}\beta_{j}=\sum_{j\text{: }\mathbf{x}\text{ is consistent with }A_{j}}\beta_{j}.

This gives us a different perspective on
G_{\text{lin}}
: instead of thinking of
G_{\text{lin}}(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi)
as a special case in which we substitute values for all variables, we can instead think of
G_{\text{lin}}
as
constructing a linear model
for
\tilde{C}
’s behavior on individual inputs: namely, the sum of all
\beta_{j}
such that the input is consistent with
A_{j}
. Then, for a given circuit
C=\tilde{C}[A]
,
G_{\text{lin}}(C\mid\Pi)
is simply the average value of
G_{\text{lin}}(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi)
over all
\mathbf{x}
that are consistent with
A
.

Remark 9.3.9
.

As discussed earlier, it makes sense to extend the definition of respect for proofs to allow for
convex
constraints. Can we can extend Algorithm
4
to deal with convex constraints? The answer is essentially yes, but with a few caveats:

•

In order to run the ellipsoid method, we must have access to a separation oracle for the convex constraints. It is easy for
G
to implement a separation oracle under the following assumptions: first, the convex constraints are expressed as
f(C(x_{1}),\dots,C(x_{k}))\leq b
for a convex function
f
. Second, there is a fast algorithm for evaluating
f
and the gradient of
f
at a point (or a subgradient, if
f
is not differentiable). Third,
G
is given a proof that
f
is convex, as well as algorithms for evaluating
f
and its gradient and proofs that the algorithms are fast and correct.

•

Even so, for general convex constraints, the ellipsoid method may not be able to find an
exact
solution to all constraints. However, the ellipsoid method can quickly find a point that that satisfies all constraints to within
\varepsilon
tolerance. Thus, we can satisfy “near”-respect-for-proofs, but not necessarily exact respect for proofs.

9.4
Desiderata beyond linearity and respect for proofs

Although
G_{\text{lin}}
satisfies linearity and respect for proofs, its estimates are in some sense arbitrary. The ellipsoid method finds
some

\hat{\mathbf{p}}
satisfying the constraints in
\Pi
, but this
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
is by no means canonical.

As a more canonical choice, we could instead find the centroid of the feasible region.
16
16

16

In order to make the centroid well-defined, we can bound the feasible region by adding the constraints
0\leq p(C^{\prime})\leq 1
for every
C^{\prime}
appearing in some constraint in
\ell
.
While we cannot efficiently compute the exact centroid, we can compute it approximately by sampling from the feasible region, which can be done efficiently (see e.g.
Chen et al. 2018c
).

However, choosing the centroid of the feasible region has some undesirable properties, even if it can be computed exactly. Let
G_{\text{cent}}
be the modified version of
G_{\text{lin}}
that defines
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
to be the centroid of the feasible region. Consider three circuits
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
, about which no information is supplied except that
0\leq p(C_{1}),p(C_{2}),p(C_{3})\leq 1
. Then
G_{\text{cent}}
calculates
\hat{p}(C_{1})=\hat{p}(C_{2})=\hat{p}(C_{3})=\frac{1}{2}
– so far so good.

Now, let
C=C_{1}\wedge C_{2}\wedge C_{3}
. Note that
0\leq p(C)\leq p(C_{1}),p(C_{2}),p(C_{3})
and also that
p(C)\geq p(C_{1})+p(C_{2})+p(C_{3})-2
. This second fact follows from the fact that

1-p(C)=p(\neg C_{1}\vee\neg C_{2}\vee\neg C_{3})\leq p(\neg C_{1})+p(\neg C_{2})+p(\neg C_{3})=3-p(C_{1})-p(C_{2})-p(C_{3}).

These are the only linear constraints on
p(C)
in terms of
p(C_{1})
,
p(C_{2})
, and
p(C_{3})
that always hold, no matter what
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
are. Now, if
G
is supplied this additional information, it turns out
\hat{p}(C_{1})=\frac{14}{25}
(as can be shown by taking an integral). In other words, giving
G_{\text{cent}}
irrelevant information – information about some auxiliary circuit that was guaranteed to be true no matter what the circuits
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
are – changed its estimate of
p(C_{1})
.

This source of potential dissatisfaction with
G_{\text{lin}}
and
G_{\text{cent}}
raises the question: what other properties should a deductive estimation algorithm
G
have, beyond linearity and respect for proofs?
Christiano et al. 2022a
informally discuss an “independence of irrelevant arguments” property as a potential desideratum: that
G
’s estimates ought not to depend “irrelevant” information. However, formally defining what it means for information to be irrelevant appears to be difficult. In this section we will consider other desiderata for
G
.

9.4.1
0-1 boundedness

In this section, we propose a simple property that we call 0-1 boundedness.

Definition 9.4.1
.

A deductive estimation algorithm
G
satisfies
0-1 boundedness
if for every boolean circuit
C
and set of arguments
\Pi
, we have
0\leq G(C\mid\Pi)\leq 1
.

As we discussed in Section
9.3
, the respect for proofs property does not force
G
to respect
universal
proofs. For example, a proof that
0\leq p(C)\leq 1
for all boolean circuits
C
cannot be turned into an argument
\pi
such that
0\leq G(C\mid\pi)\leq 1
for all
C
. The 0-1 boundedness property is weaker than “respect for universal proofs” – and yet, it cannot be satisfied together with linearity and respect for proofs.

Theorem 9.4.2
.

Unless
P=PP
, there is no polynomial-time deductive estimation algorithm that satisfies linearity, respect for proofs, and 0-1 boundedness.

Proof.

Let
G
be a deductive estimation algorithm that satisfies linearity, respect for proofs, and 0-1 boundedness. We will show that a polynomial-time algorithm with oracle access to
G
can solve
\#3CNF
in polynomial time. This means that either
P=PP
or
G
is not a polynomial-time algorithm.

Recall the circuit
\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
from Example
9.3.4
:

\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})=(x_{i_{1,1}}=b_{1,1}\vee x_{i_{1,2}}=b_{1,2}\vee x_{i_{1,3}}=b_{1,3})\wedge\dots\wedge(x_{i_{k,1}}=b_{k,1}\vee x_{i_{k,2}}=b_{k,2}\vee x_{i_{k,3}}=b_{k,3}),

for some fixed indices
i_{1,1,},\dots,i_{k,3}
that are implicit in the notation.

Consider the proof that
p(\tilde{C})=(7/8)^{k}
. Because
G
satisfies respect for proofs, this proof can be turned into an argument
\pi_{0}
such that
G(\tilde{C}\mid\Pi)=(7/8)^{k}
for all
\Pi
containing
\pi_{0}
.

We now consider
8k
additional arguments: eight arguments for every clause. Let
j\in[k]
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})\in\{0,1\}^{3}
, and define
A_{j,\mathbf{y}}
to be the partial assignment

A_{j,\mathbf{y}}=(b_{j,1}=y_{1},b_{j,2}=y_{2},b_{j,3}=y_{3},x_{i_{j,1}}=\neg y_{1},x_{i_{j,2}}=\neg y_{2},x_{i_{j,3}}=\neg y_{3}).

Then
p(\tilde{C}[A_{j,\mathbf{y}}])=0
. This is because
A
makes clause
j
false, and thus – no matter how the rest of the variables are set – the entire formula is false. The proof of this fact can be turned into an argument
\pi_{j,\mathbf{y}}
such that
G(\tilde{C}[A_{j,\mathbf{y}}]\mid\Pi)=0
for all
\Pi
containing
\pi_{j,\mathbf{y}}
.

Let
\tilde{\Pi}
consist of
\pi_{0}
together with
\pi_{j,\mathbf{y}}
for all
k
choices of
j
and eight choices of
\mathbf{y}
.

Claim 9.4.3
.

For all assignments
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
to
\tilde{C}
, we have

G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})=\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}).

Proof.

We first show that
G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})\leq\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
. First, if
\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})=1
, then
G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})\leq 1=\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
by 0-1 boundedness.

Now, let
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
be such that
\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})=0
, and suppose for contradiction that
G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})>0
.
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
violates some clause; call it
j
. Let
\mathbf{y}=(b_{j,1},b_{j,2},b_{j,3})
. By respect for proofs, we have that
G(\tilde{C}[A_{j,\mathbf{y}}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})=0
. By (repeated applications of) linearity, this means that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\text{ consistent with }A_{j,\mathbf{y}}}\left[G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})\right]=G(\tilde{C}[A_{j,\mathbf{y}}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})=0.

But
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
is consistent with
A_{j,\mathbf{y}}
and
G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})>0
by assumption. It follows that for
some

(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime})
,
G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})<0
. But this contradicts 0-1 boundedness.

We now know that
G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})\leq\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
. Now, suppose for contradiction that
G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})<\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
for some
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})
. By (repeated applications of) linearity, it follows that

G(\tilde{C}\mid\tilde{\Pi})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}}\left[G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})\right]<\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}}\left[\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})\right]=p(\tilde{C})=(7/8)^{k}.

But this contradicts respect for proofs, because
\pi_{0}\in\tilde{\Pi}
.
∎

By linearity, it follows from Claim
9.4.3
that for every
\mathbf{b}
, we have

G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\left[\tilde{C}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b})\right]=p(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{b}]).

We can now give an algorithm that, with oracle access to
G
, solves
\#3CNF
in polynomial time. Consider any 3CNF
C
on
n
variables. We have that
C=\tilde{C}[\mathbf{b}]
for a particular
\tilde{C}
(i.e. for particular choices of indices
i_{1,1},\dots,i_{k,3}
) and
\mathbf{b}
. Create
\tilde{\Pi}
as above. Return
2^{n}G(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{b}]\mid\tilde{\Pi})
. The resulting output will be
2^{n}p(\tilde{C}[\mathbf{b}])
, which is equal to the number of satisfying assignments to
C
.
∎

9.4.2
Desiderata inspired by conditional expectation

We have now shown that we cannot hope for
G
to satisfy linearity and respect for proofs while requiring
G
to output estimates in
[0,1]
. What other properties might we hope for
G
to satisfy? In this section, we informally discuss two such properties that are inspired by properties of conditional expectations.

We call the first of these properties
iterated estimation,
because it is inspired by the law of iterated expectations from probability theory. Roughly speaking, the law of iterated expectations states that for a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, a random variable
X
defined on the space, and
\sigma
-algebras
17
17

17

For readers unfamiliar with
\sigma
-algebras, we suggest thinking of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
as partial information about
X
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
as more fine-grained partial information about
X
.

\mathcal{H}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid\mathcal{H}_{2}\right]\mid\mathcal{H}_{1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid\mathcal{H}_{1}\right].

Why is this true? We can think of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
as revealing partial information about the state of the world
\omega\in\Omega
, and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
as revealing more fine-grained information than
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. The right-hand side is a random variable that is the expectation of
X
conditioned on knowing the information in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. The left-hand side is the expected value of what you
will
think the expectation of
X
is after learning the information in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, if you only know the information in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. These are the same because, if you knew that in expectation you would update your guess about
X
in a particular direction once you learned
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, it would make sense to update your guess about
X
before learning
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

Definition 9.4.4

(Informal)
.

A deductive estimation algorithm
G
satisfies
iterated estimation
if for all boolean circuits
C
and for all sets of arguments
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
, we have

G(G(C\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})\mid\Pi)=G(C\mid\Pi).

In other words: if
G
only knows the arguments in
\Pi
, then its estimate for what its estimate of
p(C)

would
be if it also considered
\Pi^{\prime}
, is just its current estimate of
p(C)
.

The statement of iterated estimation does not
quite
make sense, because we said that the first argument to
G
is a boolean circuit – whereas
G(C\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})
is not quite a boolean circuit.

However, we can make sense of this property by expanding the scope of the first argument to
G
. Suppose we allow
G
to accept not just boolean circuits, but arbitrary computer programs (Turing machines) that output a real number. Then
G(C\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})
is one such computer program – namely, it takes no inputs, runs
G
on the input
(C,(\Pi,\Pi^{\prime}))
, and outputs the output of
G
.
18
18

18

The fact that this program takes no inputs is nothing new: we have already considered expressions such as
G(C[\mathbf{x}]\mid\Pi)
, where
\mathbf{x}
is a full assignment to
C
, and so
C[\mathbf{x}]
is a circuit with no inputs that outputs
C(\mathbf{x})
.
While in this chapter we have dealt with boolean circuits for simplicity, we are interested in the deductive estimation of the outputs of computer programs in general, and do not see a fundamental obstacle to enriching the space of inputs to
G
to allow for arbitrary programs.

Returning to the iterated estimation property, it makes sense to ask: does the parallel to the law of iterated estimation make sense, or should we want
G
to behave differently from an iterated expectation? One reason for skepticism is that it requires the outer
G
in the expression
G(G(C\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})\mid\Pi)
to be “blinded” to
\Pi^{\prime}
when estimating
G(C\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})
. That is, the outer
G
sees the expression
G(C\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})
, but does not “process” it, beyond the extent allowed by
\Pi
.

But in a sense, this is the behavior we desire from
G
. We do not expect
G
to be able to deduce properties of the circuit (or program) that it is estimating, except for the properties that are given to
G
as arguments. In that sense, we should not expect the outer
G
to be able to draw conclusions from
\Pi^{\prime}
, except to the extent that it can do so based on the arguments that it has been given (i.e.
\Pi
).

The second property is inspired by a different property of conditional expectations, called “pulling out known factors.” The pulling out known factors property of conditional expectations states roughly that for an
\mathcal{H}
-measurable random variable
X
and a random variable
Y
, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[XY\mid\mathcal{H}\right]=X\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid\mathcal{H}\right]
. In other words, if the information supplied by
\mathcal{H}
is sufficient to determine the value of
X
, then
X
can be pulled out of the conditional expectation.

Definition 9.4.5

(Informal)
.

A deductive estimation algorithm
G
satisfies the
pulling out known factors
property if for boolean circuits
C_{1},C_{2}
and sets of arguments
\Pi,\Pi^{\prime}
, we have

G(G(C_{1}\mid\Pi)p(C_{2})\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})=G(C_{1}\mid\Pi)G(C_{2}\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime}).

Again, we require
G
to accept arbitrary programs as input, and interpret
G(C_{1}\mid\Pi)p(C_{2})
as the program that computes
G(C_{1}\mid\Pi)
, computes
p(C_{2})
, and returns their product.
19
19

19

This program may not run in polynomial time, but that is fine:
G
can estimate the outputs of programs that may take a long time to run.
The intuition is the same as for the corresponding property of conditional expectations: in the expression
G(G(C_{1}\mid\Pi)p(C_{2})\mid\Pi,\Pi^{\prime})
, the outer
G
knows
\Pi
, so it knows what its estimate of
p(C_{1})
would be if it only knew
\Pi
. To the outer
G
, the value
G(C_{1}\mid\Pi)
is a particular constant, rather than an unknown, so it can be “pulled out.”

9.5
Mechanistic anomaly detection for neural networks

Let
G_{\text{good}}
be a hypothetical deductive estimation algorithm that we would find satisfying: perhaps it satisfies linearity, respect for proofs, and some other important desiderata. One potential use case for
G_{\text{good}}
is to tell apart different reasons for why a circuit might output
1
. Recall Example
9.1.1
from the introduction:
C
takes as input a triple
(a,b,c)
of positive integers and accepts if
\max(a,b)=\max(b,c)
. Note that
C
can accept an input for one of two “reasons”: either
b\geq a,c
(which happens with probability
\frac{1}{3}
or so), or
a=c
(which happens extremely rarely).

Let
\pi
be the proof that if
b\geq a,c
then
C(a,b,c)=1
. We might expect the following behavior from
G_{\text{good}}
: for all
(a,b,c)
such that
b\geq a,c
, we have
G_{\text{good}}(C[a,b,c]\mid\pi)=1
. Meanwhile, if
b<a
or
b<c
, then
G_{\text{good}}(C[a,b,c]\mid\pi)
is close to zero – even if in fact
a=c
– because
\pi
does not point out the fact that if
a=c
, then
C(a,b,c)=1
. Thus,
G_{\text{good}}
allows us to distinguish inputs on which
C
outputs
1
for the “usual” reason (
b\geq a,c
) from inputs on which
C
outputs
1
for a different reason (
a=c
).

Of course, this approach is only useful insofar as it can be applied to circuits that we don’t already understand. For such circuits, there is an obvious barrier to using
G_{\text{good}}
in this way: how do we find the deductive arguments to give to
G_{\text{good}}
? In general, finding deductive arguments is as hard as finding proofs of mathematical statements. However,
in the particular case of machine learning,
we may be able to find deductive arguments that allow us to understand the neural networks that we have trained.

The key fact about neural networks is that they are trained from random initialization via gradient descent. We can hope to learn deductive arguments for the behavior of a neural network
also
via gradient descent, in parallel with training the network. These arguments might be features in some continuous parameterized space, somewhat akin to the features
\varphi_{i}
in Section
9.2
. One possibility is that these features will be simple functions of the neural network’s weights and other features. The features would be learned to maximize predictive accuracy of the neural network’s behavior: for example, if the neural network (call it
N
) is trained to get a high reward according to a reward model (call it
R
), and it learns to get high reward on the input distribution
D
, then we would hope to learn features
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}
such that
G_{\text{good}}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[R(\mathbf{x},N(\mathbf{x}))\right]\mid\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m})
is (correctly) high.
20
20

20

How does respect for proofs figure into this? The respect for proofs property is meant to capture that the space of possible features must be extremely expressive: enough that features can encode formal proofs about
N
and
R
. In practice, the features that we learn are unlikely to be proofs; however, respect for proofs is an easily formalizable property that is necessary (even if not sufficient) for the features to be arbitrarily expressive. It is thus a convenient property to work with when taking a theoretical approach to finding a satisfying deductive estimation algorithm.

We may then be able to use these features for
mechanistic anomaly detection

(
Christiano 2022a
)
. The mechanistic anomaly detection problem can be stated informally as follows: suppose that a neural network
N
gets high average reward from a reward model
R
over an input distribution
D
(that is,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[R(\mathbf{x},N(\mathbf{x}))\right]
is large). Let
\mathbf{x}^{*}\not\in D
be a new input on which
N
again gets high reward (that is,
R(\mathbf{x}^{*},N(\mathbf{x}^{*}))
is also large). Did
N
get high reward on
\mathbf{x}^{*}

for the same reasons
that it got high average reward on
D
, or
for a different reason?

For example, suppose that
N
is an financial assistant that takes actions such as buying and selling stocks, transferring money between bank accounts, and paying taxes. Suppose that
R
sanity-checks
N
’s actions for legal compliance, and then rewards
N
if it has turned a profit. Then
N
may have two ways of getting high reward:
successful task completion
(making good trades on the stock market) and
successful deception
(for example, making a profit via money laundering and then covering its tracks). Suppose that
D
is a “clean” distribution of input scenarios: on each scenario in
D
,
N
achieves high reward via the “successful task completion” route. If
\mathbf{x}^{*}
is a new input scenario on which
N
again achieves high reward, we want to be able to determine whether
N
successfully completed the task (got high reward
for the same reasons
as it did on
D
) or if it instead successfully deceived
R
(got high reward
for a different reason
). In the latter case, we would like to classify
N
’s behavior on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
as “anomalous” (and perhaps refer the input for more extensive oversight).

Mechanistic anomaly detection is a particularly challenging problem, because it cannot in general be solved by looking at
N
’s input-output behavior. After all, a sufficiently advanced reward model
R
would be able to integrate observations of input-output behavior into its oversight process. To catch successful deception, it could be necessary to analyze to analyze
N
’s internals. This is why sophisticated machinery such as deductive estimation may be necessary.

How can deductive estimation be used to solve the mechanistic anomaly detection problem? Suppose that we successfully learn features
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}
that explain
N
’s high average reward to
G_{\text{good}}
– that is,
G_{\text{good}}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim D}\left[R(\mathbf{x},N(\mathbf{x}))\right]\mid\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m})
is appropriately large. Then consider the quantity

G_{\text{good}}(R(\mathbf{x}^{*},N(\mathbf{x}^{*}))\mid\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}).

If
N
gets high reward on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
for the same reasons than it got high reward on inputs in
D
, then
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}
should be sufficient to explain
N
’s high reward on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
as well, and so we expect
G_{\text{good}}(R(\mathbf{x}^{*},N(\mathbf{x}^{*}))\mid\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m})
to be large as well. On the other hand, suppose that
N
gets high reward on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
for a
different
reason than it got high reward on
D
. Then
G_{\text{good}}(R(\mathbf{x}^{*},N(\mathbf{x}^{*}))\mid\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m})
will probably be small, because the reasons why
N
did well on
D
(which are represented as features
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}
) are
insufficient
to explain
N
’s high reward on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
. So if
R(\mathbf{x}^{*},N(\mathbf{x}^{*}))
is large, but
