G_{\text{good}}(R(\mathbf{x}^{*},N(\mathbf{x}^{*}))\mid\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m})
is small, then we classify
N
’s behavior on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
as anomalous.
21
21

21

In fact, given the input
\mathbf{x}^{*}
, we may further optimize our features
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}
, with the goal of finding features that still cause
G
to estimate that
N
’s average reward on
D
is large, while causing
G
to estimate that
N
’s reward on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
is small. If we find any such features, we label
N
’s behavior on
\mathbf{x}^{*}
anomalous. Of course, care must be taken to not over-optimize, as this runs the danger of labeling every input “anomalous.”

9.6
Conclusion

In my view, the problem of deductive circuit estimation – finding an efficient deductive estimation algorithm that satisfies important properties such as linearity, respect for proofs, and more – has two compelling motivations.

The first is theoretical elegance and importance. In the introduction, we gave several examples of deductive arguments about a circuit. Those arguments were not proofs, but in an important sense, they were sound. Barring further arguments, it is reasonable to guess that the first 128 bits of the output of SHA-256 are as likely to be larger than the last 128 bits as they are to be smaller. Barring further observations about the structure of a 3CNF with
k
clauses, it is reasonable to guess that its acceptance probability is
(7/8)^{k}
. Such deductive reasoning is commonplace in computer science and mathematics.

Christiano et al. 2022a
asked whether it is possible to formalize such reasoning: that is, to design an algorithm that estimates quantities given formal arguments similar to the ones in the introduction. This question seems fundamental: much as mathematicians have found a formalization of a mathematical proof, one could hope for a formalization of a much broader class of deductive arguments.

In this chapter, we continued this formalization project by designing an algorithm for boolean circuit estimation that satisfies linearity and respect for proofs, and by suggesting further properties that deductive estimation algorithms ought to satisfy. However, we have not satisfied those further properties, nor am I convinced that we have found the right set of properties to satisfy. We have raised more questions than we have answered. But in my opinion, the questions that we have raised are fundamental and exciting.

The second motivation for this work is practical relevance to the AI alignment problem.
22
22

22

See
Ji et al. 2023a
for a survey of the AI alignment problem.
AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic are putting substantial effort into
scalable oversight:
methods of overseeing training that will continue to be reliable even as AI capabilities advance
(
Leike & Sutskever 2023a
;
Anthropic 2023a
)
. However, a sufficiently advanced AI could find ways to exploit loopholes in its oversight process. While oversight approaches that rely on observations of input-output behavior may work, this is not guaranteed. Approaches that use model internals as part of the oversight process may be necessary to build safe advanced AI.

In Section
9.5
, we saw how deductive estimation algorithms could be used to oversee an AI by flagging anomalous behavior. There are many challenges ahead for this approach, and this path forward is more complex and speculative than most approaches to scalable oversight. However, this approach has the potential to succeed even under somewhat pessimistic assumptions about the difficulty of the AI alignment problem.
23
23

23

See
https://www.alignment.org/blog/
for further discussion.

For both of these reasons – the theoretical and the practical – I find deductive estimation to be a compelling research direction. I am excited to see what the future holds for this area.

Epilogue

Each technical chapter of this thesis explored a different facet of algorithmic Bayesian epistemology. Chapters
3
and
4
explored the elicitation of knowledge under strategic constraints. Chapter
5
bridged the problem of knowledge elicitation with the problem knowledge aggregation. Chapters
6
and
7
focused on the knowledge aggregation problem, now under informational constraints. Chapter
8
also explored aggregation,
1
1

1

After all, the question of Chapter
8
can be framed as: under what circumstances can Alice and Bob aggregate their knowledge by sharing a small amount of their information?
but now under communication constraints. Finally, Chapter
9
explored belief formation under both computational and informational constraints.

We closed every chapter with a discussion of future directions. Some chapters had a few suggestions, while others had many. Depending on how you count, Chapter
7
had as many as 1,152 suggestions! Some closed with fully formal problem statements (Chapter
4
), others with problem statements so informal that formalizing them would in itself be a breakthrough (Chapter
9
). Some suggestions were intellectual curiosities, while others had significant potential for application. Some were merely interesting, while others were (in my opinion) utterly fascinating.

In light of all these differences, I’d like to close by highlighting the directions in ABE that I find
most
exciting. These directions are listed in order of the chapter(s) to which they are relevant.

Bayesian justifications for generalized QA pooling

In Section
2.4
, we gave Bayesian justifications for generalized linear and logarithmic pooling (i.e. linear and logarithmic pooling but with weights that do not necessarily add to
1
). That is, we presented information structures in which a generalized linear pool is the
exactly correct
aggregation method, and similarly for generalized logarithmic pooling. Can we do this for
every
QA pooling method – that is, generalized QA pooling with respect to every proper scoring rule?

Directions in robust forecast aggregation

As discussed in Section
7.6
, robust forecast aggregation seems like a
particularly
exciting area for future work. Highlights include:

•

Generalization to KL divergence.
In our exploration of the known prior setting, we found that averaging experts’ forecasts and then moving the average away from the prior by a constant factor results in a robustly high-quality forecast. However, if the value
Y
being forecast is a probability, then this procedure can result in aggregate forecasts outside of
[0,1]
. If the error measure were changed from squared error to KL divergence, then such forecasts would naturally be disallowed, as they would incur an infinite penalty. So, what is a robust way to aggregate probabilistic forecasts if the error measure is KL divergence? The answer to this question may have important implications for the aggregation of probabilistic forecasts!

•

Giving the aggregator additional information.
In our problem, the aggregator learns each expert’s expectation of
Y
and (sometimes) the prior. How much better can the aggregator do if they learn additional information, either about the experts’ beliefs or about the information structure itself? Note that the answer might vary considerably depending on which assumptions are made about the information structure.

Sophisticated Bayesian models for forecast aggregation

While this thesis focused primarily on robust forecast aggregation, there is significant low-hanging fruit in Bayesian aggregation: creating a Bayesian model of experts’ information and finding the optimal aggregate (or a good aggregate) under that model. While much more well-studied than robust aggregation, most work on Bayesian aggregation focuses on specific application domains (see
McAndrew et al. 2021a
for a survey).

One particularly compelling direction is to build on the Gaussian partial information framework introduced by
Satopää et al. 2016a
(see our exposition in Section
2.3
). In this framework, the optimal aggregate is a linear combination of the experts’ forecasts, with weights determined by the overlap between the experts’ information sets (see Section
2.4
).
Satopää et al. 2017a
explore how this overlap may be inferred when multiple quantities are being forecast. But how might one aggregate forecasts within this framework if only one quantity is being forecast?

Here is one possible Bayesian model, inspired by this question. Let us say that the experts are forecasting a quantity
Y=X_{1}+X_{2}+\dots
, where each
X_{j}
is independently and normally distributed, with mean
0
and standard deviation
\frac{1}{j}
. (Essentially, this means that the factors contributing to
Y
have “importances” that follow a Zipfian distribution. Note that
Y
is almost surely finite.) There are
m
experts, each of which knows a subset of the
X_{j}
’s. In particular, we can model each expert
i
as having an
expertise score

e_{i}
(over which we have some prior), and each
X_{j}
as having an
obviousness score

o_{j}
(over which we have some prior). The odds that Expert
i
knows the value of
X_{j}
are
e_{i}\cdot o_{j}
to
1
(or in other words, the probability that expert
i
knows
X_{j}
is
\frac{e_{i}o_{j}}{e_{i}o_{j}+1}
). Each expert tells the aggregator their estimate of
Y
, which is the sum of all
X_{j}
’s whose value the expert knows. The aggregator can then do (approximate) Bayesian inference to find the optimal way to combine the experts’ estimates into a single estimate of
Y
.

Note that it is straightforward to adapt this model to the context of forecasting a binary event. Just as before, we have
Y=X_{1}+X_{2}+\dots
, with each expert knowing a subset of the
X_{j}
’s, but now the experts are estimating the probability that
Y\geq 0
, conditioned on their information. The aggregator’s task is now to combine these probabilities into an overall probability that
Y\geq 0
.

Finding a good deductive estimator

In Chapter
9
, we found a deductive estimation algorithm that satisfies linearity and respect for proofs. We then exhibited an informal property (independence of irrelevant information) that the algorithm does not satisfy, thus prompting the question of whether a “better” deductive estimation algorithm exists. This question decomposes into two sub-questions. First, what does it mean for a deductive estimation algorithm to be good? A formalization of “independence of irrelevant information” would help answer this question, as would formulating other desirable properties of an estimation algorithm (perhaps “iterated estimation" and “pulling out known factors," as discussed in Section
9.4.2
). Second, how do we create a deductive estimation algorithm that is good (as defined by the answer to the first question)? As discussed in Section
9.6
, these questions seem quite fundamental.

Wagering mechanisms that produce good aggregate forecasts

In Chapter
1
, we briefly discussed wagering mechanisms, which are a type of mechanism for eliciting probabilistic forecasts from multiple experts. Each expert submits a forecast and a
wager,
and the wagers get redistributed according to each expert’s performance.

Wagering mechanisms are similar to prediction markets, in that they elicit forecasts by giving experts opportunities to place bets. Prediction markets also aggregate experts’ forecasts into a single forecast, which can be inferred from market prices. It is natural to wonder whether wagering mechanisms can also be used for aggregation.

Lambert et al. 2015a
ask the natural question of what Bayesian experts’ wagers look like in equilibrium.
2
2

2

To circumvent the no-trade theorem,
Lambert et al. 2015a
posit that the experts get utility from gambling. Alternatively, we could circumvent the no-trade theorem by subsidizing the wagering mechanism.
For a given information structure, we could say that a wagering mechanism produces good aggregate forecasts if pooling the experts’ forecasts according to their equilibrium wagers outperforms pooling the forecasts with equal weights.
3
3

3

Intuitively, a wagering mechanism should produce good aggregate forecasts for an information structure if each expert’s equilibrium wager reflects their confidence. By
confidence,
we (informally) mean the extent to which the expert expects their forecast to remain unchanged upon learning other experts’ signals. If experts’ wagers reflect their confidence, then an expert who knows almost all of the available information will make a large wager, while an expert who knows that other experts have important private information will make a small wager. In that case, using wagers as weights for aggregation should produce a sensible result.
Perhaps it is possible to create a wagering mechanism that produces good aggregate forecasts for a large, natural class of information structures.

It seems to me that wagering mechanisms have the potential to be a powerful tool for eliciting and aggregating forecasts, alongside prediction markets. However, such wagering mechanisms would need to be designed so that optimal wagers are reflective of confidence.

In summary, algorithmic Bayesian epistemology presents a wide range of fascinating questions and possible directions for further research. I hope that this thesis has conveyed my enthusiasm for ABE, and that it has sparked a similar curiosity in you as well.

Appendix A
Details omitted from Chapter
3

A.1
Details omitted from Section
3.2

Fact A.1.1
.

If a coin’s bias is chosen uniformly from
[0,1]
, then for all
n
and
0\leq k\leq n
, the probability that exactly
k
of
n
flips come up heads is
\frac{1}{n+1}
.

Proof.

Consider the following method of choosing the
n
flip outcomes: first, choose a number
r
in
[0,1]
(the bias); then, choose
n
numbers in
[0,1]
, each of which corresponds to a heads flip if it is less than
r
. The probability of
k
heads is the probability that the first of the
n+1
numbers is the
(k+1)
-th smallest, which is
\frac{1}{n+1}
.
∎

See
3.2.4

Proof.

Fix a scoring rule
s
and let
r_{p}(x)
be the expected score of an expert with belief
p
who reports
x
(so
r_{p}(x)=ps(x)+(1-p)s(1-x)
).

We first prove that if
s
is weakly proper then it satisfies the two stated constraints. Suppose that
s
is weakly proper. It is clear that
s
satisfies the first equation: for all
p
, in order for
r_{p}(x)
to have a maximum at
x=p
, its derivative
ps^{\prime}(x)-(1-p)s^{\prime}(1-x)
must be
0
at
x=p
. So we first conclude that we must have
xs^{\prime}(x)=(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x)
for all
x\in(0,1)
. Next, observe that

r_{p}^{\prime}(x)=ps^{\prime}(x)-(1-p)s^{\prime}(1-x)=ps^{\prime}(x)-(1-p)\frac{xs^{\prime}(x)}{1-x}=s^{\prime}(x)\left(p-(1-p)\frac{x}{1-x}\right).

(A.1)

Suppose for contradiction that for some
p\in(0,1)
, we have
s^{\prime}(p)<0
. Since
s^{\prime}
is continuous,
s^{\prime}(x)<0
on some open interval containing
p
. On that open interval, then, the sign of
r_{p}^{\prime}(x)
is the opposite of the sign of
p-(1-p)\frac{x}{1-x}
– that is, negative when
x<p
and positive when
x>p
. But then
r_{p}(x)
is strictly
minimized
, rather than maximized, at
x=p
on this interval, contradicting that
s
is weakly proper.

To prove the stronger claim when
s
is proper, assume for contradiction that
s^{\prime}(p)
is not strictly positive almost everywhere. Then because
s^{\prime}
is continuous, there is an interval of non-zero length in which
s^{\prime}(x)=0
on the entire interval. Let
p
lie on the interior of this interval. Equation (
A.1
) then establishes that
r_{p}^{\prime}(x)
is
0
in an interval around
x=p
, meaning that
p
is not the unique maximizer of
r_{p}
, contradicting that
s
is proper.

Conversely, suppose that
s
satisfies the two stated constraints. We show that
s
is weakly proper by showing a stronger statement: that for all
p
,
r_{p}(x)
weakly increases on
(0,p]
and weakly decreases on
[p,1)
. By the first constraint, (
A.1
) holds. By the second constraint, for all
x
,
r_{p}^{\prime}(x)
is either
0
or has the sign of
p-(1-p)\frac{x}{1-x}
, i.e. positive if
x<p
and negative if
x>p
. This means that
r_{p}(x)
is weakly increasing on
(0,p]
and weakly decreasing on
[p,1)
, and so attains a weak global maximum at
x=p
, as desired.

To prove the stronger claim when
s^{\prime}(x)>0
almost everywhere, we show that
r_{p}(x)
strictly increases almost everywhere on
(0,p]
and strictly decreases almost everywhere on
[p,1)
. Again, (
A.1
) holds, so by the second constraint we have that
r_{p}^{\prime}(x)
has the sign of
p-(1-p)\frac{x}{1-x}
almost everywhere, i.e. positive if
x<p
and negative if
x>p
. Thus,
r_{p}(x)
is strictly increasing almost everywhere on
(0,p]
and strictly decreasing almost everywhere on
[p,1)
, and so attains a strict global maximum at
x=p
, as desired.
∎

See
3.2.5

Proof.

As
\frac{1-p}{p}
is strictly positive on
(\frac{1}{2},1)
, and
s^{\prime}(1-p)
is strictly (resp., weakly) positive almost everywhere on
(\frac{1}{2},1)
, we immediately conclude that
s^{\prime}(p)
is also strictly (resp., weakly) positive almost everywhere on
(\frac{1}{2},1)
. Therefore,
s
is proper (resp., weakly proper) by Lemma
3.2.4
.
∎

Proposition A.1.2
.

Let
s
be a proper scoring rule and define
\text{Score}_{c}(s)
to be the expected score of a globally-adaptive expert who is scored with
s
. Then
\lim_{c\rightarrow 0}\text{Score}_{c}(s)=\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx
. That is,
\text{Score}_{c}(s)=\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx\pm o(1)
.

Proof.

First, we wish to argue that as
c\rightarrow 0
,
no matter the true bias,
the number of flips any expert will choose to make approaches
\infty
. To see this, observe that after
n
flips, the expert’s current belief will always be an integer multiple of
\frac{1}{n+1}
. So if

c(m):=\min_{k<n\leq m,\text{ and }k,n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\{\frac{k+1}{n+2}G_{s}\left(\frac{k+2}{n+3}\right)+\frac{n-k+1}{n+2}G_{s}\left(\frac{k+1}{n+3}\right)-G_{s}\left(\frac{k+1}{n+2}\right)\right\}

then the expert will always flip the coin at least
m
times as long as
c\leq c(m)
. Observe also that the minimum is taken over finitely many terms, all of which are strictly positive, so
c(m)
is strictly positive. Therefore, for all
m
, there is a sufficiently small
c(m)>0
such that whenever the cost is at most
c(m)
, the expert flips at least
m
times no matter the true bias. Note that while these calculations are done for an adaptive expert, they hold for a non-adaptive expert as well because the non-adaptive expert would want to flip at least
m
coins
no matter the outcomes
.

Now, let’s consider the expected score of an expert who makes exactly
m
flips no matter what. For all
k
, such an expert sees
k
total heads with probability
\frac{1}{m+1}
(Fact
A.1.1
). And conditioned on seeing
k
heads, the expert’s expected score tomorrow is
G_{s}\left(\frac{k+1}{m+2}\right)
. Therefore, we can conclude that for all
m
, the expert’s expected score after
m
coin flips is
\frac{1}{m+1}\sum_{k=0}^{m}G_{s}\left(\frac{k+1}{m+2}\right)
.

Now, we want to understand the limit of this sum as
m\rightarrow\infty
. Observe that for each
m
, the sum is a Riemann sum for the function
G_{s}(x)
on
[0,1]
(i.e. each
\frac{k+1}{m+2}
lies inside the interval
\left[\frac{k}{m+1},\frac{k+1}{m+1}\right]
). Therefore, the limit as
m\rightarrow\infty
is just the Riemann integral, and we get:

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{m+1}\sum_{k=0}^{m}G_{s}\left(\frac{k+1}{m+2}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx.

So now we can conclude that the non-adaptive expert gets expected payoff
\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx+o(1)
. As
c\rightarrow 0
, the number of flips
m\rightarrow\infty
, and the expected payoff as
m\rightarrow\infty
approaches
\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx
. For an adaptive expert, observe that by Lemma
3.2.7
, as long as they flip the coin at least
m
times with probability
1
, their expected score is at least as large as if they flipped it exactly
m
times with probability
1
. As their expected score can certainly not exceed
\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx
(as this is the score of a perfect expert who knows exactly the bias), their expected score must also approach
\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx
as
m\rightarrow\infty
(and therefore as
c\rightarrow\infty
as well).
∎

See
3.2.9

Proof.

We have

\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx=\int_{0}^{1}(xs(x)+(1-x)s(1-x))dx=\int_{0}^{1}2xs(x)dx

where the last step follows by separating
(1-x)s(1-x)
into its own integral and substituting
u=1-x
. Now we integrate by parts, letting
u=s(x)
and
dv=2xdx
, so that
du=s^{\prime}(x)
and
v=x^{2}
, to get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx

\displaystyle=x^{2}s(x)\mid_{0}^{1}-\int_{0}^{1}x^{2}s^{\prime}(x)dx=s(1)-\int_{0}^{1}x^{2}s^{\prime}(x)dx

\displaystyle=s(1)-\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}x^{2}s^{\prime}(x)dx-\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}x^{2}s^{\prime}(x)dx

\displaystyle=s(1)-\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}x(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x)dx-\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}x^{2}s^{\prime}(x)dx

\displaystyle=s(1)-\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(x(1-x)+x^{2})s^{\prime}(x)dx=s(1)-\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}xs^{\prime}(x)dx.

Expressing
s(1)-s\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)
as
\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}s^{\prime}(x)dx
, we obtain the desired equality.
∎

A.2
Properties of respectful scoring rules

We state several sufficient conditions for a scoring rule to be respectful, confirm that typical scoring rules are respectful, and provide a brief discussion.

Claim A.2.1
.

If Conditions 1 and 2 of Definition
3.3.1
hold and
\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert
is bounded on
(0,1)
then
s
is respectful.

This should be clear: take
c
to be small enough such that
c^{-.16}
times the lower bound on
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
exceeds the upper bound on
\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\rvert
. Scoring rules such as the quadratic scoring rule and the spherical scoring rule satisfy the hypotheses of Claim
A.2.1
. One well-known generalization of the quadratic scoring rule is the Tsallis rule
(
Tsallis 1988a
)
. This rule, parametrized by
\gamma>1
, is defined to be the scoring rule
s
for which
G_{s}(x)=x^{\gamma}+(1-x)^{\gamma}
. When
\gamma=2
, this yields the quadratic scoring rule.

For
\gamma\geq 3
, it is evident that the Tsallis rule satisfies the hypotheses of Claim
A.2.1
. However, this is not so for
\gamma<3
(except for
\gamma=2
). Perhaps more importantly, the logarithmic scoring rule does not satisfy Claim
A.2.1
either. This motivates the following result (the proof appears at the end of this section).

Claim A.2.2
.

Suppose that Conditions 1 and 2 of Definition
3.3.1
are satisfied. Suppose further that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is bounded on any closed sub-interval of
(0,1)
, and that there exist constants
k\neq 0
and
r
such that
\lim_{x\to 0}x^{r}G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=k
. Then
s
is respectful.

The logarithmic scoring rule satisfies the hypotheses of Claim
A.2.2
(
r=2
and
k=-1
). The Tsallis rule with
\gamma<3
(and
\gamma\neq 2
) also satisfies these hypotheses (
r=3-\gamma
and
k=\gamma(\gamma-1)(\gamma-2)
). The proof of Claim
A.2.2
is more involved, and deferred to Appendix
A.3
.

We briefly discuss ways in which proper scoring rules can fail to be respectful. One way a scoring rule can be disrespectful is if
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
grows extremely quickly near zero (e.g.
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=e^{1/x}
). Such functions, however, are outside the scope of this entire exercise because they are not normalizable. That is, such
G_{s}
have
\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx=\infty
, and provide infinite expected payment to the expert. So this “limitation” of respectfulness is more of a restatement of normalizability.

Another way a scoring rule could be disrespectful is if
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
is not bounded away from zero. For example:
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}
or
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=x(1-x)
. If
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
remains “very flat” near
0
for a “large interval”, then
\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s)
is infinite anyway. This implies that we should expect the error to be a
larger
order of magnitude than
c^{-\ell/4}
, and for such functions to not incentivize precision well at all (although we do not explicitly prove this). This makes sense: if
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)\approx 0
, then the expert gains
\approx 0
by flipping the coin to refine their current belief (Lemma
3.2.7
). It is also possible that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
is not bounded away from zero, but also not “very flat”. We conjecture that Theorem
3.3.3
does hold for such functions, but that our approach does not establish this. While it is possible to come up with such functions (e.g., the two above) which elude Theorem
3.3.3
’s precise statement, this does not affect commonly-studied scoring rules, nor the scoring rules designed in this paper (sometimes leaning on Theorem
3.6.1
).

Finally, as with any exercise in analysis, there are continuous functions that behave erratically near zero, such as
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\sin\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)+\sin\left(\frac{1}{1-x}\right)+3
. While it may or may not be the case that Theorem
3.3.3
extends to such functions, this does not seem particularly relevant.

Proof of Claim
A.2.2
.

Let
r,k
be as in Claim
A.2.2
. If
r\leq 0
then the claim is uninteresting:
G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is bounded on
(0,1)
and so the statement is subsumed by Claim
A.2.1
. The interesting case is when
r>0
.

We first consider the case when
r>1
. Note that
\lim_{x\to 0}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\infty
. To see this, suppose for contradiction that this limit is finite. We may write

\lim_{x\to 0}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{xG_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}{x}=\lim_{x\to 0}xG_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)+G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)

by L’Hôpital’s rule, so
\lim_{x\to 0}xG_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=0
, contradicting that
\lim_{x\to 0}x^{r}G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\neq 0
.

Now, the fact that
\lim_{x\to 0}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\infty
lets us apply L’Hôpital’s rule:

\lim_{x\to 0}x^{r-1}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}{x^{1-r}}=\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)}{(1-r)x^{-r}}=\frac{1}{1-r}\lim_{x\to 0}x^{r}G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{k}{1-r}.

This means that

\lim_{x\to 0}x\frac{G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}=1-r

so in particular, there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
x\leq\delta
we have
x\frac{G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\in[-r,2-r]
and so
\frac{\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\leq\frac{r}{x}
. On the other hand,
G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is bounded on
[\delta,1-\delta]
by assumption.

To finish, let
t
as in Definition
3.3.1
equal
0.3
. Assume
c
is small enough that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\frac{\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\leq c^{-.16}
on
[\delta,1-\delta]
.

•

c^{-.01}\geq r
.

Then the last condition of Definition
3.3.1
will be satisfied on
[\delta,1-\delta]
; it will also be satisfied on
[c^{.3},\delta]
for any
c
satisfying the second condition above, because on that interval we have

\frac{\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\leq\frac{r}{x}\leq\frac{1}{c^{.16}\sqrt{x(1-x)}}\cdot\frac{c^{.16}r}{\sqrt{x}}\leq\frac{1}{c^{.16}\sqrt{x(1-x)}}\cdot\frac{c^{.16}r}{c^{.15}}\leq\frac{1}{c^{.16}\sqrt{x(1-x)}}.

By symmetry of
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
about
\frac{1}{2}
(and antisymmetry of
G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}
) we have that the condition also holds on
[1-\delta,1-c^{t}]
, as desired.

Now we consider the case that
r=1
. As above, we have
\lim_{x\to 0}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\infty
. Proceeding similarly, we have

\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}{\ln x}=\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)}{\frac{1}{x}}=\lim_{x\to 0}xG_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=k.

This means that there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
x\leq\delta
we have
x\ln x\frac{G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\in[0,2]
and so
\frac{\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\leq\frac{-2}{x\ln x}
. We finish as before.

Finally, consider the case that
0<r<1
. Let
a
be a lower bound on
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
, as in the statement of Claim
A.2.2
. It suffices to show that for for
c
small enough, we have
\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert\leq\frac{a}{c^{.16}\sqrt{x}}
on
[c^{.3},1-c^{.3}]
. Let
\delta
be such that
x^{r}G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\in[k-1,k+1]
for all
x\leq\delta
. On
[c^{.3},\delta]
we have

\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left\lvert k\right\rvert+1}{x^{r}}\leq\frac{\left\lvert k\right\rvert+1}{x}=\frac{a}{c^{.16}\sqrt{x}}\cdot\frac{\left\lvert k\right\rvert+1}{ac^{-.16}\sqrt{x}}\leq\frac{a}{c^{.16}\sqrt{x}}\cdot\frac{\left\lvert k\right\rvert+1}{ac^{-.16}\cdot c^{.15}}

if
c
is small enough that
c^{-.01}\geq\frac{\left\lvert k\right\rvert+1}{a}
. (As before, we also need to make sure that
c
is small enough that the condition is satisfied on
[\delta,1-\delta]
.) This concludes the proof.
∎

A.3
Details omitted from Section
3.3

A.3.1
Details omitted from Section
3.3.2

See
3.3.5

Proof.

By Lemma
3.2.7
, we have

\Delta_{n+1}=\frac{h+1}{n+2}G_{s}\left(\frac{h+2}{n+3}\right)+\frac{n-h+1}{n+2}G_{s}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+3}\right)-G_{s}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right).

Since
G_{s}
is twice differentiable, we may use Taylor’s approximation theorem to write

G_{s}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+3}\right)=G_{s}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)+\left(\frac{h+1}{n+3}-\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)G_{s}^{\prime}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+3}-\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)^{2}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})

for some
c_{1}\in\left[\frac{h+1}{n+3},\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right]
. Similarly we have

G_{s}\left(\frac{h+2}{n+3}\right)=G_{s}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)+\left(\frac{h+2}{n+3}-\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)G_{s}^{\prime}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{h+2}{n+3}-\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)^{2}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})

for some
c_{2}\in\left[\frac{h+1}{n+2},\frac{h+2}{n+3}\right]
. When we plug these expressions into the formula for
\Delta_{n+1}
above, the zeroth- and first-order terms cancel. We are left with

\displaystyle\Delta_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-h+1}{n+2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{h+1}{n+3}-\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)^{2}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})+\frac{h+1}{n+2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{h+2}{n+3}-\frac{h+1}{n+2}\right)^{2}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(h+1)^{2}(n-h+1)}{2(n+2)^{3}(n+3)^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})+\frac{(h+1)(n-h+1)^{2}}{2(n+2)^{3}(n+3)^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{q(1-q)}{2(n+3)^{2}}(qG_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})+(1-q)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{2})).

Note that
\left\lvert c_{1}-q\right\rvert\leq\frac{h+1}{n+2}-\frac{h+1}{n+3}\leq\frac{1}{n}
, so
c_{1}\in[q-\frac{1}{n},q+\frac{1}{n}]
, and similarly for
c_{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

See
3.3.6

Proof.

Suppose that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq a
for all
x\in(0,1)
. By Claim
3.3.5
we have

\Delta_{n+1}\geq\frac{q(1-q)a}{2(n+3)^{2}}.

Now, we have that
\frac{1}{n+2}\leq q\leq\frac{n-1}{n+2}
, and
q(1-q)
decreases as
q
gets farther from
\frac{1}{2}
. This means that

\Delta_{n+1}\geq\frac{\frac{n+1}{(n+2)^{2}}a}{2(n+3)^{2}}\geq\frac{a}{72(n+1)^{3}}.

Therefore, if
\Delta_{n+1}<c
then
n+1>\frac{1}{\left(\frac{72}{a}\right)^{1/3}c^{1/3}}
, so
n>\frac{1}{\alpha c^{1/3}}
for some
\alpha
(not to be confused with
a
), if
c
is small enough.
∎

A.3.2
Details omitted from Section
3.3.3

See
3.3.7

Proof.

Let
1\leq j_{p}\leq n-2
be such that
\frac{j_{p}}{n}\leq p\leq\frac{j_{p}+1}{n}
. We have

\frac{j_{p}}{n}-\frac{\sqrt{j_{p}(n-j_{p})}}{2n^{1.49}}\leq Q_{j_{p}/n}(n)\leq Q_{p}(n)\leq Q_{(j_{p}+1)/n}(n)\leq\frac{j_{p}+1}{n}+\frac{\sqrt{(j_{p}+1)(n-1-j_{p})}}{2n^{1.49}}

so

\displaystyle\left\lvert Q_{p}(n)-p\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\max\left(p-\frac{j_{p}}{n}+\frac{\sqrt{j_{p}(n-j_{p})}}{2n^{1.49}},\frac{j_{p}+1}{n}-p+\frac{\sqrt{(j_{p}+1)(n-1-j_{p})}}{2n^{1.49}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{2n^{1.49}}\max\left(\sqrt{j_{p}(n-j_{p})},\sqrt{(j_{p}+1)(n-1-j_{p})}\right).

For fixed
n
and for
\frac{1}{n}\leq p\leq 1-\frac{1}{n}
, this maximum divided by
n\sqrt{p(1-p)}
is maximized when
p=\frac{1}{n}
(or
p=1-\frac{1}{n}
), in which case the ratio is
\sqrt{\frac{2(n-2)}{n-1}}\leq\sqrt{2}
. Therefore we have

\frac{\left\lvert Q_{p}(n)-p\right\rvert}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}\leq\frac{1}{n\sqrt{p(1-p)}}+\frac{n\sqrt{2}}{2n^{1.49}}\leq\frac{1}{n^{.49}}

for
n
large enough. (Here we again use that
p\geq\frac{1}{n}
, so
\sqrt{p(1-p)}
is minimized at
p=\frac{1}{n}
.)
∎

See
3.3.8

Proof.

We have

\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\text{Pr}\left[\left\lvert Q_{j/n}(n)-\frac{j}{n}\right\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{j(n-j)}}{2n^{1.49}}\right].

Now, let
\mathcal{H}_{j/n}(n)
be the fraction of the first
n
coin flips that were heads (so
\mathcal{H}_{j/n}(n)
is an average of
n
i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables that are
1
with probability
\frac{j}{n}
). Note that
Q_{j/n}(n)
is within
\frac{1}{n}
of
\mathcal{H}_{j/n}(n)
, and for large
n
we have
\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{\sqrt{j(n-j)}}{2n^{1.49}}
for all
j
. This means that for large
n
, by the triangle inequality we have that if
\left\lvert\mathcal{H}_{j/n}(n)-\frac{j}{n}\right\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{j(n-j)}}{n^{1.49}}
then
\left\lvert Q_{j/n}(n)-\frac{j}{n}\right\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{j(n-j)}}{2n^{1.49}}
. Therefore, for large
N
we have

\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\text{Pr}\left[\left\lvert\mathcal{H}_{j/n}(n)-\frac{j}{n}\right\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{j(n-j)}}{n^{1.49}}\right].

We bound each of these probabilities. Recall the following version of the Chernoff bound: for
0\leq\delta\leq 1
, if
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
is a sum of i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables with
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=\mu
, then

\text{Pr}\left[\left\lvert X-\mu\right\rvert\geq\delta\mu\right]\leq 2e^{-\mu\delta^{2}/3}.

We apply this to our random variables (so
X=n\mathcal{H}_{j/n}(n)
and
\mu=j
). Assume
j\leq\frac{n}{2}
. Let
\delta=n^{-.49}\sqrt{\frac{n-j}{j}}
. Then

\text{Pr}\left[\left\lvert\frac{X}{n}-\frac{j}{n}\right\rvert\geq\frac{\sqrt{j(n-j)}}{n^{-1.49}}\right]\leq 2e^{-n^{.02}/6}.

If
j\geq\frac{n}{2}
, a symmetry argument yields the same result. Therefore, for sufficiently large
N
we have

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}2e^{-n^{.02}/6}\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}2ne^{-n^{.02}/6}\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}e^{-n^{.02}/7}=e^{-N^{.02}/7}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}e^{-(n^{.02}-N^{.02})/7}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-N^{.02}/7}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}e^{-n^{.02}/14}\leq e^{-N^{.02}/7}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\frac{14^{100}\cdot 100!}{n^{2}}.

The last step comes from observing that
e^{x}\geq\frac{x^{100}}{100!}
for positive
x
and plugging in
x=\frac{n^{.02}}{14}
. Now, this summand is bounded by a constant, since
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}
converges, and so we have

\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\leq O\left(e^{-N^{.02}/7}\right)\leq O\left(e^{-N^{.01}}\right),

as desired.
∎

A.3.3
Details omitted from Section
3.3.4

See
3.3.9

Proof.

We assume for convenience that
t<0.3
(which is safe, as Definition
3.3.1
holds for all
t\leq t^{\prime}
whenever it holds for
t^{\prime}
).

Fix
p\in[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]
. Let
\alpha
be as in Claim
3.3.6
. As before, let
Q(n)
be the predictor’s estimate for the bias of the coin after
n
flips. Then for
c
small enough that
\frac{1}{\alpha c^{1/3}}\geq N
and
c^{1/30}\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}
, for
n\geq\frac{1}{\alpha c^{1/3}}
, we have

p-\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}\leq Q(n)\leq p+\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}.

This follows from Claim
3.3.7
, noting that if
c^{1/30}\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}
and
n\geq\frac{1}{\alpha c^{1/3}}
then
\frac{1}{n}\leq p\leq 1-\frac{1}{n}
.

Now, recall Claim
3.3.5
:

\Delta_{n+1}=\frac{Q(n)(1-Q(n))}{2(n+3)^{2}}(Q(n)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})+(1-Q(n))G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{2}))

for some
c_{1},c_{2}\in[Q(n)-\frac{1}{n},Q(n)+\frac{1}{n}]
. In the remainder of this proof, what we essentially argue is that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
on this interval is not too far from
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)
, because of our bound on
Q(n)
as
p
plus or minus a small quantity.

We ask: for a given (possibly negative)
\epsilon
, how far from
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)
can
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p+\epsilon)
be? Well, since
G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is integrable, we have

\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p+\epsilon)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\int_{p}^{p+\epsilon}G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)dx\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\int_{p}^{p+\epsilon}\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert dx\right\rvert.

Now, since
s
is respectful we have that for
c
small enough, if
p,p+\epsilon\in[c^{t},1-c^{t}]
then

\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{c^{.16}\sqrt{x(1-x)}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq\frac{1}{c^{.16}\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x),

where
\hat{p}
is defined to be the number on the interval between
p
and
p+\epsilon
minimizing
\sqrt{x(1-x)}
(i.e. farthest from
\frac{1}{2}
).

Define
r:=\frac{1}{c^{.16}\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}}
. Then
\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert\leq rG_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)
. Below, we will use this fact to prove the following claim.

Claim A.3.1
.

\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p+\epsilon)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)\right\rvert\leq G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(e^{r\left\lvert\epsilon\right\rvert}-1)
.

Assuming the claim for now: how large of an
\epsilon
do we care about? The farthest that
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
can be from
p
is

\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}+\alpha c^{1/3}\leq 2\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49},

for small
c
. (This is because we assumed for convenience that
t<0.3
, which means that
p(1-p)\geq c^{.3}
, so
\alpha c^{1/3}\leq\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}
.) Therefore, by Claim
A.3.1
we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})\right\rvert,\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{2})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(e^{2r\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}}-1)

\displaystyle=G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)\exp\left(\frac{2\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}}{c^{.16}\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}}\right)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)

where
\hat{p}
is either
p\pm 2\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}
, whichever is farther from
\frac{1}{2}
. It is easy to check
1
1

1

Without loss of generality assume
p\leq\frac{1}{2}
, so
\hat{p}=p-2\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}
. Then
\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}}\leq\frac{p(1-p)}{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}\leq\frac{p}{\hat{p}}
, so it suffices to show that
2\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}\leq p
. This is indeed the case, as
p\geq c^{.3}\geq 4(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.98}
for small
c
, so
2\sqrt{p}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}\leq p
.
that for small enough
c
we have that
\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}}\leq 2
, and so we have

\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{1})\right\rvert,\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(c_{2})\right\rvert\leq G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\beta c^{1/300}\right)-G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)\leq\beta c^{1/300}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)

for small enough
c
, where
\beta=8\alpha^{.49}
. (Here we use that
e^{x}\leq 1+2x
for small positive
x
.) It follows, then, by Claim
3.3.5
, that

\frac{Q(n)(1-Q(n))}{2(n+3)^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1-\beta c^{1/300})\leq\Delta_{n+1}\leq\frac{Q(n)(1-Q(n))}{2(n+3)^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1+\beta c^{1/300}).

Note that since
Q(n)\geq p-\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}
and
1-Q(n)\geq 1-p-\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}
, we may write

\displaystyle Q(n)(1-Q(n))

\displaystyle\geq p(1-p)(1+(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.98})-\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}=p(1-p)\left(1-\frac{(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq p(1-p)\left(1-\frac{2(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}}{c^{t/2}}\right)\geq p(1-p)(1-\alpha^{.49}c^{.01})

for
c
small enough that the second-to-last step holds. (In the last step we use that
t<0.3
.) A similar calculation shows that
Q(n)(1-Q(n))\leq p(1-p)(1+\alpha^{.49}c^{.01})
for
c
small enough.
2
2

2

An extra
\alpha^{.49}c^{.49/3}
appears, but this term is dominated by
\alpha^{.49}c^{.01}
for small
c
.
Also note that
n^{2}\leq(n+3)^{2}\leq n^{2}(1+4\alpha c^{1/3})^{2}
. Putting these approximations all together, we note that the
c^{1/300}
approximation is the dominant one, which means that there is a constant
\gamma
such that

\frac{p(1-p)}{2n^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1-\gamma c^{1/300})\leq\Delta_{n+1}\leq\frac{p(1-p)}{2n^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1+\gamma c^{1/300}).

(A.2)

Therefore, since the expert stops flipping when
\Delta_{n+1}<c
, we have

\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1-\gamma c^{1/300})}\leq n_{\mathsf{stop}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1+\gamma c^{1/300})}.

This holds for any
p
such that
p\pm 2\sqrt{p(1-p)}(\alpha c^{1/3})^{.49}\in[c^{t},1-c^{t}]
; a sufficient condition is
p\in[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]
.
∎

Proof of Claim
A.3.1
.

We prove this for positive
\epsilon
. The result then follows for negative
\epsilon
because if some
G_{s}
is a counterexample for some negative
\epsilon
, then a function
G_{\tilde{s}}
defined so that
G_{\tilde{s}}^{\prime\prime\prime}(p+x):=-G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(p-x)
for
x\in[0,-\epsilon]
serves as a counterexample for
-\epsilon
. (This is because
G_{\tilde{s}}^{\prime\prime}(p+x)=G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p-x)
for all
x\in[0,-\epsilon]
, by the fundamental theorem of calculus.) Additionally, we may assume that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)=1
, because if there is a counterexample function
G_{s}
to the claim then
G_{\tilde{s}}(x):=\frac{G_{s}(x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}
also serves as a counterexample.

We prove that

e^{-r\epsilon}-1\leq G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p+\epsilon)-1\leq e^{r\epsilon}-1.

The left inequality suffices because
1-e^{-x}\leq e^{x}-1
for all
x
, so in particular
1-e^{r\epsilon}\leq e^{-r\epsilon}-1
.

We begin with the right inequality. Suppose for contradiction that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p+\epsilon)>e^{r\epsilon}
. Let
S
be the set of points in
[p,p+\epsilon]
where
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)>e^{r(x-p)}
. Since
S
contains
p+\epsilon
, it is nonempty; let
p_{1}=\inf_{S}
. Since
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
is continuous, we have
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{1})-e^{r(p_{1}-p)}=0
. Pick
\delta>0
small enough that the set
T
of points
x\in\left[p_{1},p_{1}+\min(\epsilon,\frac{1}{3r})\right]
with
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)-e^{r(x-p)}>\delta
is nonempty. Let
p_{2}=\inf_{T}
, so
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{2})-e^{r(p_{2}-p)}=\delta
. Note that

\delta=G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{2})-e^{r(p_{2}-p)}=G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{1})-e^{r(p_{1}-p)}+\int_{p_{1}}^{p_{2}}\frac{d}{dx}\left(G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)-e^{r(x-p)}\right)dx=\int_{p_{1}}^{p_{2}}\left(G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)-re^{r(x-p)}\right)dx.

It follows that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(p_{3})-re^{r(p_{3}-p)}\geq\frac{\delta}{p_{2}-p_{1}}\geq 2r\delta
for some
p_{3}\in[p_{1},p_{2}]
. (Otherwise the value of the integral would be at most the integral of
2r\delta
from
p_{1}
to
p_{2}
, which is at most
\frac{2}{3}\delta
, since
p_{2}-p_{1}\leq\frac{1}{3r}
.) Therefore, because
\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert\leq rG_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)
for all
x\in[p,p+\epsilon]
, we have that

rG_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{3})-re^{r(p_{3}-p)}\geq 2r\delta

so
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{3})-e^{r(p_{3}-p)}\geq 2\delta
. But then we have that
p_{2}<p_{3}
and
p_{3}\in T
, contradicting the definition of
p_{2}
as the infimum of
T
.

The proof of the left inequality above proceeds similarly, but is not exactly analogous. Suppose for contradiction that
t:=G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p+\epsilon)-e^{-r\epsilon}<0
. Define
p_{1}
to be the supremum of points in
[p,p+\epsilon]
where
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq e^{-r(x-p)}
(so
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{1})=e^{-r(p_{1}-p)}
). Then
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)-e^{-r(x-p)}
is zero at
x=p_{1}
and
t
at
x=p+\epsilon
, so

\int_{p_{1}}^{p+\epsilon}(G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)+re^{-r(x-p)})dx=t<0,

which means that for some
p_{2}\in[p_{1},p+\epsilon]
we have that
G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(p_{2})+re^{-r(p_{2}-p)}\leq\frac{t}{2(p+\epsilon-p_{1})}
(otherwise the value of the integral would be at least
\frac{t}{2}
). Since
\left\lvert G_{s}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\right\rvert\leq rG_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)
for all
x\in[p,p+\epsilon]
, we have that

-rG_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{2})+re^{-r(p_{2}-p)}\leq\frac{t}{2(p+\epsilon-p_{1})}<0,

so
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p_{2})>e^{-r(p_{2}-p)}
. This is a contradiction, since on the one hand we have
p_{2}>p_{1}
, but on the other hand
p_{1}
was defined as the supremum of points where
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq e^{-r(x-p)}
. This completes the proof.
∎

A.3.4
Details omitted from Section
3.3.5

See
3.3.10

A note on terminology:
We will sometimes speak of limits that hold “uniformly over
p
.” If we say that a function
g(c)
is
o(h(c))

uniformly
over
p
, we mean that
g
and
h
are implicitly functions of
p
as well, and that
\frac{h(x)}{g(x)}
approaches zero uniformly in
p
(i.e. for all
\epsilon
there exists
c_{\epsilon}
such that for all
c\leq c_{\epsilon}
, we have
\frac{h(x)}{g(x)}<\epsilon
for all (relevant) values of
p
). So for instance, the
o(1)
in the statement of Lemma
3.3.10
is uniform in
p
.

Proof.

Fix any
c
and
p\in[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]
. Let

n_{0}:=\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1-\gamma c^{1/300})}.

Let
d_{1}
be the expert’s error after
n_{0}
flips, i.e.
\left\lvert Q(n_{0})-p\right\rvert
. Let
d_{2}
be the distance from their guess after
n_{0}
flips to their guess after
n_{\mathsf{stop}}
flips. Then the expert’s error after
n_{\mathsf{stop}}
flips lies between
d_{1}-d_{2}
and
d_{1}+d_{2}
by the triangle inequality. That is, we have

\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2})\leq\text{Err}_{c}(p)\leq d_{1}+d_{2},

so

\mathbb{E}\left[\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2})^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[(\text{Err}_{c}(p))^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[(d_{1}+d_{2})^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]

which means that

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2})^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(\text{Err}_{c}(p))^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(d_{1}+d_{2})^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}.

Below, we will prove the following facts:

Claim A.3.2
.

We have

\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{2}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}=0

uniformly over
p\in[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]
. That is, for all
\epsilon
there exists
c_{\epsilon}
such that for all
c<c_{\epsilon}
, the fraction above is less than
\epsilon
for all
p\in[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]
.

Proposition A.3.3
.

Let
X_{c,p}
and
Y_{c,p}
be random variables taking values in
[0,1]
for each real number
c>0
and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
(some arbitrary set that depends on
c
). Let
\ell>0
. If
\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}=0
uniformly over
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
, then
\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}=1
uniformly over
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
. Separately, if
\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}=0
uniformly over
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
, then
\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}=1
uniformly over
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
.

Now, by combining Claim
A.3.2
with the first statement of Proposition
A.3.3
(with
X_{c,p}=d_{1}
and
Y_{c,p}=d_{2}
), we have

\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(d_{1}+d_{2})^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}=1.

By the second statement of Proposition
A.3.3
(with
X_{c,p}=\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2})
and
Y_{c,p}=d_{1}-\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2})
), we have

\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2})^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}=1.

Note that the premise of the second statement of Proposition
A.3.3
holds for these
X_{c,p}
and
Y_{c,p}
, because
Y_{c,p}=d_{1}-\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2})\leq d_{2}
and so certainly if
\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{2}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}=0
then
\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(d_{1}-\max(0,d_{1}-d_{2}))^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}=0
.

By the squeeze theorem, it follows that

\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(\text{Err}_{c}(p))^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]}=1.

Note that this limit holds uniformly over
p\in[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]
.

To complete the proof of Lemma
3.3.10
, we use the following fact, which we will also prove later.

Claim A.3.4
.

\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]=\mu_{\ell}\left(\frac{p(1-p)}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell/2}(1+o(1))

where the
o(1)
term is a function of
c
(but not
p
) that approaches zero as
c
approaches zero.

We have

n_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1-\gamma c^{1/300})}=\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}}(1+o(1)).

Therefore we have

\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]=\mu_{\ell}\left(\frac{2c}{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}\right)^{\ell/4}(1+o(1)),

as desired.
∎

We now return to the proofs that we deferred.

Proof of Proposition
A.3.3
.

The condition that
X_{c,p},Y_{c,p}\in[0,1]
is simply a convenient one to guarantee that all relevant expectations are finite. Now, for any
a\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{Pr}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}>z\right]dz=\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{Pr}\left[X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p}>z^{1/\ell}\right]dz

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\text{Pr}\left[X_{c,p}>(1-a)z^{1/\ell}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[Y_{c,p}>az^{1/\ell}\right]\right)dz

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\text{Pr}\left[\frac{X_{c,p}^{\ell}}{(1-a)^{\ell}}>z\right]+\text{Pr}\left[\frac{Y_{c,p}^{\ell}}{a^{\ell}}>z\right]\right)dz=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{(1-a)^{\ell}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{a^{\ell}},

(A.3)

where the inequality follows by a union bound.

We start with the first statement. Dividing Equation
A.3.4
by
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]
, we have

1\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}\leq\frac{1}{(1-a)^{\ell}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}\cdot\frac{1}{a^{\ell}}.

The limit of
\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}
as
c
approaches zero is
0
by assumption, so for
c
small enough we have that
\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}\cdot\frac{1}{a^{\ell}}\leq a
for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
. In other words, for every
a
there exists
c_{a}
such that for all
c\leq c_{a}
we have that
1\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}\leq\frac{1}{(1-a)^{\ell}}+a
. Since
\lim_{a\to 0}\frac{1}{(1-a)^{\ell}}+a=1
, we have that for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
c_{\epsilon}
such that for all
c\leq c_{\epsilon}
and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
we have that
1\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}\leq 1+\epsilon
. This proves the first statement.

As for the second statement, we divide Equation
A.3.4
by
\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]
to obtain

1\leq\frac{1}{(1-a)^{\ell}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}+\frac{1}{a^{\ell}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}

so

(1-a)^{\ell}\left(1-\frac{1}{a^{\ell}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}\right)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}\leq 1.

The limit of
\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}
as
c
approaches zero is
0
by assumption, so for
c
small enough we have that
\frac{1}{a^{\ell}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}\leq a
for all
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
. In other words, for every
a
there exists
c_{a}
such that for all
c\leq c_{a}
we have that
(1-a)^{\ell+1}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}\leq 1
. Since
\lim_{a\to 0}(1-a)^{\ell+1}=1
, we have that for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
c_{\epsilon}
such that for all
c\leq c_{\epsilon}
and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
we have that
1-\epsilon\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[X_{c,p}^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{c,p}+Y_{c,p})^{\ell}\right]}\leq 1
. This proves the second statement.
∎

Proof of Claim
A.3.4
.

We use the Berry-Esseen theorem, a result about the speed of convergence of a sum of i.i.d. random variables to a normal distribution.

Theorem A.3.5

(Berry-Esseen theorem)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. random variables with
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}\right]=0
,
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{2}\right]\equiv\sigma^{2}>0
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X_{1}\right\rvert^{3}\right]=\rho<\infty
. Let
Y=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}X_{i}
and let
F
be the CDF of
\frac{Y\sqrt{n}}{\sigma}
. Let
\varphi(x)
be the standard normal distribution. Then for all
x
we have

\left\lvert F(x)-\varphi(x)\right\rvert\leq\frac{C\rho}{\sigma^{3}\sqrt{n}},

for some universal constant
C
independent of
n
and the distribution of the
X_{i}
.

Define
X_{i}
to be
1-p
if the expert flips heads (which happens with probability
p
) and
-p
if the expert flips tails (which happens with probability
1-p
). Let
Y=\sum_{i}X_{i}
. Then
\sigma=\sqrt{p(1-p)}
and
\rho=p(1-p)(p^{2}+(1-p)^{2})\leq p(1-p)
. Plugging in these
X_{i}
and
n=n_{0}
into the Berry-Esseen theorem, we have

\left\lvert F(x)-\varphi(x)\right\rvert\leq\frac{Cp(1-p)}{(p(1-p))^{3/2}\sqrt{n_{0}}}=\frac{C}{\sqrt{p(1-p)n_{0}}}.

Now, we want to approximate
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Q(n_{0})-p\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]
. Note that
Q(n_{0})
is within
\frac{1}{n_{0}}
of
Y+p
, the number of heads flipped divided by
n_{0}
. This means that
Y-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\leq Q(n_{0})-p\leq Y+\frac{1}{n_{0}}
. For this reason, we focus on computing
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]
and subsequently correct for this small difference.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{Pr}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}>x\right]dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{Pr}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}>u^{\ell}\right]\cdot\ell u^{\ell-1}du

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}(\text{Pr}\left[Y>u\right]+\text{Pr}\left[Y<-u\right])\cdot\ell u^{\ell-1}du=\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\text{Pr}\left[\frac{Y\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}>\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right]+\text{Pr}\left[\frac{Y\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}<\frac{-u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right]\right)\cdot\ell u^{\ell-1}du

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-F\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)+F\left(\frac{-u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)\right)\cdot\ell u^{\ell-1}du.

Now, observe on the other hand that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(1-\varphi\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)+\varphi\left(\frac{-u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)\right)\cdot\ell u^{\ell-1}du=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Z\right\rvert^{\ell}\right],

where
Z
is a random variable drawn from a normal distribution with mean zero and variance
\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n_{0}}
. Now we ask: how different is this second quantity (the integral involving
\varphi
) from the first one (the integral involving
F
)?

The answer is: not that different. Indeed, as we derived,
F\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)
is within
\frac{C}{\sqrt{p(1-p)n_{0}}}
of
\varphi\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)
for all
u
. Furthermore, for any
r<\frac{1}{2}
, if
u\leq-\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{n_{0}^{r}}
then
F\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)
and
\varphi\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)
are both
e^{-n_{0}^{\Omega(1)}}
. (For
\varphi
this follows by concentration of normal distributions; the claim for
F
follows from Claim
3.3.8
, realizing the fact that there is nothing special about the
0.49
in the exponent except that it is less than
\frac{1}{2}
.) Similarly, if
u\geq\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{n_{0}^{r}}
then both
F\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)
and
\varphi\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)
are exponentially close to
1
. Finally, for
u\leq-1
we have
F\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)=0
and
\varphi\left(\frac{u\sqrt{n_{0}}}{\sigma}\right)=O(e^{-u^{2}n_{0}})
. This means that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]
is within

\int_{0}^{\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{n_{0}^{r}}}\frac{2C}{\sqrt{p(1-p)n_{0}}}\cdot\ell u^{\ell-1}du+e^{-n_{0}^{\Omega(1)}}=2C(p(1-p))^{(\ell-1)/2}n_{0}^{-r\ell-1/2}+e^{-n_{0}^{\Omega(1)}}

of
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Z\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]
. Now, since
p(1-p)\geq c^{t}\geq c^{.3}
and
n_{0}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha c^{1/3}}
, we have that
p(1-p)\geq(\alpha n_{0})^{-.9}
. It is easy to check that setting any
r>\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{20\ell}
shows that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Z\right\rvert^{\ell}\right](1+o(1))

where the
o(1)
depends only on
c
, not on
p
. Note that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Z\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]=\Theta\left(\frac{p(1-p)}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell/2}=\omega\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}^{\ell}}\right)
uniformly over
p
. This means that
\frac{1}{n_{0}^{\ell}}=o\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert\right]^{\ell}\right)
uniformly over
p
.

Now, recall that
Y-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\leq Q(n_{0})-p\leq Y+\frac{1}{n_{0}}
, so

\max\left(0,\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)\leq\left\lvert Q(n_{0})-p\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert Y\right\rvert+\frac{1}{n_{0}},

and thus

\mathbb{E}\left[\max\left(0,\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Q(n_{0})-p\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left\lvert Y\right\rvert+\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell}\right].

By the first statement of Proposition
A.3.3
(with
X_{c,p}=\left\lvert Y\right\rvert
and
Y_{c,p}=\frac{1}{n_{0}}
), we have that

\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left\lvert Y\right\rvert+\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]}=1

uniformly over
p
. By the second statement (with
X_{c,p}=\max\left(0,\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)
and
Y_{c,p}=\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\max\left(0,\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)
), we have that

\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\max\left(0,\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]}=1

uniformly over
p
. (The premise of the second statement is satisfied because
\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\max\left(0,\left\lvert Y\right\rvert-\frac{1}{n_{0}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{n_{0}}
.) Therefore, the squeeze theorem tells us that

\lim_{c\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]}=1

uniformly over
p
. Therefore, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Z\right\rvert^{\ell}\right](1+o(1))
where the
o(1)
term only depends on
c
.

Finally, note that

\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\Omega_{N}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right].

Since
0\leq\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\leq 1
and
\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]=e^{-n_{0}^{\Omega(1)}}
(where the
\Omega(1)
does not depend on
p
), we have that
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]
is within
e^{-n_{0}^{\Omega(1)}}
of
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}\right]
. Applying Proposition
A.3.3
in the same way as earlier, we find that
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right](1+o(1))=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Z\right\rvert^{\ell}\right](1+o(1))
. We know that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Z\right\rvert^{\ell}\right]=\mu_{\ell}\left(\frac{p(1-p)}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell/2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Claim
A.3.2
.

Let
k=n_{\text{stop}}-n_{0}
. Define
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
as follows:
Y_{0}=0
and for
i>0
,
Y_{i}
is either
Y_{i-1}+1-p
(if the
n_{0}+i
-th flip is heads, i.e. with probability
p
) or
Y_{i-1}-p
(if the
n_{0}+i
-th flip is tails, i.e. with probability
1-p
. Note that
\{Y_{i}\}
is a martingale.

Now, observe that for any
0\leq i\leq k
, we have

Q(n_{0}+i)=\frac{(n_{0}+2)Q(n_{0})+Y_{i}+pi}{n_{0}+i+2}.

This is because
(n_{0}+2)Q(n_{0})
is one more than the number of heads in the first
n_{0}
flips and
(n_{0}+i+2)Q(n_{0}+i)
is one more than the number of heads in the first
n_{0}+i
flips. Thus,

\left\lvert Q(n_{0}+i)-Q(n_{0})\right\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{Y_{i}+i(p-Q(n_{0}))}{n_{0}+i+2}\right\rvert\leq\frac{\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert+k\left\lvert p-Q(n_{0})\right\rvert}{n_{0}}=\frac{\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert+kd_{1}}{n_{0}}.

Therefore we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d_{2}^{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{i\leq k}\left(\left\lvert Q(n_{0}+i)-Q(n_{0})\right\rvert^{\ell}\right)\right]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert+kd_{1})^{\ell}\right]}{n_{0}^{\ell}}=\frac{2^{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert+kd_{1}}{2}\right)^{\ell}\right]}{n_{0}^{\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert)^{\ell}+(kd_{1})^{\ell}}{2}\right]}{n_{0}^{\ell}}=\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{n_{0}^{\ell}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{\ell}\right]+k^{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right]\right).

Here, the last inequality follows from the fact that the arithmetic mean of
\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert
and
kd_{1}
is less than or equal to the
\ell
-power mean (since
\ell\geq 1
).

Now, it is clear that
\frac{2^{\ell-1}k^{\ell}}{n^{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right]=o(\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right])
, since
k=o(n_{0})
by Proposition
3.3.9
. We now show that
\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{n_{0}^{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{\ell}\right]=o(\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right])
. We make use of a tool called the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality.

Definition A.3.6
.

Let
Y=\{Y_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
be a martingale. The
quadratic variation
of
Y
, denoted
[Y]
, is equal to

[Y]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(Y_{i}-Y_{i-1})^{2}.

Note that
[Y]
is a random variable, not a number.

Theorem A.3.7

(Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality)
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
. There is a constant
C_{\ell}
such that for every martingale
Y=\{Y_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k}
with
Y_{0}=0
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\max_{i=0}^{k}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{\ell}\right]\leq C_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[[Y]^{\ell/2}\right].

We wish to bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\max_{i=0}^{k}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{\ell}\right]
above. To do so, we bound
\mathbb{E}\left[[Y]^{\ell/2}\right]
above. Observe that
[Y]
is a sum of
k
independent random variables that are each either
p^{2}
(with probability
1-p
) or
(1-p)^{2}
(with probability
p
). Thus,
\mathbb{E}\left[[Y]\right]=kp(1-p)\equiv\mu
. Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[[Y]^{\ell/2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{Pr}\left[[Y]^{\ell/2}\geq x\right]dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{Pr}\left[[Y]\geq x^{2/\ell}\right]dx\leq(3\mu)^{\ell/2}+\int_{(3\mu)^{\ell/2}}^{\infty}\text{Pr}\left[[Y]\geq x^{2/\ell}\right]dx

\displaystyle\leq(3\mu)^{\ell/2}+\int_{(3\mu)^{\ell/2}}^{\infty}e^{\frac{\mu-x^{2/\ell}}{2}}dx.

The last line comes from a Chernoff bound. In particular, we have that
\text{Pr}\left[[Y]\geq\mu(1+\delta)\right]\leq e^{-\delta^{2}\mu/(2+\delta)}\leq e^{-\delta\mu/2}
for
\delta\geq 2
. Setting
\delta=\frac{x^{2/\ell}}{\mu}-1
gives us the expression above. Now, we can bound the integral as follows:

\int_{(3\mu)^{\ell/2}}^{\infty}e^{\frac{\mu-x^{2/\ell}}{2}}dx\leq((5\mu)^{\ell/2}-(3\mu)^{\ell/2})e^{-\mu}+((7\mu)^{\ell/2}-(5\mu)^{\ell/2})e^{-2\mu}+\dots\equiv B(\mu).

Note that
B(\mu)
is continuous, converges on
[0,\infty)
, and approaches zero as
\mu\to\infty
. It follows that
B(\mu)
is bounded on
[0,\infty)
; in other words, our integral is
O(1)
(i.e. possibly depends on
\ell
but is at most a constant for fixed
\ell
). Therefore, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{\ell}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[[Y]^{\ell/2}\right]\leq 3^{\ell/2}(kp(1-p))^{\ell/2}+O(1).

Therefore we have

\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{n_{0}^{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\max_{i}\left\lvert Y_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{\ell}\right]=O\left(\left(\frac{p(1-p)}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{k}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell/2}\right)=O\left(\left(\frac{k}{n_{0}}\right)^{\ell/2}\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right]\right)=o(\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right]).

Therefore, we have that
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{2}^{\ell}\right]=o(\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right])
. By the same reasoning as in the proof of Claim
A.3.4
below, it follows that
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{2}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]=o(\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right])
. We previously showed that
\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\right]=\Theta(\mathbb{E}\left[d_{1}^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right])
. This completes the proof.
∎

A.3.5
Details omitted from Section
3.3.6
and proof of Theorem
3.3.3

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
3.3.3
. We begin by proving the analog of Theorem
3.3.3
for a locally adaptive expert.

Theorem A.3.8
.

If
s
is a respectful, normalizable, continuously differentiable proper scoring rule, and
\text{Err}_{c}(p)
is the expected error of a locally adaptive expert scored by
s
when the coin has bias
p
and the cost of a flip is
c
, then

\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\right]=\mu_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{2x(1-x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx.

Proof.

Let
N=\frac{1}{\alpha c^{1/3}}
, i.e. a large enough function of
c
that it is guaranteed that the expert flips the coin at least
N
times. We have

c^{-\ell/4}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\right]=c^{-\ell/4}\left(\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\Omega_{N}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\right).

We wish to compute the limit of this quantity as
c
approaches zero. Note that

\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]=0.

This is because
\text{Err}_{c}(p)
is bounded between
0
and
1
and
\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]=O(e^{-N^{.01}})=O(e^{-\Omega(c^{-1/300})})
, which goes to zero faster than
c^{-\ell/4}
goes to infinity. Therefore we have

\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\right]=\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right].

(We may ignore the
\text{Pr}\left[\Omega_{N}\right]
term above because it approaches
1
in the limit.) We may write this quantity as

\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\left((1-4c^{t})\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]+4c^{t}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,2c^{t}]\cup[1-2c^{t},1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]\right).

Let us focus on the second summand. Let
p\in[0,2c^{t}]\cup[1-2c^{t},1]
. We assume
p\in[0,2c^{t}]
; the other case is analogous.

We consider two sub-cases:
p\in[0,\alpha c^{1/3}]
and
p\in[\alpha c^{1/3},2c^{t}]
. First suppose that
p\in[c^{1/3},2c^{t}]
. Note that since
\Omega_{N}
holds, we have for all
n\geq N
that

\left\lvert Q(n)-p\right\rvert\leq\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{n^{.49}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2c^{t}}}{N^{.49}}=\sqrt{2}\alpha^{.49}c^{t/2+.49/3}.

This in particular is true of
n=n_{\mathsf{stop}}
, so

\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\leq(\sqrt{2}\alpha^{.49})^{\ell}c^{\ell(t/2+.49/3)}=o(c^{\ell/4})

since
t>\frac{1}{4}
and so
\frac{t}{2}+\frac{.49}{3}>\frac{1}{4}
.

Now suppose that
p\in[0,\alpha c^{1/3}]
. Recall the notation
Q_{p}(n)
from the discussion preceding the definition of
\Omega_{N}
. For any
n\geq N
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert Q(n)-p\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert Q(n)-Q_{\alpha c^{1/3}}(n)\right\rvert+\left\lvert Q_{\alpha c^{1/3}}(n)-\alpha c^{1/3}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\alpha c^{1/3}-p\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq Q_{\alpha c^{1/3}}(n)+\left\lvert Q_{\alpha c^{1/3}}(n)-\alpha c^{1/3}\right\rvert+\alpha c^{1/3}\leq 2\alpha c^{1/3}+2\left\lvert Q_{\alpha c^{1/3}}(n)-\alpha c^{1/3}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha c^{1/3}+\frac{2\sqrt{\alpha c^{1/3}}}{n^{.49}}\leq 2\alpha c^{1/3}+2\sqrt{\alpha}c^{1/6+.49/3}=o(c^{1/4})

so
\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}=o(c^{\ell/4})
.

This means that

\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\cdot 4c^{t}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,2c^{t}]\cup[1-2c^{t},1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]=0

so we can ignore this summand. Therefore, we have

\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\right]=\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}(1-4c^{t})\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right].

From Lemma
3.3.10
, we have that

\displaystyle(1-o(1))\int_{2c^{t}}^{1-2c^{t}}\mu_{\ell}\left(\frac{2x(1-x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx

\displaystyle\leq c^{-\ell/4}(1-4c^{t})\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[2c^{t},1-2c^{t}]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\mid\Omega_{N}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\int_{2c^{t}}^{1-2c^{t}}\mu_{\ell}\left(\frac{2x(1-x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx.

By the squeeze theorem, we conclude that.

\lim_{c\to 0}c^{-\ell/4}\mathbb{E}_{p\leftarrow U_{[0,1]}}\left[\text{Err}_{c}(p)^{\ell}\right]=\lim_{c\to 0}\int_{2c^{t}}^{1-2c^{t}}\mu_{\ell}\left(\frac{2x(1-x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx=\mu_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{2x(1-x)}{G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx.

∎

See
3.3.11

Proof.

Our approach will be to compare the behavior of a locally adaptive expert to that of a globally adaptive one. We will assume that the experts observe the same stream of coin flips (each heads with probability
p
unknown to the experts) but that they may decide to stop at different times. As before, we will let
Q(n)=\frac{h+1}{n+2}
where
h
is the number of the first
n
flips to have come up heads; since the experts see the same coin flips, we do not need to distinguish between
Q(n)
for the locally adaptive expert and for the globally adaptive expert. We will let
n_{l}
and
n_{g}
be the number of times the locally and globally adaptive experts flip the coin, respectively (so
n_{g}\geq n_{l}
). (We used the notation
n_{\mathsf{stop}}
in place of
n_{l}
in Proposition
3.3.9
.) Let
t
be as in the definition of respectful scoring rules, and in particular we will assume that
t<0.3
as before (for any
t
that witnesses that a scoring rule is respectful, any smaller
t>\frac{1}{4}
also works).

Suppose the globally adaptive expert flips the coin
N:=(1+6\gamma c^{1/300})n_{l}
times. We show that they do not flip the coin another time.

By definition of
\Omega_{n_{l}}
, we have that

p-\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{n_{l}^{.49}}\leq Q(n_{l})\leq p+\frac{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}{n_{l}^{.49}}.

It is easy to check that because
n_{l}=\Omega(c^{-1/3})
(by Claim
3.3.6
) and
4c^{t}\leq Q(n_{l})\leq 1-4c^{t}
(so
Q(n_{l}),1-Q(n_{l})=\Omega(n_{l}^{-.9})
), the above relationship between
Q(n_{l})
and
p
implies that
2c^{t}\leq p\leq 1-2c^{t}
. This allows us to use some results from our analysis of locally adaptive experts. In particular, by Equation
A.2
in the proof of Proposition
3.3.9
, we have that

\frac{p(1-p)}{2n_{l}^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1-\gamma c^{1/300})\leq\Delta_{n_{l}+1}\leq\frac{p(1-p)}{2n_{l}^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1+\gamma c^{1/300}).

Conditional on
\Omega_{N}
(and by definition
\Omega_{n_{l}}
implies
\Omega_{N}
), we also have

\frac{p(1-p)}{2N^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1-\gamma c^{1/300})\leq\Delta_{N+1}\leq\frac{p(1-p)}{2N^{2}}G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)(1+\gamma c^{1/300}).

In particular this means that

\Delta_{N+1}\leq\frac{n_{l}^{2}}{N^{2}}\cdot\frac{1+\gamma c^{1/300}}{1-\gamma c^{1/300}}\Delta_{n_{l}+1}\leq\left(\frac{n_{l}}{N}\right)^{2}(1+3\gamma c^{1/300})\Delta_{n_{l}+1}

for
c
small enough. This means that if
\left(\frac{n_{l}}{N}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{1+6\gamma c^{1/300}}
then
\Delta_{N+1}\leq(1-2\gamma c^{1/300})\Delta_{n_{l}+1}<c(1-2\gamma c^{1/300})
. Furthermore, for any
n\geq N
we will have
\Delta_{n+1}<c(1-2\gamma c^{1/300})
.

However, this does not mean that the globally adaptive expert won’t flip the coin for the
N+1
-th time, because they don’t know that
\Omega_{n_{l}}
is true. From the expert’s perspective,
if
they knew
\Omega_{n_{l}}
(or even
\Omega_{N}
) to be true, they would stop flipping the coin; but perhaps they should keep flipping the coin because of the outside chance that
\Omega_{N}
is false.

This turns out not to be the case, because the probability that
\Omega_{N}
is false is so small. In particular, from the expert’s perspective, if
\Omega_{N}
is false, they cannot achieve a score that is better than the expectation of
G_{s}(p)
conditional on the coins they’ve flipped and on
\Omega_{N}
being false. We show that if the scoring rule
s
is normalizable (i.e.
\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx
is finite), then this quantity isn’t too large. In particular, we show the following:

Claim A.3.9
.

Let
H_{N}
be the random variable corresponding to the number of heads flipped in the first
N
flips. Then for any
3c^{t}N\leq h\leq(1-3c^{t})N
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[G_{s}(p)\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}},H_{N}=h\right]\leq\frac{2}{c^{t}}\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx.

Proof.

We have

\mathbb{E}\left[G_{s}(p)\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}},H_{N}=h\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[G_{s}(p)\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}},H_{N}=h,p<\frac{1}{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[G_{s}(p)\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}},H_{N}=h,p>\frac{1}{2}\right].

Let us consider the expectation conditioned on
p<\frac{1}{2}
. Consider the distribution
D
of
p
conditioned on
\overline{\Omega_{N}}
,
H_{N}=h
, and
p<\frac{1}{2}
. Consider also the uniform distribution
D^{\prime}
on
[0,c^{t}]
.

We claim that
D
stochastically dominates
D^{\prime}
, i.e.
\text{Pr}_{x\leftarrow D}\left[x\leq y\right]\leq\text{Pr}_{x\leftarrow D^{\prime}}\left[x\leq y\right]
for all
y
. To see this, observe that the PDF of
D
is an increasing function on
[0,c^{t}]
. This is because
D
on
[0,c^{t}]
is a constant multiple of the distribution
D^{\prime\prime}
of
p
conditioned on
\overline{\Omega_{N}}
,
H_{N}=h
, and
p\leq c^{t}
; but in this case the condition
\overline{\Omega_{N}}
is redundant because if
p\leq c^{t}
then
\overline{\Omega_{N}}
holds. So
D^{\prime\prime}
is the distribution of
p
conditioned on
H_{N}=h
and
p\leq c^{t}
. Clearly the PDF of
D^{\prime\prime}
increases on
[0,c^{t}]
(because the expert starts with uniform priors and updates more strongly on against values of
p
farther from
\frac{h+1}{N+2}
, which is greater than
2.9c^{t}
for
c
small enough).

Now, the expectation of
G_{s}(p)
if
p
were drawn from
D^{\prime}
instead of
D
is equal to
\frac{1}{c^{t}}\int_{0}^{c^{t}}G_{s}(x)dx\leq\frac{1}{c^{t}}\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx
. On the other hand, the actual expectation of
G_{s}(p)
(i.e. with
p
drawn from
D
) is necessarily smaller. This is because
G_{s}
is convex and symmetric about
\frac{1}{2}
, meaning that
G_{s}
is decreasing on
(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Since
D
stochastically dominates
D^{\prime}
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\left[G_{s}(p)\mid\overline{\Omega_{N}},H_{N}=h,p<\frac{1}{2}\right]\leq\frac{1}{c^{t}}\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx.

The same inequality holds conditional instead on
p>\frac{1}{2}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

From the expert’s perspective, this means that if they flip the coin for the
N+1
-th time, then:

•

In the case that
\Omega_{N}
is true, the best case is that they never flip the coin again, in which case they will pay a total cost of
c
and get expected score at most
c(1-2\gamma c^{1/300})
.

•

In the case that
\Omega_{N}
is false, the best case is that they get a score of
\frac{2}{c^{t}}\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx
.

In other words, the expert’s expected score if they flip the coin for the
N+1
-th time and pursue the optimal strategy from there is at most

\frac{2}{c^{t}}\int_{0}^{1}G_{s}(x)dx\cdot\text{Pr}\left[\overline{\Omega_{N}}\right]-2\gamma c^{1/300}\text{Pr}\left[\Omega_{N}\right]\leq O\left(\frac{e^{-N^{.01}}}{c^{t}}\right)-\gamma c^{1/300}=O\left(\frac{e^{-\Omega(c^{1/300})}}{c^{t}}\right)-\gamma c^{1/300}.

The first step is nontrivial: it uses the fact that the probability that the expert assigns to
\overline{\Omega_{N}}
after the first
N
flips is
O(e^{-N^{.01}})
. This doesn’t immediately follow from Claim
3.3.8
because the claim only states that the prior probability of
\overline{\Omega_{N}}
, i.e. before any flips, is
O(e^{-N^{.01}})
. To see that the posterior probability (after the first
N
flips) is also of this order, we first observe that the posterior probability cannot depend on the order of the flip outcomes; this is apparent from the definition of
\Omega_{N}
. However, perhaps the
number
of heads, i.e. the value of
H_{N}
, affects the posterior probability of
\overline{\Omega_{N}}
. This may be so, but it cannot increase the probability by more than a factor of
N+1
. That is because the prior for
H_{N}
is uniform over
\{0,\dots,N\}
(Fact
A.1.1
).

Now, the quantity on the right is negative for
c
small enough, so the expert will not flip the
N+1
-th coin. This proves the claim.
∎

The following corollary is essentially identical to Proposition
3.3.9
but for globally adaptive experts.

Corollary A.3.10
.

Assume that
\Omega_{N}
holds for some
N
. For sufficiently small
c
, for all
p\in[8c^{t},1-8c^{t}]
, we have

\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1-14\gamma c^{1/300})}\leq n_{g}\leq\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1+14\gamma c^{1/300})}.

Proof.

Because
\Omega_{N}
holds for some
N
, for sufficiently small
c
the fact that
p\in[8c^{t},1-8c^{t}]
implies that
Q(n_{l})\in[4c^{t},1-4c^{t}]
. This means that we may apply Lemma
3.3.11
to say that
n_{g}\leq N
. Consequently we have that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1-\gamma c^{1/300})}\leq n_{l}\leq n_{g}\leq N=(1+6\gamma c^{1/300})n_{l}

\displaystyle\leq(1+6\gamma c^{1/300})\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1+\gamma c^{1/300})}\leq\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1+14\gamma c^{1/300})}

for
c
small enough. Therefore we have

\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1-14\gamma c^{1/300})}\leq n_{g}\leq\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(p)}{2c}(1+14\gamma c^{1/300})}.

∎

Theorem
3.3.3
follows as a simple corollary.

See
3.3.3

Proof.

The lemma analogous to Lemma
3.3.10
but for expected globally adaptive error, and for
p\in[8c^{t},1-8c^{t}]
, follows immediately from Corollary
A.3.10
. This is because the proof of Lemma
3.3.10
makes no assumptions about the specific value of
\gamma
(other than that it is positive), which means that the proof goes through just as well for
14\gamma
in place of
\gamma
. Theorem
3.3.3
follows from this fact exactly in the same way that Theorem
A.3.8
followed from Lemma
3.3.10
.
∎

A.4
Details omitted from Section
3.4

See
3.4.2

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{\ell}=\frac{\ell}{4\kappa_{\ell}^{\ell/4+1}}
. Consider the functional

\displaystyle\chi(h)
