\displaystyle:=\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)^{2}}{h(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx+\lambda_{\ell}\left(\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)h(x)dx-1\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\left(\frac{x(1-x)^{2}}{h(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}+\lambda_{\ell}(1-x)h(x)\right)dx-\lambda_{\ell}.

It suffices to show that among all
h
satisfying
\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)h(x)dx=1
,
\tilde{h}
minimizes
\chi(h)
. This is because among such
h
, the second summand in the definition of
\chi
is always zero. In fact, we prove something stronger:
\tilde{h}
minimizes
\chi
, among all functions from
[\frac{1}{2},1)
to
\mathbb{R}
. To show this, it suffices to show that for every
x\in[\frac{1}{2},1)
, the value
y
that minimizes

\left(\frac{x(1-x)^{2}}{y}\right)^{\ell/4}+\lambda_{\ell}(1-x)y

is
y=\tilde{h}(x)
. The derivative with respect to
y
of this expression is

\frac{-\ell}{4}(x(1-x)^{2})^{\ell/4}y^{-(\ell/4+1)}+\lambda_{\ell}(1-x),

which is an increasing function of
y
(since
y^{-(\ell/4+1)}
is a decreasing function of
y
and
\frac{-\ell}{4}(x(1-x)^{2})^{\ell/4}
is negative). It is equal to
0
precisely when

y=\left(\frac{\ell}{4\lambda_{\ell}}\right)^{4/(\ell+4)}(x^{\ell}(1-x)^{2\ell-4})^{1/(\ell+4)}=\kappa_{\ell}(x^{\ell}(1-x)^{2\ell-4})^{1/(\ell+4)}=\tilde{h}(x).

It remains only to note that
\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)\tilde{h}(x)dx=1
, and this follows immediately from the definition of
\tilde{h}
and
\kappa_{\ell}
.
∎

See
3.4.3

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that there is another continuous function
\hat{h}
satisfying the above properties that achieves the minimum. Then
\chi(\hat{h})=\chi(\tilde{h})
, with
\chi
as in the proof of Lemma
3.4.2
, since
\tilde{h}
minimizes
\chi
and the second summand in the definition of
\chi
is zero for both
\hat{h}
and
\tilde{h}
. In particular, we have that
\chi(\hat{h})-\chi(\tilde{h})=0
, i.e.

\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\left(\left(\frac{x(1-x)^{2}}{\hat{h}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}+\lambda_{\ell}(1-x)\hat{h}(x)\right)-\left(\left(\frac{x(1-x)^{2}}{\tilde{h}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}+\lambda_{\ell}(1-x)\tilde{h}(x)\right)\right)dx=0.

Let
\Delta(x)
be the integrand. Note that
\Delta
is always nonnegative, and is zero precisely for those values of
x
where
\hat{h}(x)=\tilde{h}(x)
(since, as we showed earlier,
y=\tilde{h}(x)
is the unique value minimizing
\left(\frac{x(1-x)^{2}}{y}\right)^{\ell/4}+\lambda_{\ell}(1-x)y
). Since
\hat{h}\neq\tilde{h}
,
\Delta
is positive at some
x_{0}
; say
\Delta(x_{0})=y_{0}
. Also, note that
\Delta
is continuous because
\hat{h}
and
\tilde{h}
are continuous. This means that for some
\delta>0
,
\left\lvert\Delta(x)-y_{0}\right\rvert<\frac{y_{0}}{2}
for all
x
such that
x_{0}\leq x\leq x_{0}+\delta
. But this means that the integral of
\Delta
on
[x_{0},x_{0}+\delta]
is at least
\frac{\delta\cdot y_{0}}{2}>0
, so
\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\Delta(x)>0
, a contradiction. Therefore,
\tilde{h}
is indeed the unique continuous function satisfying the stated constraints.
∎

See
3.4.1

Proof.

We have reasoned that
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is the antiderivative of
\tilde{h}
on
[\frac{1}{2},1)
, which gives us
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
for
x\geq\frac{1}{2}
. For
x<\frac{1}{2}
, we have

s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1-x}{x}s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}^{\prime}(1-x)=\kappa_{\ell}\frac{1-x}{x}((1-x)^{\ell}x^{2\ell-4})^{1/(\ell+4)}=\kappa_{\ell}(x^{\ell-8}(1-x)^{2\ell+4})^{1/(\ell+4)},

which extends to the stated function
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
by the fundamental theorem of calculus. The constant we want to add (upon taking the antiderivative) is zero so that
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)=0
. We need to check that
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is continuously differentiable at
\frac{1}{2}
, which means checking that
\tilde{h}
is continuous at
\frac{1}{2}
when extended to
(0,1)
. This is indeed the case because

\lim_{x\to\frac{1}{2}^{-}}s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{x\to\frac{1}{2}^{-}}\frac{1-x}{x}s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}^{\prime}(1-x)=\lim_{x\to\frac{1}{2}^{-}}s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}^{\prime}(1-x)=\lim_{x\to\frac{1}{2}^{+}}s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}^{\prime}(x).

Finally,
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is the unique continuous normalized minimizer because its derivative is unique, by Corollary
3.4.3
. Note that
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is in fact strictly proper since
\tilde{h}(x)
is positive on
[\frac{1}{2},1)
.
∎

See
3.4.4

Proof of Remark
3.4.4
for
1\leq\ell\leq 8
.

This proof handles the case of
\ell\in[1,8]
. Let us write
G_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
to mean
G_{s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}}
. Then

G^{\prime\prime}_{\ell,\text{Opt}}(x)=\frac{s^{\prime}_{\ell,\text{Opt}}(x)}{1-x}=\begin{cases}\kappa_{\ell}(x^{\ell-8}(1-x)^{\ell})^{1/(\ell+4)}&x\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
\kappa_{\ell}(x^{\ell}(1-x)^{\ell-8})^{1/(\ell+4)}&x\geq\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

First note that
G_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is strongly convex. Since
G^{\prime\prime}_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is symmetric, it suffices to show this on
(0,\frac{1}{2}]
. We have
1-x\geq\frac{1}{2}
on this interval, and
x^{(\ell-8)/(\ell+4)}
is bounded away from zero when
x\leq 8
. Next, the fact that
G^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is Riemann integral on any closed sub-interval of
(0,1)
is evident. Finally, there are constants
k\neq 0
and
r
such that
\lim_{x\to 0}x^{r}G^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\ell,\text{Opt}}(x)=k
: in particular,
r=\frac{12}{\ell+4}
and
k=\frac{(\ell-8)\kappa_{\ell}}{\ell+4}
.

(Note that for
\ell>8
,
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is not respectful, since
\lim_{x\to 0}G^{\prime\prime}_{\ell,\text{Opt}}(x)=0
.)
∎

A.5
Details omitted from Section
3.6

See
3.6.2

Proof.

Suppose that
s
is a proper scoring rule. Then
s
is nonconstant,
s^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
everywhere by Lemma
3.2.4
, and by the same lemma we have that

xs^{\prime}(x)=(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x).

Taking successive derivatives of both sides, we have

\displaystyle s^{\prime}(x)+xs^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=-s^{\prime}(1-x)-(1-x)s^{\prime\prime}(1-x)

\displaystyle 2s^{\prime\prime}(x)+xs^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=2s^{\prime\prime}(1-x)+(1-x)s^{\prime\prime\prime}(1-x)

\displaystyle 3s^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)+xs^{(4)}(x)

\displaystyle=-3s^{\prime\prime\prime}(1-x)-(1-x)s^{(4)}(1-x)

and so on. Plugging in
x=\frac{1}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle s^{\prime}\left(1/2\right)+1/2s^{\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)

\displaystyle=-s^{\prime}\left(1/2\right)-1/2s^{\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)

\displaystyle 2s^{\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)+1/2s^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)

\displaystyle=2s^{\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)+1/2s^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)

\displaystyle 3s^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)+1/2s^{(4)}\left(1/2\right)

\displaystyle=-3s^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)-1/2s^{(4)}\left(1/2\right)

and so on. These equations alternate between giving us tautologies and simplifying to the following identities:

s^{\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right)=-2s^{\prime}\left(1/2\right);\quad s^{(4)}\left(1/2\right)=-6s^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(1/2\right);\quad s^{(6)}\left(1/2\right)=-10f^{(5)}\left(1/2\right);

and so on, the general form of the identities being that for
k
odd, we have

s^{(k+1)}\left(1/2\right)=-2ks^{(k)}\left(1/2\right).

Since
s
is analytic, we have

\displaystyle s(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}s^{(n)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=s\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)+\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\left((k+1)s^{(k)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}+s^{(k+1)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k+1}\right).

Letting
c_{k}=\frac{1}{(k+1)!}s^{(k)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)
for
k=0,1,3,5,\dots
, we have

\displaystyle s(x)

\displaystyle=c_{0}+\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}c_{k}\left((k+1)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}-2k\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k+1}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{0}+\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}c_{k}(2k+1-2kx)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}.

This proves the forward direction. Conversely, we claim that if
s
is nonconstant,
s^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
everywhere, and
s
can be written in the stated form for some
c_{0},c_{1},c_{3},\dots
, then
s
is a proper scoring rule. We only have to verify that
xs^{\prime}(x)=(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x)
everywhere (by Lemma
3.2.4
and Lemma
A.5.1
(stated and proven below)). Taking the derivative of
(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x)
term by term, we have

\displaystyle(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x)

\displaystyle=(x-1)(s(1-x))^{\prime}=(x-1)\frac{d}{dx}\left(c_{0}+\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}c_{k}(2kx+1)\left(\frac{1}{2}-x\right)^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=(x-1)\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}c_{k}\left(2k\left(\frac{1}{2}-x\right)^{k}-k(2kx+1)\left(\frac{1}{2}-x\right)^{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}(x-1)kc_{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}-x\right)^{k-1}(1-2x-2kx-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}2k(k+1)c_{k}x(1-x)\left(\frac{1}{2}-x\right)^{k-1}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle xs^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=x\frac{d}{dx}\left(c_{0}+\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}c_{k}(2k+1-2kx)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=x\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}c_{k}\left(-2k\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}+k(2k+1-2kx)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}xkc_{k}\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}(1-2x+2k+1-2kx)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0\text{ odd}}2k(k+1)c_{k}x(1-x)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}=(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x),

as desired.
∎

Lemma A.5.1
.

The only infinitely differentiable scoring rules that are weakly proper but not proper are constant functions.

Proof.

Let
s
be an infinitely differentiable scoring rule that is weakly proper but not proper. Recall that in the proof of Lemma
3.2.4
, we showed that for all
p
, the function
r_{p}(x)
weakly increases on
(0,p]
and weakly decreases on
[p,1)
. Since
s
is not proper, there is some
p
such that
r_{p}(x)
does not strictly increase on
(0,p]
or does not strictly decrease on
[p,1)
. But this means that
r_{p}(x)
is constant on some open interval, which means that
r_{p}(x)
is constant (because
s
is infinitely differentiable, which means that
r_{p}(x)
is also infinitely differentiable). Thus, for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
we have that
ps(x)+(1-p)s(1-x)=c
for all
x
. Taking the derivative, we have that
ps^{\prime}(x)=(1-p)s^{\prime}(1-x)
for all
x
. But we also have that
xs^{\prime}(x)=(1-x)s^{\prime}(1-x)
for all
x
. The only way for both of these equations to hold is for
s^{\prime}(x)
to be uniformly zero, so
s
is indeed constant.
∎

See
3.6.3

Proof.

By Theorem
3.6.2
,
s
is a proper scoring rule if and only if
s
is nonconstant,
s^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
everywhere, and

s(x)=c_{0}+\sum_{k>0\text{\emph{ odd}}}c_{k}(2k+1-2kx)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}

for some
c_{0},c_{1},c_{3},c_{5},\dots\in\mathbb{R}
. Equivalently,

\displaystyle s^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0\text{\emph{ odd}}}kc_{k}(2k+1-2kx)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}-2kc_{k}\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0\text{\emph{ odd}}}kc_{k}\left(2k+1-2kx-2\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0\text{\emph{ odd}}}2kc_{k}(k+1)(1-x)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0\text{\emph{ even}}}2(k+1)c_{k+1}(k+2)(1-x)\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}=(1-x)\sum_{k\geq 0\text{\emph{ even}}}d_{k}\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k},

where
d_{k}=2(k+1)(k+2)c_{k+1}
. Noting that
s
is constant if and only if
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
is uniformly zero, and that
s^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
if and only if
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq 0
, this completes the proof.
∎

In proving Theorem
3.6.1
, we will make substantial use of
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
, the second derivative of the expected score function of
s
. For convenience, we will write
\varphi
instead of
G_{s}^{\prime\prime}
.

Note that if
s
is a continuously differentiable (but not necessarily infinitely differentiable) proper scoring rule, then we have

\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s)=2\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx.

Also, note that
s
is normalized if and only if
s\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)=0
and (by Claim
3.2.9
and Remark
3.2.6
)

\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)s^{\prime}(x)dx=\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}\varphi(x)dx=1.

See
3.6.1

Proof.

The Weierstrass approximation theorem says that any continuous function can be uniformly approximated by polynomials on a closed interval. A constructive proof of this theorem (for the interval
[0,1]
) is given by the Bernstein polynomials:
b_{i,n}(x)=\binom{n}{i}x^{i}(1-x)^{n-i}
. Given a continuous function
\psi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, define

B_{n}(\psi)(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\psi\left(\frac{i}{n}\right)b_{i,n}(x).

Then the polynomials
B_{n}(\psi)
converge uniformly to
\psi

(
Estep 1998a
, §36)
. Suppose that
\psi
also satisfies
\psi(x)=\psi(1-x)
. Then
\psi\left(\frac{i}{n}\right)=\psi\left(\frac{n-i}{n}\right)
, which means
B_{n}(\psi)
can be written as a linear combination of polynomials
(b_{i,n}+b_{n-i,n})(x)
. These polynomials are equal at
x
and
1-x
, and thus
B_{n}(\psi)(x)=B_{n}(\psi)(1-x)
. From this we conclude that
\psi
can be uniformly approximated on
[0,1]
by a sequence polynomials
p_{i}
that satisfy
p_{i}(x)=p_{i}(1-x)
.

Let
\varphi_{\ell}
be the
\varphi
corresponding to
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
. Recall that

\varphi_{\ell}(x)=\begin{cases}\kappa_{\ell}(x^{\ell-8}(1-x)^{\ell})^{1/(\ell+4)}&x\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
\kappa_{\ell}(x^{\ell}(1-x)^{\ell-8})^{1/(\ell+4)}&x\geq\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

(A.4)

Let
0<\epsilon<\min(\frac{1}{2},\varphi_{\ell}(\frac{1}{2}))
. Consider the following function
\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
.

\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)=\begin{cases}\varphi_{\ell}(\epsilon)&x\leq\epsilon\\
\varphi_{\ell}(x)&\epsilon\leq x\leq 1-\epsilon\\
\varphi_{\ell}(1-\epsilon)&x\geq 1-\epsilon.\end{cases}

Observe that
\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)=\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(1-x)
for all
x\in[0,1]
; this is a straightforward consequence of the fact that
\varphi_{\ell}
is symmetric about
\frac{1}{2}
. Per our discussion above, there exists a polynomial
p_{\epsilon}
satisfying
p_{\epsilon}(x)=p_{\epsilon}(1-x)
such that for all
x\in[0,1]
,
\left\lvert p_{\epsilon}(x)-\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon
. In particular, we take
p_{\epsilon}=B_{n(\epsilon)}(\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon})
, where
n(\epsilon)
is any
n
large enough that
p_{\epsilon}
is uniformly within
\epsilon
of
\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}
.

Observe that such a polynomial, when written as a sum of powers of
x-\frac{1}{2}
, must only contain even powers of
x-\frac{1}{2}
, since
p_{\epsilon}(x)-p_{\epsilon}(1-x)
must be the zero polynomial. Consequently, by Lemma
3.6.3
,
(1-x)p_{\epsilon}(x)
is the derivative of a proper scoring rule.
3
3

3

The fact that
p_{\epsilon}
is nonnegative everywhere follows from the fact that it is a uniform
\epsilon
-approximation of
\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}
, which is greater than
1
on
[0,1]
.
To find the associated normalized proper scoring rule (call it
s_{\epsilon}
), we take the antiderivative (taking the constant coefficient in the
\left(x-\frac{1}{2}\right)
-expansion to be zero), and divide by
\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}p_{\epsilon}(x)dx
. Thus, corresponding to each
\epsilon
we have a normalized polynomial scoring rule
s_{\epsilon}
with incentivization index

\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\epsilon})=2\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{p_{\epsilon}(x)\cdot\frac{1}{\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}p_{\epsilon}(x)dx}}\right)^{\ell/4}dx=2\left(\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}p_{\epsilon}(x)dx\right)^{\ell/4}\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{p_{\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx.

Claim A.5.2
.

s_{\epsilon}
is respectful.

Proof.

Since
s_{\epsilon}
is polynomial (and thus bounded and infinitely differentiable), it suffices to show that the second derivative of its expected score function is bounded away from zero. The second derivative of
s_{\epsilon}
’s expected score function is a positive multiple of
p_{\epsilon}
, so it suffices to show that
p_{\epsilon}
is bounded away from zero. This is indeed the case. To see this, note that
\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}
is bounded away from zero (as
\varphi_{\ell}
is bounded away from zero on
[\epsilon,1-\epsilon]
); let
L>0
be such that
\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)\geq L
on
[0,1]
. Then

p_{\epsilon}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n(\epsilon)}\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}\left(\frac{i}{n(\epsilon)}\right)\binom{n(\epsilon)}{i}x^{i}(1-x)^{n(\epsilon)-i}\geq\sum_{i=0}^{n(\epsilon)}L\binom{n(\epsilon)}{i}x^{i}(1-x)^{n(\epsilon)-i}=L.

∎

Our goal is to upper bound
\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\epsilon})
in a way that shows that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\epsilon})=\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\ell,\text{Opt}})
. To do this, it suffices to show that the first of the two integrals in our formula for
s_{\epsilon}
converges to
1
as
\epsilon\to 0
and that twice the second integral converges to
\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\ell},\text{Opt})
as
\epsilon\to 0
. We begin by working with the first of the two integrals.

Claim A.5.3
.

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}p_{\epsilon}(x)dx\leq 1.

Proof.

First observe that, since
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
is normalized, we have
\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}\varphi_{\ell}(x)dx=1
. Next, note that for
1\leq\ell\leq 8
,
\varphi_{\ell}
is increasing on
[\frac{1}{2},1)
(as is evident from Equation
A.4
), which means that

\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)dx\leq\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}\varphi_{\ell}(x)dx=1.\qquad(1\leq\ell\leq 8)

On the other hand, for
\ell>8
, observe that
\varphi_{\ell}
is bounded above on
[\frac{1}{2},1)
, say by a constant
M_{\ell}
, which means that in this case

\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)dx\leq\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}\varphi_{\ell}(x)dx+\epsilon M_{\ell}=1+\epsilon M_{\ell}.\qquad(\ell>8)

Now, we have

\left(\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}p_{\epsilon}(x)dx\right)^{\ell/4}\leq\left(\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}(\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)+\epsilon)dx\right)^{\ell/4}\leq\left(\epsilon+\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}(1-x)^{2}\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)dx\right)^{\ell/4}

which is at most
(1+\epsilon)^{\ell/4}
(for
\ell\leq 8
) and at most
(1+(M_{\ell}+1)\epsilon)^{\ell/4}
(for
\ell>8
).
∎

Next we work with the second integral.

Claim A.5.4
.

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}2\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{p_{\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx\leq I(s_{\ell},\text{Opt}).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{p_{\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)-\epsilon}\right)^{\ell/4}dx=\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}\right)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\max_{x\in[\frac{1}{2},1]}\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx.

Let us consider
\max_{x\in[\frac{1}{2},1]}\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}
as a function of
\epsilon
. For
\ell\leq 8
, since
\varphi_{\ell}
is increasing on
[\frac{1}{2},1)
, this is just
\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi_{\ell}(\frac{1}{2})}
, a quantity that approaches zero as
\epsilon
approaches zero.

For
\ell>8
, as
\epsilon
approaches zero we have that
\min_{x\in[\frac{1}{2},1]}\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)
approaches
\kappa_{\ell}\epsilon^{(\ell-8)/(\ell+4)}=\omega(\epsilon)
. This means that
\max_{x\in[\frac{1}{2},1]}\frac{\epsilon}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}
approaches zero as
\epsilon
approaches zero.

Therefore, we have

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}2\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{p_{\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx\leq\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}2\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}.

Next, note that for
x\in[1-\epsilon,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell}(1-\epsilon)}\right)^{\ell/4}\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon(1-\epsilon)}{\varphi_{\ell}(1-\epsilon)}\right)^{\ell/4}=\left(\frac{\epsilon(1-\epsilon)}{\kappa_{\ell}((1-\epsilon)^{\ell}\epsilon^{\ell-8})^{1/(\ell+4)}}\right)^{\ell/4}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\epsilon^{3}(1-\epsilon))^{\ell/(\ell+4)}}{\kappa_{\ell}^{\ell/4}}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa_{\ell}^{\ell/4}}.

Therefore we have

\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx\leq\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}dx+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa_{\ell}^{\ell/4}}=\frac{1}{2}\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\ell,\text{Opt}})+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa_{\ell}^{\ell/4}},

so

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}2\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{1}\left(\frac{x(1-x)}{\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}(x)}\right)^{\ell/4}\leq\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}).

∎

It therefore follows that
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\epsilon})\leq\text{Ind}^{\ell}(s_{\ell,\text{Opt}})
. But in fact, the inequality is an equality; this is because no continuously differentiable function has incentivization index less than that of
s_{\ell,\text{Opt}}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
3.6.1
.
∎

. “Predicted average error” means the error that Theorem
3.3.3
predicts in the limit as

approaches
0
(but multiplied by
c^{-1/4}
in the stated value of

). “Ratio” refers to the ratio between the average error and the predicted average error. “Maximum number of flips” refers to the maximum number of flips for that cost and rule in the
100,000
simulations. (Note that because of the large number of simulations and comparatively small number of flips, these are likely to be universal upper bounds on the number of flips given that cost and rule.)

Appendix B
Details omitted from Chapter
5

B.1
Details omitted from Section
5.4

See
5.4.5

Proof.

We apply Theorem 3.1 of
Hazan 2023a
; this theorem tells us that in order to prove the stated bound, it suffices to show that for all
t
and
\mathbf{w}
,
\left\lVert\nabla L^{t}(\mathbf{w})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{2m}M
.

Let
L
be an arbitrary loss function, i.e.
L(\mathbf{w})=-\text{WS}_{j}(\mathbf{w})
for some
j,\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{m}
. Let
\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})=\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}\limits_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i})
. We claim that

\nabla L(\mathbf{w})=\begin{pmatrix}\text{--- }\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\text{ ---}\\
\vdots\\
\text{--- }\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{m})\text{ ---}\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})-\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}),

(B.1)

where this
m
-dimensional vector should be interpreted modulo translation by
\mathbf{1}_{m}
(see Remark
5.2.2
). To see this, observe that

\nabla L(\mathbf{w})=-\nabla WS_{j}(\mathbf{w})=-\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}s(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w});j)=-\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\left(G(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w}))+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\right),

where
\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}
denotes the gradient with respect to change in the weight vector
\mathbf{w}
(as opposed to change in the probability vector). Now, by the chain rule for gradients, we have

\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}G(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w}))=(J_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(\mathbf{w}))^{\top}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})),

where
J_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}
denotes the Jacobian matrix of the function
\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})
. Also, we have

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w}))=\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),

so (again by the chain rule) we have

\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}(\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{m})\end{pmatrix}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w}))-(J_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(\mathbf{w}))^{\top}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})).

This gives us Equation
B.1
.
1
1

1

Note that the cancellation of the Jacobian terms stems not from the specific relationship between
\mathbf{p}^{*}
and
\mathbf{w}
but from the nature of proper scoring rules. We obtain the same cancellation if we consider
\nabla s(\mathbf{p};j)
, where after differentiating
G(\mathbf{p})+\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}),\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}-\mathbf{p}\right\rangle
we find that the
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})
terms cancel.
Now, for any
i
, we have

\left\lvert\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})-\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})-\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}M.

Therefore,

\left\lVert\nabla L(\mathbf{w})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{m\cdot\left(\sqrt{2}M\right)^{2}}=\sqrt{2m}M,

completing the proof.
∎

B.2
Details omitted from Section
5.6

We claim that our axioms in Definition
5.6.2
can be restated equivalently in a form similar to that of Kolmogorov introduced at the top of Section
5.6
(though with weights.)

Claim B.2.1
.

Given a pooling operator
\oplus
on
\mathcal{D}
satisfying Definition
5.6.2
, the function
M
defined on arbitrary tuples of weighted forecasts defined by
M(\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{m}):=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\Pi_{i}
satisfies the following axioms:

(1)

M(\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{m})
is strictly increasing in each
\text{pr}(\Pi_{i})
and continuous in its inputs.
2
2

2

That is, it is a continuous function of its input in
\mathcal{D}^{m}\times(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}\setminus\mathbf{0})
, where weighted forecasts with weight
0
are ignored when computing
M
.

(2)

M
is symmetric in its arguments.

(3)

M((p,w_{1}),\dots,(p,w_{m}))=(p,\sum_{i}w_{i})
.

(4)

M(\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{k},\Pi_{k+1},\dots,\Pi_{m})=M(\Pi^{\prime},\Pi_{k+1},\dots,\Pi_{m})
, where
y:=M(\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{k})
.

(5)

M((p_{1},w_{1}),\dots,(p_{m},w_{m}))
has weight
w_{1}+\dots+w_{m}
.

Additionally, given any
M
defined on arbitrary tuples of weighted forecasts, the operator
\oplus
defined by
\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}:=M(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2})
satisfies Definition
5.6.2
.

Proof.

We first prove that given
\oplus
satisfying Definition
5.6.2
,
M
satisfies the stated axioms. The last four axioms are clear, so we prove the first one. The fact that
M
is strictly increasing in each probability follows immediately by considering the continuous, strictly increasing function
g
such that
\oplus=\oplus_{g}
, which exists by Theorem
5.6.6
. Continuity likewise follows, since the quantity in Definition
5.6.2
is continuous.

We now prove that given
M
satisfying the stated axioms,
\oplus
satisfies Definition
5.6.2
. Weight additivity, commutativity, continuity, and idempotence are clear. To prove associativity, note that

\Pi_{1}\oplus(\Pi_{2}\oplus\Pi_{3})=M(\Pi_{1},M(\Pi_{2},\Pi_{3}))=M(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\Pi_{3})=M(M(\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}),\Pi_{3})=(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})\oplus\Pi_{3}.

To prove monotonicity, let
p_{1}>p_{2}
and
w>x>y
. We wish to prove that
\text{pr}((p_{1},x)\oplus(p_{2},w-x))>\text{pr}((p_{1},y)\oplus(p_{2},w-y))
. We have

\displaystyle\text{pr}((p_{1},x)\oplus(p_{2},w-x))

\displaystyle=\text{pr}(M((p_{1},x),(p_{2},w-x)))=\text{pr}(M((p_{1},y),(p_{1},x-y),(p_{2},w-x)))

\displaystyle>\text{pr}(M((p_{1},y),(p_{2},x-y),(p_{2},w-x)))=\text{pr}(M((p_{1},y),(p_{2},w-y)))

\displaystyle=\text{pr}((p_{1},y)\oplus(p_{2},w-y)).

∎

B.2.1
Extending the results of Section
5.6
to
n>2
outcomes

We now discuss extending our axiomatization to arbitrary values of
n
in a way that, again, describes the class of QA pooling operators. Just as we fixed a two-outcome forecast domain
\mathcal{D}
in Section
5.6
, we now fix an
n
-outcome forecast domain
\mathcal{D}
for any
n\geq 2
. Our definition of weighted forecasts remains the same (except that now
\text{pr}(\Pi)
is a vector). Our definition of quasi-arithmetic pooling, however, needs to change to make
\mathbf{g}
vector-valued. This raises the question: what is the analogue of “increasing” for vector-valued functions? It turns out that the relevant notion for us is
cyclical monotonicity
, introduced by
Rockafellar 1970c
(see also
Rockafellar 1970b
). We will define this notion shortly, but first we give the definition of quasi-arithmetic pooling with arbitrary weights (analogous to Definition
5.6.2
) for this setting. Throughout this section, we will use the notation
H_{n}(c):=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\sum_{i}x_{i}=c\}
. Recall from Remark
5.2.2
that the range of the gradient of a function defined on
\mathcal{D}
is a subset of
H_{n}(0)
.

Definition B.2.2

(Quasi-arithmetic pooling with arbitrary weights)
.

Given a continuous, strictly cyclically monotone vector-valued function
\mathbf{g}:\mathcal{D}\to H_{n}(0)
whose range is a convex set, and weighted forecasts
\Pi_{1}=(\mathbf{p}_{1},w_{1}),\dots,\Pi_{m}=(\mathbf{p}_{m},w_{m})
, define the
quasi-arithmetic pool
of
\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{m}
with respect to
\mathbf{g}
as

\sideset{}{{}_{\mathbf{g}}}{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i}):=\left(\mathbf{g}^{-1}\left(\frac{\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}\right),\sum_{i}w_{i}\right).

Note that QA pooling as defined in Definition
5.2.5
can be written in the form of Definition
B.2.2
if and only if the scoring rule has convex exposure; if it does not, then for some choices of parameters,
\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})
will be equal to a subgradient – but not the gradient – of
G
at some point.

Definition B.2.3

(Cyclical monotonicity)
.

A function
\mathbf{g}:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
cyclically monotone
if for every list of points
\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{k-1},\mathbf{x}_{k}=\mathbf{x}_{0}\in U
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}_{i}),\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i-1}\right\rangle\geq 0.

We also say that
\mathbf{g}
is
strictly cyclically monotone
if the inequality is strict except when
\mathbf{x}_{0}=\dots=\mathbf{x}_{k-1}
.

To gain an intuition for this notion, consider the case of
k=2
; then this condition says that
\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}_{0}),\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0}\right\rangle\geq 0
. In other words, the change in
\mathbf{g}
from
\mathbf{x}_{0}
to
\mathbf{x}_{1}
is in the same general direction as the direction from
\mathbf{x}_{0}
to
\mathbf{x}_{1}
. This property is called
2-cycle
(or
weak
)
monotonicity
.

Cyclical monotonicity is a stronger notion, and has applications to mechanism design and revealed preference theory (see e.g.
Lavi & Swamy 2007a
;
Ashlagi et al. 2010a
;
Frongillo & Kash 2014a
;
Vohra 2007a
). In such settings, it is usually the case that two-cycle and cyclical monotonicity are equivalent. Indeed, Saks and Yu showed that these conditions are equivalent in settings where the set of outcomes (i.e. the range of
\mathbf{g}
) is finite
(
Saks & Yu 2005a
)
. However, cyclical monotonicity is substantially stronger than two-cycle monotonicity when the range of
\mathbf{g}
is infinitely large, as in our setting. In fact, the difference between these two conditions is that a two-cycle monotone function is cyclically monotone if and only if it is also
vortex-free

(
Archer & Kleinberg 2014a
, Theorem 3.9)
.
Vortex-freeness
means that the path integral of
\mathbf{g}
along any triangle vanishes. See
Archer & Kleinberg 2014a
for a detailed comparison of these two notions.

The immediately relevant fact for us is that cyclically monotone functions are gradients of convex functions (and vice versa). Speaking more precisely:

Theorem B.2.4
.

A vector-valued function
\mathbf{g}
is continuous and strictly cyclically monotone if and only if it is the gradient of a differentiable, strictly convex function
G
.

Proof.

Per a theorem of Rockafellar (
Rockafellar 1970c
, see also
Rockafellar 1970b
), a function
\mathbf{g}
is cyclically monotone if and only if it is a subgradient of a convex function
G
. The proof of this fact shows just as easily that a function is strictly cyclically monotone if and only if it is a subgradient of a strictly convex function.

Consider a differentiable, strictly convex function
G
. Its gradient is continuous (see
Rockafellar 1970b
). Conversely, consider a continuous, strictly cyclically monotone vector-valued function
\mathbf{g}
. As we just discussed, it is a subgradient of some strictly convex function
G
. A convex function with a continuous subgradient is differentiable
(
Bauschke & Combettes 2011a
, Proposition 17.41)
.
∎

This means that the conditions on
\mathbf{g}
in Definition
B.2.2
are precisely those necessary to let
\mathbf{g}
be any function that it could be in our original definition of quasi-arithmetic pooling (Definition
5.2.5
). Our new definition is thus equivalent to the old one (after normalizing weights to add to
1
).

We now discuss our axioms for pooling operators that will again capture the class of QA pooling operators. We will keep the weight additivity, commutativity, associativity, and idempotence verbatim from our discussion of the
n=2
case. We will slightly strengthen the continuity argument (see below).

We will also add a new axiom,
subtraction
, which states that if
\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}=\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{3}
then
\Pi_{2}=\Pi_{3}
. Subtraction in the
n=2
case follows from monotonicity; in this case, however, we the subtraction axiom will help us state the monotonicity axiom. In particular, it allows us to make the following definition, which essentially extends the notion of pooling to allow for negative weights.

Definition B.2.5
.

Let
\oplus
be a pooling operator satisfying weight additivity, commutativity, associativity, and subtraction. Fix
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{k}\in\mathcal{D}
. Define a function
\mathbf{p}:\Delta_{k}\to\mathcal{D}
(with
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{k}
serving as implicit arguments) defined by

\mathbf{p}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=\text{pr}\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i})\right).

We extend the definition of
\mathbf{p}
to a partial function on
H_{k}(1)
, as follows: given input
(w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
, let
S\subseteq[k]
be the set of indices
i
such that
w_{i}<0
and
T\subseteq[k]
be the set of indices
i
such that
w_{i}>0
. We define
\mathbf{p}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
to be the
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{D}
such that

(\mathbf{q},1)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i\in S}(\mathbf{p}_{i},-w_{i})\right)=\bigoplus_{i\in T}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i}).

Note that
\mathbf{q}
is not guaranteed to exist, which is why we call
\mathbf{p}
a partial function. However, if
\mathbf{q}
exists then it is unique, by the subtraction axiom.

We can now state the full axiomatization, including the monotonicity axiom.

Definition B.2.6

(Axioms for pooling operators)
.

For a pooling operator
\oplus
on
\mathcal{D}
, we define the following axioms.

1.

Weight additivity
:
\text{wt}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=\text{wt}(\Pi_{1})+\text{wt}(\Pi_{2})
for every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
.

2.

Commutativity
:
\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}=\Pi_{2}\oplus\Pi_{1}
for every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
.

3.

Associativity
:
\Pi_{1}\oplus(\Pi_{2}\oplus\Pi_{3})=(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})\oplus\Pi_{3}
for every
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2},\Pi_{3}
.

4.

Continuity
: For every positive integer
k
and
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{k}
, the quantity
3
3

3

The continuity axiom is only well-defined conditioned on
\oplus
being associative, which is fine for our purposes. We allow a proper subset of weights to be zero by defining the aggregate to ignore forecasts with weight zero.

\text{pr}\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i})\right)

is a continuous function of
(w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

5.

Idempotence
: For every
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
, if
\text{pr}(\Pi_{1})=\text{pr}(\Pi_{2})
then
\text{pr}(\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2})=\text{pr}(\Pi_{1})
.

6.

Subtraction
: If
\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{2}=\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{3}
then
\Pi_{2}=\Pi_{3}
.

7.

Monotonicity
: There exist vectors
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathbf{p}
(as in Definition
B.2.5
) is a strictly cyclically monotone function from its domain to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

This monotonicity axiom essentially extends our previous monotonicity axiom (in Definition
5.6.5
) to a multi-dimensional setting. It states that there are
n
“anchor points” in
\mathcal{D}
such that the function
\mathbf{p}
from weight vectors to
\mathcal{D}
that pools the anchor points with the weights given as input obeys a notion of monotonicity (namely cyclical monotonicity). Informally, this means that the vector of weights that one would need to give to the anchor points in order to arrive at a forecast
\mathbf{p}
“correlates” with the forecast
\mathbf{p}
itself.

We now state the main theorem of our axiomatization.

Theorem B.2.7
.

A pooling operator satisfies the axioms in Definition
B.2.6
if and only if it is a QA pooling operator as in Definition
B.2.2
.
4
4

4

Recall that Definition
B.2.2
is narrower than Definition
5.2.5
, since it excludes QA pools with respect to scoring rules that do not have convex exposure. Without convex exposure, the associativity, subtraction, and monotonicity axioms may be violated. For example, consider
\Pi_{i}=(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{i},1)
for
i=1,2,3
, for the scoring rule with expected score function
G(\mathbf{x})=x_{1}^{4}+x_{2}^{4}+x_{3}^{4}
. We have that
(\Pi_{1}\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}\Pi_{2})\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}\Pi_{3}=((1/2,1/2,0),2)\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{3},1)\approx((0.182,0.182,0.635),3)
, whereas
\Pi_{1}\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}(\Pi_{2}\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}\Pi_{3})=((0.635,0.182,0.182),3)
.

Proof.

We begin by noting the following fact, which follows from results in
Rockafellar 1970b
.

Proposition B.2.8
.

A strictly cyclically monotone function
\mathbf{g}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is injective, and its inverse
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
is strictly cyclically monotone and continuous.
5
5

5

Why can’t we apply this result again to
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
to conclude that
\mathbf{g}
is continuous, even though we did not assume it to be? The reason is that the proof of continuity relies on the convexity of
\mathcal{D}
; if
\mathbf{g}
is discontinuous then the domain of
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
may not be convex (or even connected), so we cannot apply the result to
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
.

We provide a partial proof below; it relies on the following observation.

Remark B.2.9
.

We can instead write the condition as

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}_{i})-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}_{i-1}),\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rangle\geq 0.

This is equivalent to the condition in Definition
B.2.3
, because it is the same statement (with rearranged terms) when the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
’s are listed in reverse order.

Proof.

First, suppose that
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{y})
. Then

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})+\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{y})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})=0.

Since
\mathbf{g}
is
strictly
cyclically monotone, this implies that
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}
. (Note that we only use two-cycle monotonicity.)

We now show that
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
is strictly cyclically monotone. That is, we wish to show that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{g}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}_{i})-\mathbf{g}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}_{i-1})\right\rangle>0

for any
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{k}=\mathbf{x}_{0}
that are not all the same. (See Remark
B.2.9
.) By the cyclical monotonicity of
\mathbf{g}
, we have that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i}),\mathbf{p}_{i}-\mathbf{p}_{i-1}\right\rangle>0

(the strictness of the inequality follows by the injectivity of
\mathbf{g}
: if
\mathbf{x}_{i}\neq\mathbf{x}_{j}
then
\mathbf{p}_{i}\neq\mathbf{p}_{j}
). This means that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{g}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}_{i})-\mathbf{g}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}_{i-1})\right\rangle>0,

as desired. As for continuity, we defer to
Rockafellar 1970b
.
∎

Back to the proof of Theorem
B.2.7
, we first prove that any such
\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}
satisfies the stated axioms. Weight additivity, commutativity, associativity, and idempotence are clear. Continuity follows from the formula

\text{pr}((\mathbf{p}_{1},w_{1})\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{p}_{2},w_{2}))=\mathbf{g}^{-1}\left(\frac{w_{1}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{1})+w_{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{2})}{w_{1}+w_{2}}\right),

noting that
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
is continuous by Proposition
B.2.8
. Likewise, subtraction follows from the fact that
\mathbf{g}
is injective (by Proposition
B.2.8
), as is
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
(likewise). Monotonicity remains.

The range of
\mathbf{g}
contains an open subset
6
6

6

This follows from the invariance of domain theorem, which states that the image of an open subset of a manifold under an injective continuous map is open.
of
H_{n}(0)
, so in particular it contains the vertices of some translated and dilated copy of the standard simplex. That is, there are
n
points
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n}
in the range of
\mathbf{g}
for which there is a positive scalar
a
and vector
\mathbf{b}
such that
a\boldsymbol{\delta}_{i}+\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{x}_{i}
for every
i
. (Here
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{i}
is the
i
-th standard basis vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.) We will let
\mathbf{p}_{i}
be the pre-image of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
under
\mathbf{g}
, so that
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})=a\boldsymbol{\delta}_{i}+\mathbf{b}
.

Observe that for any
\mathbf{w}
in the domain of
\mathbf{p}
, we have

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p}_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(a\boldsymbol{\delta}_{i}+\mathbf{b})=a\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b},

so

\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{g}^{-1}(a\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b}).

We have that
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
is strictly cyclically monotone (by Proposition
B.2.8
), and it is easy to verify that for any strictly cyclically monotone function
\mathbf{f}
and any
a>0
and
\mathbf{b}
,
\mathbf{f}(a\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b})
is a strictly cyclically monotone function of
\mathbf{x}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{g}^{-1}(a\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{b})
is strictly cyclically monotone, as desired.

Now we prove the converse. Assume that we have a pooling operator
\oplus
satisfying the axioms in Definition
B.2.6
. We wish to show that
\oplus
is
\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}
for some
\mathbf{g}:\mathcal{D}\to H_{n}(0)
.

For the remainder of this proof, let
\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{n}
be vectors certifying the monotonicity of
\oplus
, and let
\mathbf{p}(\cdot)
be as in Definition
B.2.5
.

For any
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{D}
, let
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}):=\mathbf{w}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}
, where
\mathbf{w}\in H_{n}(1)
is such that
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{1}_{n}
is the all-ones vector. This raises the question of well-definedness: does this
\mathbf{w}
necessarily exist, and if so, is it unique? The following claim shows that this is indeed the case.

Claim B.2.10
.

The function
\mathbf{p}
, from the subset of
H_{n}(1)
where it is defined to
\mathcal{D}
, is bijective.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathbf{p}
is injective follows from the fact that it is strictly cyclically monotone (see Proposition
B.2.8
). We now show that
\mathbf{p}
is surjective.

Let
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{D}
. Define the function
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}:\Delta_{n+1}\to\mathcal{D}
by

\tilde{\mathbf{p}}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n+1}):=\text{pr}\left(\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{i})\right)\oplus(\mathbf{q},w_{n+1})\right).

Since
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}
is a continuous map
7
7

7

By the continuity axiom; here we use the more generalized form we stated earlier.
from
\Delta_{n+1}
(an
n
-dimensional manifold) to
\mathcal{D}
(an
(n-1)
-dimensional manifold),
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}
is not injective.
8
8

8

This follows e.g. from the Borsuk-Ulam theorem.
So in particular, let
\mathbf{w}_{1}\neq\mathbf{w}_{2}\in\Delta_{n+1}
be such that
\mathbf{\tilde{p}}(\mathbf{w}_{1})=\mathbf{\tilde{p}}(\mathbf{w}_{2})
. That is, we have

\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{1,i})\right)\oplus(\mathbf{q},w_{1,n+1})=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{2,i})\right)\oplus(\mathbf{q},w_{2,n+1}).

(B.2)

Observe that
w_{1,n+1}\neq w_{2,n+2}
; for otherwise it would follows from the subtraction axiom that two different combinations of the
\mathbf{p}_{i}
’s would give the same probability, contradicting the fact that
\mathbf{p}
is injective. Without loss of generality, assume that
w_{1,n+1}>w_{2,n+1}
. We can rearrange the terms in Equation
B.2
to look as follows.

(\mathbf{q},w_{1,n+1}-w_{2,n+1})\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i\in S}(\mathbf{p}_{i},v_{i})\right)=\bigoplus_{i\in T\subseteq[n]\setminus S}(\mathbf{p}_{i},v_{i})

for some positive
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. By the distributive property, we may multiply all weights by
\frac{1}{w_{1,n+1}-w_{2,n+1}}
. The result will be an equation as in Definition
B.2.5
, certifying that
\mathbf{q}
is in the range of the function
\mathbf{p}
, as desired.
∎

We return to our main proof, now that we have shown that our function
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}):=\mathbf{w}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}
, where
\mathbf{w}\in H_{n}(1)
is such that
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{q}
, is well-defined. In fact, we can simply write
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q})=\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{q})-\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}
. (The vector
\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}
is fairly arbitrary; it only serves the purpose of forcing the range of
\mathbf{g}
to lie in
H_{n}(0)
instead of
H_{n}(1)
.)

We first show that the equation that defines
\oplus_{\mathbf{g}}
holds – that is, that if
(\mathbf{q}_{1},v_{1})\oplus(\mathbf{q}_{2},v_{2})=(\mathbf{q},v_{1}+v_{2})
(with
v_{1},v_{2}\geq 0
, not both zero), then

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q})=\frac{v_{1}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}_{1})+v_{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}_{2})}{v_{1}+v_{2}}.

Let
\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{2}\in H_{n}(1)
be such that
\mathbf{q}_{1}=\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w}_{1})
and
\mathbf{q}_{2}=\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w}_{2})
. It is intuitive that
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{p}\left(\frac{v_{1}\mathbf{w_{1}}+v_{2}\mathbf{w_{2}}}{v_{1}+v_{2}}\right)
, but we show this formally.

Claim B.2.11
.

Given
\mathbf{q}_{1},\mathbf{q}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
with
\mathbf{q}_{1}=\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w}_{1}),\mathbf{q}_{2}=\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w}_{2})
, and
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, we have

\mathbf{p}(\alpha\mathbf{w}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{w}_{2})=(\mathbf{q}_{1},\alpha)\oplus(\mathbf{q}_{2},1-\alpha).

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{q}_{1},1)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i:w_{1,i}<0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{1,i})\right)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i:w_{1,i}>0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{1,i})

\displaystyle(\mathbf{q}_{2},1)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i:w_{2,i}<0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{2,i})\right)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i:w_{2,i}>0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},w_{2,i}).

Applying the distributive property to the two above equations with constants
\alpha
and
1-\alpha
, respectively, and adding them, we get that

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{q}_{1},\alpha\right)\oplus\left(\mathbf{q}_{2},1-\alpha\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i:w_{1,i}<0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},\alpha w_{1,i})\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i:w_{2,i}<0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},(1-\alpha)w_{2,i})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i:w_{1,i}>0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},\alpha w_{1,i})\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i:w_{2,i}>0}(\mathbf{p}_{i},(1-\alpha)w_{2,i})\right).

We have that
\left(\mathbf{q}_{1},\alpha\right)\oplus\left(\mathbf{q}_{2},1-\alpha\right)=(\mathbf{q},1)
. It follows (after rearranging terms, from Definition
B.2.5
) that
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{p}\left(\frac{v_{1}\mathbf{w_{1}}+v_{2}\mathbf{w_{2}}}{v_{1}+v_{2}}\right)
.
∎

Applying Claim
B.2.11
with
\alpha=\frac{v_{1}}{v_{1}+v_{2}}
, we find that

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q})=\frac{v_{1}\mathbf{w_{1}}+v_{2}\mathbf{w_{2}}}{v_{1}+v_{2}}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}=\frac{v_{1}\left(\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}_{1})+\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}\right)+v_{2}\left(\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}_{2})+\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}\right)}{v_{1}+v_{2}}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}=\frac{v_{1}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}_{1})+v_{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}_{2})}{v_{1}+v_{2}},

as desired.

It remains to show that
\mathbf{g}
is continuous, strictly cyclically monotone, and has convex range. By the monotonicity axiom,
\mathbf{p}
is strictly cyclically monotone. It follows by Proposition
B.2.8
that its inverse its continuous and strictly cyclically monotone. Therefore,
\mathbf{g}
is continuous and cyclically monotone (as it is simply a translation of
\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\mathbf{q})
by
\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}
).

Finally, to show that
\mathbf{g}
has convex range, we wish to show that
\mathbf{p}^{-1}
has convex range; or, in other words, that the domain on which
\mathbf{p}
is defined is convex. And indeed, this follows straightforwardly from Claim
B.2.11
. Let
\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{2}
be in the domain of
\mathbf{p}
, with
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w}_{1})=\mathbf{q}_{1},\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{w}_{2})=\mathbf{q}_{2}
. Then for any
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, we have that

\mathbf{p}(\alpha\mathbf{w}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{w}_{2})=(\mathbf{q}_{1},\alpha)\oplus(\mathbf{q}_{2},1-\alpha),

so in particular
\alpha\mathbf{w}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{w}_{2}
is in the domain of
\mathbf{p}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

B.3
The convex exposure property

Several of our results have been contingent on the convex exposure property. In this e-companion, we consider when the convex exposure property holds. Our first result is that it always holds in the case of a binary outcome (i.e.
n=2
).

Proposition B.3.1
.

If
n=2
, every (continuous) proper scoring rule has convex exposure.

Proof.

Consider a proper scoring rule
s
with forecast domain
\mathcal{D}
. Since
\mathcal{D}
is connected and
\mathbf{g}
is continuous on
\mathcal{D}
, the range of
\mathbf{g}
over
\mathcal{D}
is connected. In the
n=2
outcome case, the range of
\mathbf{g}
lies on the line
\{(x_{1},x_{2}):x_{1}+x_{2}=0\}
, and a connected subset of a line is convex.
∎

As we shall see, the convex exposure property holds for nearly all of the most commonly used scoring rules even in higher dimensions.

(A note on notation: in this section we use
p_{j}
instead of
p(j)
to refer to the
j
-th coordinate of a probability distribution
\mathbf{p}
.)

We now show that scoring rules that – like the logarithmic scoring rule – “go off to infinity” have convex exposure.

Proposition B.3.2
.

Let
s
be a proper scoring rule whose forecast domain is the interior of
\Delta_{n}
, such that for any point
\mathbf{x}
on the boundary of
\Delta_{n}
, and for any sequence
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\dots
converging to
\mathbf{x}
,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}=\infty
.
9
9

9

Abernethy et al. 2013a
say that
G
is a
pseudo-barrier function
if this condition is satisfied.
Then
s
has convex exposure.

This is a statement of convex analysis – namely that if
\left\lVert\mathbf{g}\right\rVert_{2}
approaches
\infty
on the boundary of a convex set, then the range of
\mathbf{g}
is convex (assuming
\mathbf{g}
is the gradient of a differentiable convex function). See
Rockafellar 1970b
for the proof. In non-pathological cases, the basic intuition is that
every

\mathbf{v}\in\{\mathbf{x}:\sum_{i}x_{i}=0\}
is the gradient of
G
at some point. In these cases,
\nabla G(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{v}
where
\mathbf{x}
minimizes
G(\mathbf{v})-\left\langle\mathbf{v},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle
; the
\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert g(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}=\infty
condition means that this minimum does not occur on the boundary of
\Delta_{n}
.

Corollary B.3.3
.

The following scoring rules have convex exposure:

•

The logarithmic scoring rule.

•

The scoring rule given by
G(\mathbf{p})=-\sum_{j}p_{j}^{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

•

The scoring rule given by
G(\mathbf{p})=-\sum_{j}\ln p_{j}
, which can be thought of as the limit of the
G
in the previous bullet point as
\gamma\to 0
.
10
10

10

This is a natural way to think of this scoring rule because
\nabla G(\mathbf{p})=-(p_{1}^{-1},\dots,p_{n}^{-1})
.

•

The scoring rule
hs
given by
G_{hs}(\mathbf{p})=-\prod_{j}p_{j}^{1/n}
.

The
hs
scoring rule

The last of these scoring rules is a generalization of the scoring rule
hs(q)=1-\sqrt{\frac{1-q}{q}}
used by
Ben-David & Blais 2020a
as part of proving their minimax theorem for randomized algorithms.
11
11

11

Here we are using the shorthand notation for the
n=2
outcome case discussed in Remark 3.5 of the main article.
The authors used this scoring rule as a key ingredient in their minimax theorem for randomized algorithms. The key property of the scoring rule was a result about its
amplification

(
Ben-David & Blais 2020a
, Lemma 3.10)
. The authors define a
forecasting algorithm
to be a generalization of a randomized algorithm that outputs an estimated
probability
that an output should be accepted. Then, roughly speaking, the authors show that given a forecasting algorithm
R
, it is possible to create a forecasting algorithm
R^{\prime}
that has a much larger expected score from the scoring rule
hs
by combining running
R
a small number of times and combining the outputs. This is an important new result in theoretical computer science and suggests that
hs
deserves more attention.

Since additive and multiplicative constants are irrelevant, we may treat
hs(q)=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1-q}{q}}
. Observe that (in the case of two outcomes), the expected score
G_{hs}
on a report of
q
is

G_{hs}(q)=q\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1-q}{q}}\right)+(1-q)\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1-q}{q}}\right)=-\sqrt{q(1-q)}.

That is,
G_{hs}
is precisely negative the geometric mean of
q
and
1-q
. This motivates us to generalize
hs
to a setting with
n
outcomes by setting

G_{hs}(\mathbf{p}):=-\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{1/n}.

It should not be obvious that this function is convex, but it turns out to be; this is the precise statement of an inequality known as Mahler’s inequality
(
Wikipedia contributors 2018a
)
.

Next we note that the quadratic scoring rule has convex exposure, since its exposure function
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})=2\mathbf{p}
(modulo
\mathbf{1}_{n}
as discussed in Remark 3.12 of the main article) maps any convex set to a convex set.

Proposition B.3.4
.

The quadratic scoring rule has convex exposure.

Spherical
scoring rules – the third most studied proper scoring rules, after the quadratic and logarithmic rules – also have convex exposure.

Definition B.3.5

(Spherical scoring rules)
.

(
Gneiting & Raftery 2007a
, Example 2)

For any
\alpha>1
, define the
spherical scoring rule with parameter
\alpha
to be the scoring rule given by

G_{\text{sph},\alpha}(\mathbf{p}):=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{\alpha}\right)^{1/\alpha}.

If the “spherical scoring rule” is referenced with no parameter
\alpha
given,
\alpha
is presumed to equal
2
.

Proposition B.3.6
.

For any
\alpha>1
, the spherical scoring rule with parameter
\alpha
has convex exposure.

Proof.

Fix
\alpha>1
. We will write
G
in place of
G_{\text{sph},\alpha}
. We have
12
12

12

As discussed in Remark 3.12 of the main article, the range of
\mathbf{g}
should be thought of as modulo
T(\mathbf{1}_{n})
. However, we find it convenient for this proof to think of it as lying in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and project later.

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}^{\alpha}\right)^{(1/\alpha)-1}(p_{1}^{\alpha-1},\dots,p_{n}^{\alpha-1}).

(B.3)

Now, define the
n
-dimensional unit
\beta
-sphere
, i.e.
\{\mathbf{x}:\sum_{j}x_{j}^{\beta}=1\}
, and define the
n
-dimensional unit
\beta
-ball
correspondingly (i.e. with
\leq
in place of
=
). The range of
\mathbf{g}
is precisely the part of the
n
-dimensional unit
\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}
-sphere with all non-negative coordinates. Indeed, on the one hand, for any
\mathbf{p}
we have

\sum_{j}g_{j}(\mathbf{p})^{\alpha/(\alpha-1)}=\left(\sum_{j}p_{j}^{\alpha}\right)^{-1}\cdot\sum_{j}p_{j}^{\alpha}=1

(where
g_{j}(\mathbf{p})
denotes the
j
-th coordinate of
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})
as in Equation
B.3
). On the other hand, given a point
\mathbf{x}
on the unit
\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}
-sphere with all non-negative coordinates,

\mathbf{p}=\left(\sum_{j}x_{j}^{1/(\alpha-1)}\right)^{-1}\left(x_{1}^{1/(\alpha-1)},\dots,x_{n}^{1/(\alpha-1)}\right)

lies in
\Delta_{n}
and satisfies
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{x}
.

The crucial point for us is that for
\beta>1
, the unit
\beta
-ball is convex. This means that for any such
\beta
, the convex combination of any number of points on the unit
\beta
-sphere will lie in the unit
\beta
-ball. Since
\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}>1
for
\alpha>1
, we have that for arbitrary
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\Delta_{n}
and
w\in[0,1]
,
w\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})+(1-w)\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q})
lies in the unit
\beta
-ball – in fact, in the part with all non-negative coordinates. Now, consider casting a ray from this convex combination point in the positive
\mathbf{1}_{n}
direction. All points on this ray are equivalent to this point modulo
T(\mathbf{1}_{n})
, and this ray will intersect the unit
\beta
-sphere at some point
\mathbf{x}
with all non-negative coordinates. The point
\mathbf{p}\in\Delta_{n}
with
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{x}
satisfies

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})+(1-w)\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark B.3.7
.

The above proof gives a geometric interpretation of the QA pooling with respect to the spherical scoring rule, particularly for
\alpha=2
. In the
\alpha=2
case, pooling amounts to taking the following steps:

(1)

Scale each forecast so it lies on the unit sphere.

(2)

Take the weighted average of the resulting points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(3)

Shift the resulting point in the positive
\mathbf{1}_{n}
direction to the unique point in that direction that lies on the unit sphere.

(4)

Scale this point so that its coordinates add to
1
.

Finally we consider the parametrized family known as
Tsallis scoring rules

(
Tsallis 1988a
)
.

Definition B.3.8

(Tsallis scoring rules)
.

For
\gamma>1
, the
Tsallis scoring rule with parameter
\gamma
is the rule given by

G_{\text{Tsa},\gamma}(\mathbf{p})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{j}^{\gamma}.

Setting
\gamma=2
above yields the quadratic scoring rule. Note also that we have already addressed the scoring rule given by
G(\mathbf{p})=\pm\sum_{j}p_{j}^{\gamma}
for
\gamma\leq 1
(except
\gamma=0,1
, which are degenerate), with the sign chosen to make
G
convex: these scoring rules have convex exposure by Proposition
B.3.2
. The following proposition completes our analysis for this natural class of scoring rules.

Proposition B.3.9
.

For
\gamma\leq 2
, the Tsallis scoring rule with parameter
\gamma
has convex exposure. For
\gamma>2
, this is not the case if
n>2
.

Proof.

Fix
\gamma>1
. We will write
G
in place of
G_{\text{Tsa},\gamma}
. Up to a multiplicative factor of
\gamma
that we are free to ignore, we have

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})=(p_{1}^{\gamma-1},\dots,p_{n}^{\gamma-1}).

Let
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\Delta_{n}
and
w\in[0,1]
. We wish to find an
\mathbf{x}\in\Delta_{n}
such that
\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})=w\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{p})+(1-w)\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{q})
, i.e.

wp_{j}^{\gamma-1}+(1-w)q_{j}^{\gamma-1}+c=x_{j}^{\gamma-1},

for all
j\in[n]
, for some
c
. Since
\sum_{j}x_{j}=1
, this
c
must satisfy

\sum_{j}(wp_{j}^{\gamma-1}+(1-w)q_{j}^{\gamma-1}+c)^{1/(\gamma-1)}=1.

(B.4)

Let
h(x):=\sum_{j}(wp_{j}^{\gamma-1}+(1-w)q_{j}^{\gamma-1}+x)^{1/(\gamma-1)}
. Note that
h
is increasing in
x
.

First consider the case that
\gamma\leq 2
. By concavity, we have that
wp_{j}^{\gamma-1}+(1-w)q_{j}^{\gamma-1}\leq(wp_{j}+(1-w)q_{j})^{\gamma-1}
. This means that

h(0)=\sum_{j}(wp_{j}^{\gamma-1}+(1-w)q_{j}^{\gamma-1})^{1/(\gamma-1)}\leq\sum_{j}(wp_{j}+(1-w)q_{j})=1.

On the other hand,
\lim_{x\to\infty}h(x)=\infty
. Since
h
is continuous, there must be some
x\in[0,\infty)
such that
h(x)=1
; call this value
c
. Then let

x_{j}=(wp_{j}^{\gamma-1}+(1-w)q_{j}^{\gamma-1}+c)^{1/(\gamma-1)}.

Then every
x_{j}
is nonnegative and
\sum_{j}x_{j}=1
, so we have succeeded.

Now consider the case that
\gamma>2
, and consider as a counterexample
\mathbf{p}=(1,0,\dots,0)
,
\mathbf{q}=(0,1,0,\dots,0)
, and
w=\frac{1}{2}
. To satisfy Equation
B.4
, we are looking for
c
such that

h(c)=2\left(\frac{1}{2}+c\right)^{1/(\gamma-1)}+(n-2)c^{1/(\gamma-1)}=1.

Note that
h(0)=2\cdot 2^{-1/(\gamma-1)}=2^{(\gamma-2)/(\gamma-1)}>1
, so
c<0
(as
h
is increasing). But in that case
x_{j}^{\gamma-1}<0
for any
j\geq 3
, a contradiction (assuming
n>2
).
∎

Note that because
\nabla G_{\text{Tsa},\gamma}(\mathbf{p})=(p_{1}^{\gamma-1},\dots,p_{n}^{\gamma-1})
(up to a constant factor), QA pooling with respect to the Tsallis scoring rule can be thought of as an appropriately scaled coordinate-wise
(\gamma-1)
-power mean. For
\gamma=2
it is the coordinate-wise arithmetic average. For
\gamma=3
it is the coordinate-wise root mean square, but with the average of the squares scaled by an appropriate additive constant so that, upon taking the square roots, the probabilities add to
1
. (However, as the Tsallis score with parameter
3
does not have convex exposure, this is not always well-defined.)

In Corollary
B.3.3
we mentioned that the scoring rule given by
G(\mathbf{p})=-\sum_{j}\ln p_{j}
can be thought of as an extension to
\gamma=0
of (what we are now calling) the Tsallis score, because the derivative of
\ln x
is
x^{-1}
. QA pooling with respect to this scoring rule is, correspondingly, the
-1
-power mean, i.e. the harmonic mean. This pooling method is appropriately referred to as
harmonic pooling
, see e.g.
Dawid et al. 1995a
.

Finally, we note that the logarithmic scoring rule can likewise be thought of as an extension of the Tsallis score to
\gamma=1
, in that the second derivative of
x\ln x
is
x^{-1}
. It is likewise natural to call the geometric mean the
0
-power mean; notice that logarithmic pooling is precisely an appropriately scaled coordinate-wise geometric mean.

Appendix C
Details omitted from Chapter
6

C.1
Efficiency of Algorithm
3

The only nontrivial step of the algorithm is finding the weight vector satisfying the equation on the last line of the algorithm. To do so, it is first necessary to compute the gradient of the loss. This gradient, given by Equation
C.2
below, can clearly be computed in time
O(mn)
. After that, it is necessary to find the weight vector
\mathbf{w}^{t+1}
that satisfies the equation on the last line. This can be done efficiently through local search: the goal amounts to find weights
(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})
such that the vector
(w_{1}^{\alpha-1},\dots,w_{m}^{\alpha-1})
is equal to a target vector (call it
\mathbf{v}
) plus a constant
c
times the all-ones vector. That is, we need to simultaneously solve the equation
w_{i}^{\alpha-1}=v_{i}+c
for all
i
, with weights that add to
1
. (Here, the
v_{i}
are knowns and the
w_{i}
and
c
are unknowns.)

We start by finding
c
, by solving the equation
\sum_{i}(v_{i}+c)^{1/(\alpha-1)}=1
. Such a
c
exists because the left-hand side of this equation is continuous and monotone decreasing, going from infinity to zero as
c
ranges from
-\min_{i}v_{i}
to infinity. We can solve for
c
very efficiently, e.g. with Newton’s method. Once we know
c
, we know each
w_{i}
: we have
w_{i}=(v_{i}+c)^{1/(\alpha-1)}
. Thus, Algorithm 1 takes
O(mn)
time.

C.2
Details omitted from Section
6.4

See
6.4.4

Proof.

Fix any
t
. Note that since the space of possible weights is
\Delta_{m}
, it is most natural to think of
\nabla R
as a function from
\Delta_{m}
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}/T(\mathbf{1}_{m})
, i.e.
\mathbb{R}^{m}
modulo translation by the all-ones vector (which is orthogonal to
\Delta_{m}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
). That is,
\nabla R(\mathbf{w})=-((w_{1})^{\alpha-1},\dots,(w_{m})^{\alpha-1})
, where this vector may be thought of as modulo translation by the all-ones vector. Nevertheless, we find it convenient to define
\partial_{i}R(\mathbf{w}):=-(w_{i})^{\alpha-1}
. We define
\partial_{i}L^{t}(\mathbf{w})
similarly (see Section
6.4.4
).

Define
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to have coordinates
h_{i}:=\partial_{i}R(\mathbf{w}^{t})-\eta_{t}\partial_{i}L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})
. Per the update rule, we have that
h_{i}\equiv R(\mathbf{w}^{t+1})\mod T(\mathbf{1}_{m})
. We have

-(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}-\eta_{t}\zeta=\partial_{i}R(\mathbf{w}^{t})-\eta_{t}\zeta\leq h_{i}\leq\partial_{i}R(\mathbf{w}^{t})+\frac{\eta_{t}\zeta}{w_{i}^{t}}=-(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}+\frac{\eta_{t}\zeta}{w_{i}^{t}}

(C.1)

Applying the first and last claims of Lemma
C.2.1
(below) with
a=\alpha-1
,
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}^{t}
,
\kappa=\eta_{t}\zeta
, and
\mathbf{g}=-\mathbf{h}
, we have that there exists a unique
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-h_{i}+c)^{1/(\alpha-1)}=1,

and in fact that
-\eta_{t}\zeta\leq c\leq m\eta_{t}\zeta
. (Equation
C.1
is relevant here because it is equivalent to the
v_{i}^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{w_{i}^{t}}\leq g_{i}\leq v_{i}^{a}+\kappa
conditions in Lemma
C.2.1
. This is also where we use that
\eta_{t}\zeta\leq(1-\alpha)^{2}(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha}
, which is equivalent to
\kappa\leq a^{2}v_{i}^{a+1}
.) The significance of this fact is that
(-h_{i}+c)^{1/(\alpha-1)}
is precisely
w_{i}^{t+1}
, since (in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
) we have that
\left(\partial_{i}R(\mathbf{w}^{t+1}),\dots,\partial_{i}R(\mathbf{w}^{t+1})\right)=\mathbf{h}-c\cdot\mathbf{1}
for
some

c
, and in particular this
c
must be such that
\sum_{i}w_{i}^{t+1}=1
. In particular, this means that for all
i
, we have

(w_{i}^{t+1})^{\alpha-1}=-h_{i}+c\leq(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}+\eta_{t}\zeta+\frac{1}{\min_{k}w_{k}}\eta_{t}\zeta=(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}+\left(\frac{1}{\min_{k}w_{k}}+1\right)\eta_{t}\zeta.

Here, the inequality comes from the left inequality of Equation
C.1
and the fact that
c\leq\frac{1}{\min_{k}w_{k}}\eta_{t}\zeta
. If we also have that
\eta_{t}\zeta\leq(1-\alpha)^{2}(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha}
, then the last claim of Lemma
C.2.1
gives us that

(w_{i}^{t+1})^{\alpha-1}=-h_{i}+c\leq(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}+\eta_{t}\zeta+m\eta_{t}\zeta=(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}+(m+1)\eta_{t}\zeta.

Similarly, we have

(w_{i}^{t+1})^{\alpha-1}=-h_{i}+c\geq(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}-\frac{\eta_{t}\zeta}{w_{i}^{t}}-\eta_{t}\zeta=(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}-\left(\frac{1}{w_{i}^{t}}+1\right)\eta_{t}\zeta.

∎

Lemma C.2.1
.

Let
-1<a<0
and
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. There is a unique
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\sum_{i}(g_{i}+c)^{1/a}=1
. Furthermore, let
\mathbf{v}\in\Delta_{m}
and
\kappa\geq 0
. Then:

•

If
g_{i}\leq v_{i}^{a}+\kappa
for all
i
, then
c\geq-\kappa
.

•

If
g_{i}\geq v_{i}^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{v_{i}}
for all
i
, then
c\leq\frac{\kappa}{\min_{i}v_{i}}
.

–

And if, furthermore,
\kappa\leq a^{2}v_{i}^{a+1}
for all
i
, then
c\leq m\kappa
.

Proof.

Observe that
\sum_{i}(g_{i}+c)^{1/a}
is a continuous, monotone decreasing function on
c\in(-\min_{i}g_{i},\infty)
; the range of the function on this interval is
(0,\infty)
. Therefore, there is a unique
c\in(-\min_{i}g_{i},\infty)
such that the sum equals
1
.

We now prove the first bullet. Since
x^{1/a}
decreases in
x
and
g_{i}\leq v_{i}^{a}+\kappa
, we have that

1=\sum_{i}(g_{i}+c)^{1/a}\geq\sum_{i}(v_{i}^{a}+\kappa+c)^{1/a}.

Suppose for contradiction that
c<-\kappa
. Then
v_{i}^{a}+\kappa+c<v_{i}^{a}
for all
i
, so

\sum_{i}(v_{i}^{a}+\kappa+c)^{1/a}>\sum_{i}(v_{i}^{a})^{1/a}=\sum_{i}v_{i}=1.

This is a contradiction, so in fact
c\geq-\kappa
.

The first claim of the second bullet is analogous. Since
x^{1/a}
decreases in
x
and
g_{i}\geq v_{i}^{a}-{\kappa}{v_{i}}
, we have that

1=\sum_{i}(g_{i}+c)^{1/a}\leq\sum_{i}\left(v_{i}^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{v_{i}}+c\right)^{1/a}.

(C.2)

Suppose for contradiction that
c>\frac{\kappa}{v_{i}}
for every
i
. Then
v_{i}^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{v_{i}}+c>v_{i}^{a}
for all
i
, so

\sum_{i}\left(v_{i}^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{v_{i}}+c\right)^{1/a}<\sum_{i}(v_{i}^{a})^{1/a}=\sum_{i}v_{i}=1.

This is a contradiction, so in fact
c\leq\frac{\kappa}{\min_{i}v_{i}}
.

We now prove the second claim of the second bullet. To do so, we note the following technical lemma (proof below).

Lemma C.2.2
.

For
-1<a<0
and
\kappa,c\geq 0
, the function
f(x)=\left(x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\right)^{1/a}
is defined and concave at any value of
x>0
such that
a^{2}x^{a+1}\geq\kappa
.

Since for a general concave function
f
it holds that
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(x_{i})\leq f\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\right)
, the following inequality follows from Lemma
C.2.2
:

\sum_{i}\left(v_{i}^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{v_{i}}+c\right)^{1/a}\leq m\left(\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)^{a}-\kappa m+c\right)^{1/a}.

(Here we are using the fact that
\sum_{i}v_{i}=1
.) Now, combining this fact with Equation
C.2
, we have that

\displaystyle m\left(\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)^{a}-\kappa m+c\right)^{1/a}

\displaystyle\geq 1

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)^{a}-\kappa m+c

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)^{a}

so
c\leq m\kappa
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
C.2.2
.

To show that
f
is defined for any
x
such that
a^{2}x^{a+1}\geq\kappa
, we need to show that
x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c>0
for such values of
x
. This is indeed the case:

x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\geq x^{a}-a^{2}x^{a}+c=(1-a^{2})x^{a}+c>c\geq 0.

Now we show concavity. We have

f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{-a}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{-a}\right)\left(x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\right)^{1/a-2}\left(ax^{a-1}+\frac{\kappa}{x^{2}}\right)^{2}-\left(x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\right)^{1/a-1}\left(a(a-1)x^{a-2}-\frac{2\kappa}{x^{3}}\right)\right)

so we wish to show that

\left(1+\frac{1}{-a}\right)\left(x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\right)^{1/a-2}\left(ax^{a-1}+\frac{\kappa}{x^{2}}\right)^{2}\leq\left(x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\right)^{1/a-1}\left(a(a-1)x^{a-2}-\frac{2\kappa}{x^{3}}\right)

for every
x
such that
a^{2}x^{a+1}\geq\kappa
. Fix any such
x
, and let
d=\frac{\kappa}{x^{a+1}}
(so
0\leq d\leq a^{2}
). We have

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle\leq a^{2}

\displaystyle(1+a)(a^{2}-d)d

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle(1-a)(a+d)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-a(1-d)(a(a-1)-2d)

(rearrange terms)

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{a}\right)(a+d)^{2}x^{a}

\displaystyle\leq((1-d)x^{a})(a(a-1)-2d)

(multiply by
\frac{x^{a}}{-a}
)

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{a}\right)(a+d)^{2}x^{a}

\displaystyle\leq((1-d)x^{a}+c)(a(a-1)-2d)

(
c(a(a-1)-2d)\geq 0
)

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{a}\right)((a+d)x^{a-1})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq((1-d)x^{a}+c)(a(a-1)-2d)x^{a-2}

(multiply by
x^{a-2}
)

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{a}\right)\left(ax^{a-1}+\frac{\kappa}{x^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\right)\left(a(a-1)x^{a-2}-\frac{2\kappa}{x^{3}}\right).

(substitute
d=\kappa x^{-a-1}
)

Note that the fifth line is justified by the fact that
c\geq 0
and
a(a-1)\geq 2d
(because
a^{2}\geq d
and
-a>a^{2}\geq d
). Now, multiplying both sides by
\left(x^{a}-\frac{\kappa}{x}+c\right)^{1/a-2}
completes the proof.
∎

See
6.4.5

Proof.

Note that
\eta\gamma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}m^{(1+\alpha)/2}}
and also that
\eta_{t}\leq\eta
for all
t
; we will be using these facts.

To prove (#1), we proceed by induction on
t
. In the case of
t=1
, all weights are
1/m
, so the claim holds for sufficiently large
T
. Now assume that the claim holds for a generic
t<T
; we show it for
t+1
.

By the small gradient assumption, we may use Lemma
6.4.4
with
\zeta=\gamma
. By the inductive hypothesis (and the fact that
\eta_{t}\leq\eta
), we may apply the second part of Lemma
6.4.4
:

\displaystyle(w_{i}^{t+1})^{\alpha-1}

\displaystyle\leq(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}+(m+1)\eta\gamma\leq\dots\leq(1/m)^{\alpha-1}+t(m+1)\eta\gamma.

\displaystyle\leq(1/m)^{\alpha-1}+\frac{(T-1)(m+1)}{m^{(1+\alpha)/2}\sqrt{T}}\leq 3m^{(1-\alpha)/2}\sqrt{T}.

Since
\frac{-1}{2}<\alpha-1<0
, this means that
w_{i}^{t}\geq\frac{1}{10\sqrt{m}}T^{1/(2(\alpha-1))}
.

We also have that

(w_{i}^{t+1})^{\alpha}\geq\frac{1}{(10\sqrt{m})^{\alpha}}T^{\alpha/(2(\alpha-1))}\geq\frac{4}{m^{(1+\alpha)/2}}T^{-1/2}=4\eta\gamma

for
T
sufficiently large, since
\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}>\frac{-1}{2}
. This completes the inductive step, and thus the proof of (#1).

To prove (#2), we use the following technical lemma (see below for the proof).

Lemma C.2.3
.

Fix
x>0
and
-1<a<0
. Let
f(y)=(x^{a}+y)^{1/a}
. Then for all
y>-x^{a}
, we have

x-f(y)\leq\frac{-1}{a}x^{1-a}y

(C.3)

and for all
-1<c\leq 0
, for all
cx^{a}\leq y\leq 0
, we have

f(y)-x\leq\frac{1}{a}(1+c)^{1/a-1}x^{1-a}y.

(C.4)

We apply Equation
C.3
to
x=w_{i}^{t}
,
y=(m+1)\eta\gamma
, and
a=\alpha-1
. This tells us that

w_{i}^{t}-w_{i}^{t+1}\leq w_{i}^{t}-((w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}+(m+1)\eta\gamma)^{1/(\alpha-1)}\leq 2(w_{i}^{t})^{2-\alpha}(m+1)\eta\gamma.

The first step follows by the second part of Lemma
6.4.4
and the fact that
\eta_{t}\leq\eta
. The second step follows from Equation
C.3
and uses the fact that
\frac{1}{1-\alpha}>2
.

For the other side of (#2), we observe that since by (#1) we have
(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha}\geq 4\eta\gamma
, it follows that
\frac{1}{2}(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha}\geq(w_{i}^{t}+1)\eta\gamma
, and so
\left(\frac{1}{w_{i}^{t}}+1\right)\eta\gamma\leq\frac{1}{2}(w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}
. Therefore, we can apply Equation
C.4
to
x=w_{i}^{t}
,
y=-\left(\frac{1}{w_{i}^{t}}+1\right)\eta\gamma
,
a=\alpha-1
, and
c=-\frac{1}{2}
. This tells us that

\displaystyle w_{i}^{t+1}-w_{i}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq\left((w_{i}^{t})^{\alpha-1}-\left(\frac{1}{w_{i}^{t}}+1\right)\eta\gamma\right)^{1/(\alpha-1)}-w_{i}^{t}\leq 16(w_{i}^{t})^{2-\alpha}\left(\frac{1}{w_{i}^{t}}+1\right)\eta\gamma

\displaystyle\leq 32(w_{i}^{t})^{1-\alpha}\eta\gamma.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
C.2.3
.

For all
y>-x^{a}
, we have

f^{\prime}(y)=\frac{1}{a}(x^{a}+y)^{1/a-1}

and

f^{\prime\prime}(y)=\frac{1}{a}\left(\frac{1}{a}-1\right)(x^{a}+y)^{1/a-2}>0,

so
f^{\prime}
is increasing. Thus, for positive values of
y
we have

f^{\prime}(0)\leq\frac{f(y)-f(0)}{y}=\frac{f(y)-x}{y}\leq f^{\prime}(y)

and for negative values of
y
we have

f^{\prime}(y)\leq\frac{f(y)-f(0)}{y}=\frac{f(y)-x}{y}\leq f^{\prime}(0).

Regardless of whether
y
is positive or negative, this means that
x-f(y)\leq-yf^{\prime}(0)=\frac{-1}{a}x^{1-a}y
.

Now, let
-1<c\leq 0
and suppose that
cx^{a}\leq y\leq 0
. Since
f^{\prime}
is increasing, we have that

f^{\prime}(y)\geq f^{\prime}(cx^{a})=\frac{1}{a}((1+c)x^{a})^{1/a-1}=\frac{1}{a}(1+c)^{1/a-1}x^{1-a},

so

f(y)-x\leq yf^{\prime}(y)\leq\frac{1}{a}(1+c)^{1/a-1}x^{1-a}y.

∎

See
6.4.8

Proof.

We first derive an expression for
\partial_{i}L(\mathbf{w})
given expert reports
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{m}
, where
L(\mathbf{w})
is the log loss of the logarithmic pool
\mathbf{p}^{*}(\mathbf{w})
of
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{m}
with weights
\mathbf{w}
, and
j
is the realized outcome. We have
1
1

1

It should be noted that
\nabla L(\mathbf{w})
is most naturally thought of as living in
\mathbb{R}^{m}/T(\mathbf{1}_{m})
, i.e.
m
-dimensional space modulo translation by the all-ones vector, since
\mathbf{w}
lives in a place that is orthogonal to the all-ones vector. As an arbitrary but convenient convention, we define
\partial_{i}L(\mathbf{w})
to be the specific value derived below, and define the small gradient assumption accordingly.

\displaystyle\partial_{i}L(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=-\partial_{i}\ln\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p^{k}_{j})^{w_{k}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p^{k}_{\ell})^{w_{k}}}=\partial_{i}\ln\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p^{k}_{\ell})^{w_{k}}\right)-\partial_{i}\ln\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p^{k}_{j})^{w_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\ln p^{i}_{\ell}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p^{k}_{\ell})^{w_{k}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p^{k}_{\ell})^{w_{k}}}-\ln p^{i}_{j}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}p^{*}_{\ell}(\mathbf{w})\ln p^{i}_{\ell}-\ln p^{i}_{j}.

(C.5)

Equation
6.2
now follows fairly straightforwardly. Equation
C.2
tells us that
\partial_{i}L(\mathbf{w})\leq-\ln p^{i}_{J}
, where
J
is the random variable corresponding to the realized outcome. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\partial_{i}L(\mathbf{w})\geq\zeta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\text{Pr}\left[-\ln p_{J}^{i}\geq\zeta\right]=\text{Pr}\left[p_{J}^{i}\leq e^{-\zeta}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\text{Pr}\left[J=j\enskip\&\enskip p_{j}^{i}\leq e^{-\zeta}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\text{Pr}\left[p_{j}^{i}\leq e^{-\zeta}\right]\text{Pr}\left[J=j\mid p_{j}^{i}\leq e^{-\zeta}\right]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\text{Pr}\left[J=j\mid p_{j}^{i}\leq e^{-\zeta}\right]\leq ne^{-\zeta},

where the last step follows by the calibration property. This proves Equation
6.2
.

We now prove Equation
6.3
. The proof has a similar idea, but is somewhat more technical. We begin by proving the following lemma; we again use the calibration property in the proof.

Lemma C.2.4
.

For all
q
, we have

\text{Pr}\left[\forall j\exists i:p^{i}_{j}\leq q\right]\leq mnq.

Proof.

Let
J
be the random variable corresponding to the index of the outcome that ends up happening. We have

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left[\forall j\exists i:p^{i}_{j}\leq q\right]

\displaystyle\leq\text{Pr}\left[\exists i:p^{i}_{J}\leq q\right]=\sum_{j\in[n]}\text{Pr}\left[J=j\enskip\&\enskip\exists i:p^{i}_{j}\leq q\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in[n]}\sum_{i\in[m]}\text{Pr}\left[J=j\enskip\&\enskip p^{i}_{j}\leq q\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[n]}\sum_{i\in[m]}\text{Pr}\left[p^{i}_{j}\leq q\right]\text{Pr}\left[J=j\mid p^{i}_{j}\leq q\right]\leq\sum_{j\in[n]}\sum_{i\in[m]}1\cdot q=mnq,

where the fact that
\text{Pr}\left[J=j\mid p^{i}_{j}\leq q\right]\leq q
follows by the calibration property.
∎

Corollary C.2.5
.

For any reports
\mathbf{p}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{m}
, weight vector
\mathbf{w}
,
i\in[m]
, and
j\in[n]
, we have

\text{Pr}\left[p^{*}_{j}(\mathbf{w})\geq\frac{(p^{i}_{j})^{w_{i}}}{q}\right]\leq mnq.

Proof.

We have

p^{*}_{j}(\mathbf{w})=\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p_{j}^{k})^{w_{k}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p_{\ell}^{k})^{w_{k}}}\leq\frac{(p_{j}^{i})^{w_{i}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{m}(p_{\ell}^{k})^{w_{k}}}.

Now, assuming that there is an
\ell
such that for every
k
we have
p^{k}_{\ell}>q
, the denominator is greater than
q
, in which case we have
p^{*}_{j}(\mathbf{w})<\frac{(p^{i}_{j})^{w_{i}}}{q}
. Therefore, if
p^{*}_{j}(\mathbf{w})\geq\frac{(p^{i}_{j})^{w_{i}}}{q}
, it follows that for every
\ell
there is a
k
such that
p^{k}_{\ell}\leq q
. By Lemma
C.2.4
, this happens with probability at most
mnq
.
∎

We now use Corollary
C.2.5
to prove Equation
6.3
. Note that the equation is trivial for
\zeta<n
, so we assume that
\zeta\geq n
. By setting
q:=e^{\frac{-\zeta}{n}}
, we may restate Equation
6.3
as follows: for any
q\leq\frac{1}{e}
, any
i\in[m]
, and any weight vector
\mathbf{w}
,

\text{Pr}\left[\partial_{i}L(\mathbf{w})\leq-\frac{n\ln 1/q}{w_{i}}\right]\leq mn^{2}q.

(Note that the condition
q\leq\frac{1}{e}
is equivalent to
\zeta\geq n
.) We prove this result.

From Equation
C.2
, we have

\partial_{i}L(\mathbf{w})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}p^{*}_{j}(\mathbf{w})\ln p^{i}_{j}-\ln p^{i}_{j}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}(\mathbf{w})\ln p^{i}_{j}.

Now, it suffices to show that for each
j\in[n]
, the probability that
p_{j}(\mathbf{w})\ln p^{i}_{j}\leq-\frac{\ln 1/q}{w_{i}}=\frac{\ln q}{w_{i}}
is at most
mnq
; the desired result will then follow by the union bound. By Corollary
C.2.5
, for each
j
we have that

\text{Pr}\left[p_{j}(\mathbf{w})\ln p_{j}^{i}\leq\frac{(p_{j}^{i})^{w_{i}}}{q}\ln p_{j}^{i}\right]\leq mnq.

Additionally, we know for a fact that
p_{j}(\mathbf{w})\ln p^{i}_{j}\geq\ln p^{i}_{j}
(since
p_{j}(\mathbf{w})\leq 1
), so in fact

\text{Pr}\left[p_{j}(\mathbf{w})\ln p_{j}^{i}\leq\max\left(\frac{(p_{j}^{i})^{w_{i}}}{q}\ln p_{j}^{i},\ln p_{j}^{i}\right)\right]\leq mnq.

It remains only to show that
\max\left(\frac{(p_{j}^{i})^{w_{i}}}{q}\ln p_{j}^{i},\ln p_{j}^{i}\right)\geq\frac{\ln q}{w_{i}}
. If
p_{j}^{i}\geq q^{1/w_{i}}
then this is clearly true, since in that case
\ln p_{j}^{i}\geq\frac{\ln q}{w_{i}}
. Now suppose that
p_{j}^{i}<q^{1/w_{i}}
. Observe that
\frac{x^{w_{i}}}{q}\ln x
decreases on
(0,e^{-1/w_{i}})
, and that (since
q\leq\frac{1}{e}
) we have
q^{1/w_{i}}\leq e^{-1/w_{i}}
. Therefore,

\frac{(p_{j}^{i})^{w_{i}}}{q}\ln p_{j}^{i}\leq\frac{(q^{1/w_{i}})^{w_{i}}}{q}\ln q^{1/w_{i}}=\frac{\ln q}{w_{i}}.

This completes the proof of Equation
6.3
, and thus of Lemma
6.4.8
.
∎

The following lemma lower bounds the regret of Algorithm
3
as a function of
\zeta
.

Lemma C.2.6
.

Consider a run of Algorithm
3
. Let
\zeta
be such that
-\frac{\zeta}{w_{i}^{t}}\leq\partial_{i}L^{t}(\mathbf{w}^{t})\leq\zeta
for all
i,t
. The total regret is at most

O\left(\zeta^{2(2-\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}T^{(5-\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}\right).

Proof.

We first bound
w_{i}^{t}
for all
i,t
. From Lemma
6.4.4
, we have that
